“Two transports cutting engines!”

“Destroyer Seven requesting surrender instructions!”

Rook slowly sat down.

The old soldier looked suddenly exhausted.

Sera opened the station-wide network.

“All Kestrel defenders.”

Her voice travelled across the fortress.

“The Coalition admiral has ordered surrender.”

A cheer began somewhere.

Then another.

Across several channels, people shouted.

Sera raised her voice.

“Hold fire on anyone who disarms.”

The cheering softened.

“Medical treatment applies to wounded Coalition personnel as well.”

Rook looked toward her.

Sera continued.

“No executions.”

“No revenge.”

“No looting prisoners.”

Joel smiled.

Anton looked at him.

“She keeps doing that.”

“Yes.”

“I am starting to think you were right.”

Joel looked genuinely surprised.

“I would like that recorded.”

“Absolutely not.”

Sera heard them.

“Both of you.”

They stopped.

Mostly.

She continued broadcasting.

“Kestrel survived because people held their positions when they had every reason to run.”

She looked toward Rook.

“Old garrison.”

Toward her own crew.

“Wayward Star.”

Toward the House troops.

“And our reinforcements.”

She paused.

“Now we finish this properly.”

Joel stepped toward the communications station.

“House fleet.”

Vigil Lead answered.

“Listening.”

“Coalition surrender order confirmed.”

“Confirmed.”

“Transition to capture and rescue.”

“Understood.”

The House formation changed immediately.

Frigates stopped hunting enemy weapons.

They began escorting damaged ships.

Sanctuary tenders moved forward.

Medical beacons activated across the battlefield.

Boarding teams prepared not for assault—

but stabilization.

The war outside began changing shape.

Weapons fire faded.

Emergency signals replaced targeting locks.

Drifting Coalition ships transmitted damage reports.

House frigates moved between them.

The battle was ending the way Hjertstedt preferred:

with ships still intact.

Crews still alive.

Prisoners instead of wreckage.

Evidence instead of rumours.

The Charter of Steel treated surrender as part of warfare rather than weakness, and House doctrine prioritized capture, medical aid and stabilization once resistance ended.

Sera returned to the command chair.

For the first time since she arrived, the tactical display was becoming quiet.

Tal stood before her.

“What happens to me?”

Sera looked at him.

“You tried to capture my station.”

Tal said nothing.

“You ordered troops into civilian habitation.”

Silence.

“You deliberately used those civilians to limit House fire.”

His face hardened.

“Military necessity.”

Joel’s expression cooled.

Sera leaned back.

“You will get a hearing.”

Tal looked surprised.

Anton looked disappointed.

“A hearing?”

Sera nodded.

“You surrender. You get prisoner protections.”

Tal looked toward Joel.

“Your rules?”

Joel shook his head.

“Her contract.”

Sera smiled faintly.

“My station.”

Joel laughed.

“Yes, Captain.”

The command doors opened.

A group of medics entered.

Behind them came several Iron Guards escorting captured Coalition officers.

Christer looked around.

“Finished?”

Anton nodded.

“Tal surrendered.”

Daniel looked almost disappointed.

“That was sensible.”

Christer looked at Tal.

“Unfortunate for the entertainment.”

Sera pointed toward all four Hjertstedts.

“No more entertainment.”

Anton protested.

“We have an old bottle.”

Joel immediately looked at him.

“No.”

Christer raised an eyebrow.

“What bottle?”

Daniel turned toward Anton.

“You brought something?”

Anton smiled.

Sera closed her eyes.

The battle was over.

And somehow she suspected the next problem would involve the Hjertstedt family drinking inside her command centre.

Then the sensor officer spoke.

“Captain.”

Sera opened her eyes.

“What now?”

He looked toward the gate.

“More House ships.”

Joel slowly turned.

The gate bloom was growing again.

Larger than before.

Much larger.

Anton started laughing.

Sera stared at Joel.

“You called how many?”

Joel looked toward the incoming signatures.

Then at his family.

Then back at Sera.

“Apparently more than necessary.”

## 16. The Acceptance Table

The shooting stopped before the station stopped shaking.

Kestrel still groaned around them.

Damage-control teams moved through the corridors. House medics treated Coalition wounded beside old Vardos soldiers. Sanctuary tenders attached themselves to damaged docking arms and began pumping power, water and medical supplies into the station.

Outside, surrendered Coalition ships drifted under Hjertstedt escort.

Inside Command, Captain Sera Venn stared at the damage report.

It was terrible.

It was also survivable.

That was enough.

Joel walked into the command centre carrying a metal case.

Anton followed him.

Christer and Daniel came behind.

Sera looked at all four.

“No.”

Joel stopped.

“I have not said anything.”

“I know.”

Anton looked offended.

“That seems unfair.”

Sera pointed toward them.

“Every time the four of you arrive together, something strange happens.”

Daniel nodded.

“That is statistically reasonable.”

Christer looked toward Joel’s case.

“Especially considering what is in there.”

Sera slowly looked at the case.

“What is in there?”

Anton smiled.

Joel put the case on the tactical table.

“First, business.”

That changed the room.

Sera’s crew gathered around.

Dax came from Medical with fresh bandages around one arm. Their engineer had grease across her face. Several others still wore damaged combat armour.

Rook stood with the old garrison.

Joel looked at Sera.

Then at her crew.

“No secrets now.”

Anton whispered:

“Less secrets.”

Joel looked at him.

“Anton.”

“Sorry.”

Joel returned his attention to Sera.

“House von Hjertstedt came to Terminus Spire because we were looking for captains.”

Sera folded her arms.

“You followed us because of that.”

“Yes.”

“You boarded as passengers.”

“Yes.”

“You lied.”

Joel considered it.

“We omitted important information.”

Dax laughed.

Sera looked at Joel.

“You lied.”

“Yes.”

“Better.”

Anton smiled.

Joel continued.

“We had read your contract history. Your missions. Civilian evacuation records. Disputes with clients. Payment problems.”

Sera frowned.

“You investigated all of that?”

Christer answered.

“Joel investigates everything.”

Daniel nodded.

“It is exhausting.”

Joel ignored them.

“Records tell you what someone did when people were watching.”

He looked around Kestrel.

“This told us who you were when nobody important was supposed to be watching.”

Sera became quiet.

Joel continued.

“You crossed a hostile gate rather than surrender something you had legally earned.”

“You accepted responsibility for abandoned civilians.”

“You protected people who refused your contract.”

“You accepted enemy surrender.”

“You ordered medical care for prisoners.”

“And when powerful allies arrived, you continued acting like Kestrel belonged to the people living here rather than to the strongest fleet outside.”

Anton nodded.

“You also threatened to throw me through an airlock.”

Sera looked at him.

“That remains possible.”

“Excellent.”

Joel smiled.

Then his expression became formal.

“Captain Sera Venn.”

Sera straightened.

Something about his voice made her crew do the same.

“House von Hjertstedt formally offers you affiliation under the Charter of Steel.”

Nobody spoke.

Joel continued.

“You retain command of the *Wayward Star*.”

Sera’s eyes sharpened.

“You retain command of Kestrel.”

That surprised her.

“This station remains under your protection contract. House forces do not replace your authority unless you surrender command voluntarily or violate the protection terms you established.”

Rook nodded slowly.

Good.

Joel looked at the crew.

“Your crew may join with you.”

Then he added:

“Or refuse.”

Sera looked toward him.

“No forced transfer?”

“No.”

“No House oath behind my back?”

“No.”

“No one suddenly tells me Kestrel belongs to some distant noble council?”

Anton raised one hand.

“We are part of the distant noble council.”

Joel stared at him.

Anton lowered his hand.

Sera almost smiled.

Joel continued.

“You receive House access to logistics, repair yards, medical support, intelligence networks and contract channels.”

Dax leaned toward the table.

“And what does the House receive?”

Joel looked directly at him.

“You.”

Dax frowned.

“All of us?”

“If you accept.”

“What kind of service?”

“Hard.”

Anton nodded.

“Very.”

Joel continued.

“Convoy protection. Pirate suppression. Refugee defence. Recovery missions. Station wars. Lane actions.”

Sera looked at him.

“And when your House orders something I believe is wrong?”

Joel did not hesitate.

“You refuse.”

The room became silent.

Sera studied him.

Joel continued.

“Then you explain yourself.”

“And if I am right?”

“The order changes.”

“And if I am wrong?”

Anton grinned.

“You receive an extremely long lecture.”

Daniel nodded.

“Joel specializes in them.”

Joel ignored them.

Sera looked toward her people.

“This is not my decision alone.”

Joel nodded.

“Correct.”

Sera turned toward Dax.

He shrugged.

“They brought medical supplies.”

The engineer spoke next.

“And reactor parts.”

Another crew member lifted his hand.

“And they shot the people shooting at us.”

Anton nodded approvingly.

“A compelling argument.”

Sera looked across the rest.

One by one, her crew agreed.

Not immediately.

Not automatically.

But willingly.

Finally Sera turned back toward Joel.

“We accept.”

Anton smiled.

Christer smiled.

Daniel smiled.

Joel offered his hand.

Sera looked at it.

Then took it.

“Welcome to House von Hjertstedt.”

Her crew started cheering.

Anton immediately reached for the metal case.

Joel slapped his hand away.

“Not yet.”

“I have waited.”

“Ten minutes.”

“I have waited years.”

Sera frowned.

“What is in that case?”

All four Hjertstedts looked at her.

That should have been warning enough.

---

The acceptance ceremony was moved to Kestrel’s old officers’ mess.

The room had not been used properly in years.

Refugees cleaned the tables.

House engineers restored the lights.

Someone found old Vardos banners and hung them beside the new Kestrel colours.

Food began arriving from the Sanctuary tenders.

Actual food.

Meat.

Bread.

Vegetables.

Fruit.

Hot stew.

Rook stood staring at a tray of fresh bread as if it were treasure.

For some of the refugees, it was.

Then Joel arrived with the case.

Anton carried another.

Christer had a third.

Daniel carried two.

Sera stared.

“I thought there was one bottle.”

Joel looked toward Anton.

“There was.”

Anton smiled.

“I mentioned the occasion.”

Christer placed his case on the table.

“Recruitment requires proper hospitality.”

Daniel placed both of his down.

“And testing.”

Dax immediately became suspicious.

“What testing?”

Anton opened the first case.

Black glass appeared inside.

Heavy bottles.

Metal-sealed.

No labels.

The nearest House soldier took one look and moved his drink farther away.

Sera noticed.

“That worries me.”

Joel removed one bottle.

“This is Hjertstedt Winter Reserve.”

Rook inspected it.

“What percentage?”

Daniel answered.

“You do not want the technical answer.”

Dax frowned.

“I definitely want the technical answer.”

Christer shook his head.

“No.”

Sera looked at Joel.

“Dangerous?”

“Yes.”

Anton nodded happily.

“Very.”

Joel pointed at him.

“Which is why you are not pouring.”

“That happened once.”

Christer laughed.

“Three times.”

“Different bottles.”

Joel placed small metal cups around the table.

“This is an old acceptance tradition.”

Sera looked at the tiny cup.

“That small?”

“Yes.”

Dax looked disappointed.

Anton looked at him.

“You say that because you have not tried it.”

Joel poured.

The liquid was almost black.

A faint red-gold line appeared where the light passed through it.

Sera smelled hers.

Then moved the cup farther away.

“That smells like engine cleaner.”

Daniel nodded.

“Better ingredients.”

The engineer sniffed hers.

“I have used cleaning solvent weaker than this.”

“Probably.”

Sera looked at Joel.

“And this is the test?”

Joel raised his cup.

“The first test was Kestrel.”

Anton raised his.

“This is the stupid test.”

Christer nodded.

“Every family needs one.”

Daniel lifted his cup.

“The rule is simple.”

Sera waited.

“One cup.”

Anton looked disappointed.

“No one proves courage by drinking two.”

Joel looked directly at Anton.

“No one.”

Anton sighed.

Sera’s crew laughed.

Joel lifted his cup.

“To Captain Sera Venn.”

Anton raised his.

“To the *Wayward Star*.”

Christer added:

“To Kestrel.”

Daniel looked toward the refugees gathered throughout the old mess hall.

“To everyone who was abandoned and remained anyway.”

Rook lifted his cup.

“To the ones who did not live long enough to see this.”

That silenced the room.

Sera looked toward her own crew.

Two empty places had been left at their table.

For the people lost reaching Kestrel.

She raised her cup.

“To everyone who got us here.”

They drank.

Sera swallowed.

Stopped.

Her eyes widened.

Dax started coughing.

The engineer slapped both hands against the table.

Anton watched them with enormous satisfaction.

Sera looked at Joel.

“What…”

She coughed once.

“…is wrong with your family?”

Joel’s crimson eyes brightened.

“A long list.”

Dax managed:

“That is not alcohol.”

Anton started laughing.

“It absolutely is.”

“That tried to kill me.”

“You survived.”

Dax pointed at him.

“Do not make this another test.”

“Too late.”

Then the food arrived.

Not ration blocks.

Not emergency paste.

Food.

House cooks took over the old station kitchens.

Refugees brought family dishes from the supplies they had hidden for years.

The old garrison opened its last preserved stores.

Someone found music.

Someone else found an old station speaker system.

The command deck ordered the mess halls opened across Kestrel.

Prisoners received food too.

Medical remained supplied.

Nobody was forgotten.

Hours later, the fortress looked different.

Still damaged.

Still scarred.

Still surrounded by crippled ships.

But alive.

Long tables filled the old military halls.

Crimson Guard sat beside refugee mechanics.

Iron Guard argued with Vardos veterans about armour.

Black Guard somehow acquired an entire table in the darkest corner and refused to explain where the extra food had come from.

Dax challenged Anton to another cup.

Joel immediately stopped both of them.

Christer laughed until he nearly fell from his chair.

Daniel began telling Sera’s engineer exactly how many station systems had failed inspection.

She threatened him with a wrench.

Rook sat among soldiers who had spent seven years believing nobody was coming back for them.

Now they ate until they could not eat anymore.

Sera sat at the centre table.

Joel beside her.

Anton across from her.

Christer and Daniel arguing nearby.

Her battered crew surrounded them.

She looked through the observation window.

House ships filled the space around Kestrel.

Sanctuary tenders repaired its docks.

Coalition ships waited peacefully under surrender protocols.

For the first time, the station did not feel abandoned.

Joel lifted his glass.

Not Winter Reserve this time.

Something safer.

“Kestrel.”

Sera lifted hers.

“My station.”

Joel smiled.

“Your station.”

Anton raised his cup.

“Our newest captain.”

Sera looked at him.

“Careful.”

Anton laughed.

Then the entire table joined him.

The laughter spread.

Mercenaries.

Old soldiers.

House Guards.

Refugees.

A captain who had gone to Terminus Spire expecting payment and instead received a broken fortress, five thousand people, a war and a new family she had never asked for.

Kestrel’s damaged lights burned above them.

The feast continued long into the station night.

And for once, no alarm interrupted it.

# Chapter 2: Return to Kestrel

## 1. Return to Kestrel

By the time Joel returned with the engineering convoy, Kestrel had moved from emergency survival into reconstruction.

The station appeared on the main screen as the House convoy cleared the gate.

For several seconds, Joel von Hjertstedt said nothing.

The station looked nothing like the wreck he remembered.

Lights covered the broken asteroid from pole to pole. Docking arms moved steadily around the outer structure. Patrol ships held disciplined positions beyond the traffic lanes. New armour surrounded the command tower. Defensive platforms tracked incoming vessels without pointing weapons directly at them.

Civilian freighters waited in organized holding patterns.

Repair craft moved between them.

Kestrel was alive.

Anton stood beside Joel.

This time neither man looked like a passenger.

Joel wore the full black military uniform of House von Hjertstedt.

Armoured fabric covered his chest beneath a long command coat lined in deep crimson. The House crest—crown and blade—rested over his heart.

Across his collar sat the insignia that removed any possibility of misunderstanding.

**HIGH MARSHAL.**

The highest military authority of House von Hjertstedt.

A ceremonial crimson command sash crossed beneath his coat, but nothing about the uniform looked decorative. The material was reinforced. His sidearm rested openly at his hip. His dark-steel augmented arm was uncovered from wrist to elbow.

Anton stood beside him in heavier black combat uniform.

His coat remained open over reinforced command armour. A heavy pistol rested on one thigh and his compact breaching hammer was locked across his back.

His collar carried a different insignia.

**BREACH COMMANDER.**

Beneath it sat the crimson mark identifying him as House blood.

Anton looked at the station.

“Well.”

Joel nodded.

“Well.”

“The guns work now.”

“Some of them.”

“Better than last time.”

“Last time they had fourteen missiles.”

Anton smiled.

“That was funny.”

“It was not funny.”

“It became funny after we survived.”

Behind their command transport came the rest of the convoy.

Not warships.

Work ships.

Two Sanctuary tenders.

Three heavy engineering transports.

A fabrication barge.

A cargo vessel carrying reactor parts, structural materials and replacement environmental systems.

And one very large agricultural transport.

Anton looked toward its identification marker.

“You really brought the whole thing?”

Joel glanced at him.

“Yes.”

“Sera asked for agricultural equipment.”

“She is getting agricultural equipment.”

Anton looked at the huge vessel.

“That is not equipment.”

“No.”

“It is a farm.”

“Yes.”

“A very large farm.”

Joel folded his arms.

“Kestrel spent years depending on ration shipments and failing recycling systems. That will not happen again.”

Anton smiled.

“There he is.”

Joel looked at him.

“What?”

“The High Marshal.”

“I am wearing the rank.”

“You could be wearing a cooking apron and still somehow turn breakfast into a strategic logistics problem.”

Joel ignored him.

Kestrel Control opened a channel.

“House command vessel, authentication complete.”

The controller paused.

Then his voice became more formal.

“High Marshal von Hjertstedt. Breach Commander von Hjertstedt. Welcome back to Kestrel.”

Anton leaned toward the microphone.

“Much better greeting than last time.”

The controller answered immediately.

“Last time you brought a Coalition fleet with you.”

Joel looked at Anton.

Anton looked offended.

“We did not bring them.”

“They followed you.”

“Important distinction.”

The controller laughed.

“Command Collar Three is prepared. Sector Commander Venn is waiting.”

Joel looked at Kestrel.

“Take us in.”

---

The military docking collar opened twenty minutes later.

Sera Venn was waiting.

She wore the grey-black uniform of Kestrel Sector Command with House affiliation markings beneath the station crest.

Dax stood beside her.

Commander Halden Rook stood on the other side.

Behind them waited a formal honour guard made from Kestrel soldiers.

Joel stopped when he entered.

Sera stared at him.

Then Anton.

Then back at Joel.

“You wore uniforms.”

Anton looked down at himself.

“We own them.”

“I had started to think you only had traveller coats and illegal weapons.”

“They were not illegal,” Joel said.

“On Terminus Spire?”

Joel paused.

“Probably illegal.”

Dax looked at Anton’s rank insignia.

“Breach Commander.”

Anton smiled.

“You sound disappointed.”

“No. It explains several things.”

Rook looked at Joel’s collar.

Unlike Dax, he did not smile.

He came to attention.

“High Marshal.”

Joel sighed.

“Rook.”

“You are wearing the rank.”

“That does not mean you need to become ceremonial.”

Rook remained perfectly straight.

“It absolutely does.”

Anton laughed.

Sera looked past Joel toward the docking windows.

More House transports were moving into position.

Her expression changed.

“How many people did you bring?”

Joel looked at his slate.

“Two hundred and forty-eight engineers and technical specialists.”

Sera stared.

“For an inspection?”

“For the next phase.”

“What next phase?”

Joel pointed toward Kestrel.

“The part where we stop repairing battle damage and start rebuilding the station properly.”

Sera folded her arms.

“The station is operational.”

“Yes.”

“Fully repaired.”

“Most visible systems are repaired.”

Anton leaned toward Sera.

“He read your engineering report.”

Sera’s eyes narrowed.

“All of it?”

Joel answered.

“Yes.”

“That report was nine hundred pages.”

“Eight hundred eighty-seven.”

Sera stared.

Anton smiled.

“I told you. Strategic breakfast.”

Joel continued.

“Your outer systems are repaired. Your civilian districts are stable. Your main reactor is healthy. Your docks work.”

He looked toward the asteroid.

“But you still have sealed lower levels, old coolant trunks, incomplete industrial automation, and too much dependence on imported replacement parts.”

Sera was silent.

Joel smiled slightly.

“So I brought engineers.”

---

They walked into the docking concourse.

Behind them, House engineering personnel began leaving the transports.

They did not march like soldiers.

They arrived pushing equipment.

Diagnostic crawlers.

Portable fabrication units.

Pressure-test drones.

Cable spools.

Reactor tools.

Hull scanners.

Replacement pumps.

Some wore House black.

Others wore the dark grey of Chaplain-Mechanist engineering orders.

Sera watched them spreading into organized teams.

“You planned this before asking me.”

Joel nodded.

“Yes.”

Anton added:

“He expected you to say yes.”

Sera looked at Joel.

“And if I said no?”

“They return home.”

Anton looked shocked.

“All of them?”

“Yes.”

“That would be very embarrassing.”

Joel continued walking.

---

Their next stop was the main cargo dock.

That was where Sera finally saw the agricultural system.

The transport had opened along its entire side.

Inside were rows of sealed modules stacked like giant containers.

But these were not ordinary cargo pods.

Transparent sections showed growing racks.

Green.

Actual green.

Plants already growing under controlled light.

Sera stopped.

Dax stopped beside her.

Rook stared.

“What is that?” Sera asked.

Joel looked pleased.

“Kestrel Autonomous Agricultural Network.”

Anton nodded toward the modules.

“We need a shorter name.”

Joel ignored him.

Sera walked closer.

A House agricultural engineer approached.

“Commander.”

She activated a holographic model.

The system expanded across the loading dock.

Hydroponic towers.

Aeroponic racks.

Algae protein systems.

Mushroom beds.

Seed storage.

Pollination drones.

Automated harvesting machines.

Water recovery.

Waste processing.

Nutrient reclamation.

Robotic maintenance crawlers.

The engineer continued.

“The system is designed to operate with minimal permanent staff. Crop rotation, nutrient balance, irrigation, harvesting and reseeding are automated.”

Sera looked toward Joel.

“You brought us a complete farming network.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

Joel looked at her as if the answer were obvious.

“You have a station.”

“Yes.”

“You have people.”

“Yes.”

“People eat.”

Anton nodded seriously.

“Constantly.”

Sera ignored him.

Joel continued.

“Kestrel survived the Coalition because people refused to abandon one another.”

He looked around the huge dock.

“It should never again be possible to defeat this station simply by cutting food shipments.”

Rook became very quiet.

He remembered.

Seven years of rationing.

Children growing up hungry.

People dying because supply ships never came.

The engineer changed the hologram.

“The system is modular. We install the first sections inside former military storage levels. Later we expand into the repaired habitation ring.”

Dax studied the production figures.

“This is enough for the current population?”

“Yes.”

“Reserve?”

“Considerable.”

Joel added:

“And scalable.”

Sera looked at him.

“You intend Kestrel to grow.”

“No.”

She raised an eyebrow.

Joel corrected himself.

“I expect Kestrel to grow.”

Anton smiled.

“Better answer.”

---

The inspection continued.

Dock Ring Two had been rebuilt completely.

The old barricades were gone.

Armoured checkpoints had replaced them.

Independent pressure controls divided the docking zones.

Automated weapons remained recessed behind armour.

Joel inspected each section carefully.

This time people knew exactly who he was.

Officers came to attention when he passed.

Engineers stopped to salute.

Joel returned every salute, but he did not interfere with local command.

Sera noticed.

“This is still my station?”

Joel looked at her.

“Why would that have changed?”

“You arrived with two hundred engineers, automated farms and enough military rank to frighten half the sector.”

“That sounds like a logistics visit.”

Anton nodded.

“A very aggressive logistics visit.”

Sera laughed.

---

The military training levels were active.

Six hundred personnel now rotated through Kestrel’s defence structure.

Permanent security.

Reservists.

Technical combat teams.

Flight crews.

Boarding personnel.

House instructors were present, but Kestrel officers commanded every unit.

Joel stood above the training floor with his hands behind his back.

“Better.”

Sera looked at him.

“That is all?”

“Much better.”

“That sounded painful for you.”

“I am practicing encouragement.”

Anton stepped beside him.

“He is terrible at it.”

Joel pointed toward one defensive drill.

“That squad is bunching at the doorway.”

Sera sighed.

“There he is.”

---

Then they saw the *Wayward Star*.

Anton stopped immediately.

The rebuilt frigate rested inside her dock under white maintenance lights.

New armour.

New point defence.

Rebuilt drives.

Replacement weapon mounts.

But still unmistakably Sera’s ship.

Anton stared.

“Oh.”

Dax smiled.

“You want to see the weapons.”

“Yes.”

Joel answered without looking at him.

“No.”

“I outrank most people here.”

“You do not outrank me.”

Anton looked toward Sera.

“Sector Commander?”

Sera considered it.

Anton smiled hopefully.

“No.”

Anton looked betrayed.

Dax laughed.

---

Their final inspection was Central Habitation.

That silenced everyone.

The old refugee camps were gone.

Markets filled the main concourse.

Schools occupied former military offices.

The inspection group stepped into Kestrel Medical.

A proper hospital was open: staffed treatment bays, diagnostics, sterile preparation and patients under active care.

Families lived in proper apartments.

Children ran beneath a restored observation window.

Near the centre stood the memorial.

Names covered the wall.

Old garrison.

Refugees.

Mercenaries.

House soldiers.

Everyone lost during Kestrel’s abandoned years and the battle that ended them.

Joel set a small bundle of station-grown flowers among the offerings with his mechanical left hand.

Beside it, Anton placed a simple glass memorial candle and steadied the flame.

Joel bowed his bare head.

Anton did the same.

For several seconds, the High Marshal and Breach Commander stood silently in full military uniform before the dead.

Then Joel lifted his gaze.

“Good.”

This time nobody joked.

---

The debrief began inside Kestrel Command.

Sera sat in the same old commander’s chair.

Joel remained standing at the tactical table.

His High Marshal insignia caught the light whenever he moved.

Anton sat beside Dax, breaching hammer resting against the chair.

Sera activated the station model.

“Structural restoration: ninety-four percent.”

Joel nodded.

“Better than your report.”

“Your engineers already started changing numbers.”

“That is why I brought them.”

“Life support: full operational capacity.”

“Defence?”

“Point-defence grid eighty-eight percent. Two heavy towers fully operational. Third tower undergoing final alignment.”

Anton nodded.

“Missiles?”

Sera looked at him.

“Enough.”

“That is not a number.”

“You do not need the number.”

“That hurts.”

Sera continued.

“Military readiness is green.”

Another display appeared.

“Civilian population continues increasing.”

Then she added the agricultural modules.

“With your farming network installed, Kestrel should become largely food-independent.”

Joel nodded once.

That mattered more to him than the weapon report.

Sera noticed.

“You like that more than the guns.”

“Yes.”

Anton leaned toward Dax.

“He always has.”

Joel looked at Sera.

“A fortress without food is a coffin with artillery.”

Rook slowly nodded.

He understood that better than anyone.

Sera changed the display again.

The Vardos Sector appeared.

Several systems were stable.

Several were neutral.

Others were marked yellow and red.

“The station is fixed,” Sera said.

“The military works.”

“The farms will make us harder to isolate.”

She looked at Joel and Anton.

“But Kestrel is no longer the problem.”

Joel studied the sector map.

Anton’s smile returned.

Sera continued.

“The Coalition is gone.”

“But the sector it left behind is beginning to reorganize.”

Joel rested both hands on the tactical table.

The inspection was over. Sera had brought him a sector problem.

“Then,” Joel said, “we debrief the sector.”

## 2. The Next Contract

The tactical map of the Vardos Sector filled Kestrel’s command table.

Sera Venn stood at one side.

Joel stood opposite her in full High Marshal uniform.

Anton remained beside him, arms folded across his Breach Commander armour.

Commander Halden Rook and Dax Corven completed the circle.

The repaired command centre was quiet.

No alarms.

No boarding warnings.

No Coalition fleet hammering the outer armour.

Joel preferred it that way.

Sera pointed toward the first system.

“Harven Reach.”

The marker changed from yellow to blue.

“Former Coalition mining territory. The local council has declared independence.”

Anton leaned toward the display.

“Stable?”

“For now.”

“Military?”

“Four patrol ships. Two armed freighters.”

Anton looked at Joel.

“Not stable.”

Sera continued.

“They have requested trade access through Kestrel.”

Joel nodded.

“Give it.”

Rook looked toward him.

“Immediately?”

“Trade creates dependence in both directions. Better they depend on Kestrel markets than some local warlord.”

Sera smiled slightly.

“That sounds almost imperial.”

Joel looked offended.

“It is practical.”

Anton leaned toward Rook.

“That means yes.”

Sera selected another system.

“Damaris.”

The marker turned red.

“Two former Coalition captains are fighting for control.”

“Civilian population?” Joel asked.

“About two hundred thousand across three habitats.”

“Any attacks against civilian infrastructure?”

“Not yet.”

Joel nodded.

“Then we watch.”

Anton frowned.

“No intervention?”

“No.”

“They are fighting.”

“They are allowed.”

Anton looked disappointed.

Joel continued.

“If they begin destroying life support, food production or civilian transport, the situation changes.”

Sera looked at him.

“You really intend to let the sector solve some of its own problems.”

“Yes.”

“You have enough ships to take half of it.”

Joel’s expression hardened.

“That is exactly why we should not.”

The room became quiet.

The House did not need another empire.

It needed allies.

Ports.

Contracts.

People who opened their gates when the House arrived instead of preparing for occupation.

Joel pointed at Kestrel.

“This station becomes stronger if neighbours choose to work with it.”

Then he pointed toward the surrounding systems.

“It becomes weaker if every neighbour believes Sera Venn is simply the first stage of Hjertstedt expansion.”

Sera nodded.

“Good.”

Anton looked at her.

“You sound surprised.”

“I have met powerful people before.”

“That does sound unpleasant.”

“It usually was.”

---

The next system appeared.

“Korlan Free Port,” Sera said.

A station icon appeared.

“Mercenary market?”

Dax nodded.

“Small.”

“Growing,” Sera corrected.

“Most captains avoided the Vardos Sector when the Coalition controlled the lanes. Now they are coming back.”

Joel became interested.

“How many independent crews?”

“Twenty-seven registered this month.”

Anton smiled.

“There we are.”

Joel ignored him for the moment.

“Quality?”

Sera shrugged.

“Mixed.”

“Pirates?”

“Three crews are under investigation.”

“Slavers?”

“None confirmed.”

Anton looked disappointed again.

“You really need better enemies.”

Dax stared at him.

“We are trying to have fewer enemies.”

Anton considered this.

“That sounds reasonable when you say it.”

Sera changed the display.

The final marker was Kestrel itself.

It was surrounded by a new defensive formation.

Six Vigil frigates.

Two destroyers.

One Oath cruiser.

A Sanctuary tender.

Several local Kestrel patrol craft moved inside the larger formation.

Sera looked at Joel.

“House Shield Group Kestrel.”

Joel nodded.

“Temporary assignment.”

“How temporary?”

“Until your local fleet can protect the station without needing them.”

Rook looked toward the display.

“That could take years.”

“Then they stay for years.”

Sera studied him.

“No annexation clauses?”

“No.”

“No House governor?”

“No.”

“No claim to station revenue?”

“No.”

Anton leaned toward her.

“He will, however, complain about your ammunition storage.”

Joel looked at him.

“The storage is inefficient.”

Sera folded her arms.

“It passed inspection.”

“It passed minimum inspection.”

“It passed.”

Joel sighed.

“This is why I brought engineers.”

Sera smiled.

“And they are staying too.”

“Some.”

“How many?”

“One hundred and twelve for the next phase.”

Rook almost laughed.

Sera stared at Joel.

“You brought two hundred and forty-eight.”

“They finished the emergency work.”

“And half are staying?”

“Yes.”

Anton nodded.

“He has become attached to your reactor problems.”

Joel ignored him.

Sera looked toward the agricultural section of the station model.

Green areas now occupied several former military storage levels.

The automated farming network was expanding.

Food reserves were rising.

Water recycling had improved.

New habitation sections were opening.

Kestrel was becoming something more than a fortress.

A town.

A port.

A mercenary base.

Perhaps eventually a city.

Sera looked around the table.

“So this is the plan.”

Joel nodded.

“You rebuild.”

“The House protects the station while I rebuild.”

“Yes.”

“And the sector?”

Joel looked toward the surrounding systems.

“We help those who ask.”

Anton added:

“And shoot those who make that necessary.”

Joel glanced at him.

“Within the Charter.”

“Obviously.”

Sera laughed.

“Somehow it sounds less obvious when you say it.”

---

The meeting lasted another two hours.

Trade agreements.

Patrol routes.

Refugee intake.

Repair capacity.

Docking tariffs.

Salvage law.

Weapons inspections.

The glamorous work of rebuilding civilization.

By the end, Anton looked ready to invade someone simply to escape the paperwork.

Sera closed the final display.

“That is everything.”

Joel stood.

“Good work.”

Sera looked at him.

“That sounded almost encouraging.”

“I have been practicing.”

Anton stood beside him.

“He practiced in the transport.”

Joel stared at him.

Sera laughed.

The meeting ended.

---

Several hours later, Joel and Anton walked through Kestrel.

No guards followed.

No officers.

No formal inspection party.

They had changed clothes.

The High Marshal coat was gone.

The Breach Commander armour was gone.

Joel wore a dark civilian jacket.

Anton wore an old brown coat that had clearly survived several adventures it should not have.

Their House insignia was hidden.

Their weapons were smaller.

Mostly.

Anton looked toward the growing market district.

“You realize everyone knows what we look like now.”

“Not everyone.”

“We were on station broadcasts.”

“People rarely look closely.”

Anton glanced toward him.

“You have crimson eyes.”

“Not constantly.”

“That is true.”

They walked past food stalls.

Repair shops.

Recruiting offices.

A merchant selling salvaged ship parts.

Children ran between the tables.

Two Iron Guard soldiers sat outside a noodle shop arguing with Kestrel mechanics.

Joel smiled faintly.

This was why they had come back.

Not the guns.

Not the armour.

This.

Life.

Anton stopped beside a viewing window.

Outside, House frigates held position beyond Kestrel.

“You stationed an Oath cruiser.”

“Yes.”

“That is not subtle.”

“The Coalition tried to take the station twice.”

“Fair.”

Anton looked toward the civilian ships entering the docks.

“So what now?”

Joel continued walking.

“We find someone else.”

Anton smiled.

“There it is.”

---

The bar was called **The Broken Gate**.

Anton loved the name immediately.

Joel hated the lighting.

That made it acceptable.

They took a table near the back.

Not hidden.

Just unimportant.

A place where two travelling men could drink without anyone asking questions.

The bar was full of mercenaries.

Pilots.

Bounty hunters.

Cargo escorts.

Former soldiers.

Independent captains.

A few people who were probably criminals and several people who were definitely pretending not to be intelligence agents.

Anton looked around.

“Better.”

Joel ordered two drinks.

“Do not start betting immediately.”

“I have not.”

“You are looking at people.”

“That is normally necessary for recruitment.”

Joel looked toward the bar.

A captain was arguing with a broker about an unpaid rescue contract.

At another table, three mercenaries quietly divided their payment with the family of a dead crew member.

Near the door, a young pilot refused a high-paying job after discovering the client intended to transport forced labour.

Anton noticed the same things Joel did.

Neither commented.

Not yet.

The drinks arrived.

Anton lifted his glass.

“To Kestrel.”

Joel touched his glass against it.

“To Sera.”

Anton drank.

Then his eyes moved toward the crowded room again.

“So.”

Joel leaned back.

“So.”

“Captain?”

“Maybe.”

“Crew?”

“Maybe.”

“Interesting disaster?”

Joel looked around the bar.

“That usually finds us.”

Anton smiled.

Across the room, someone suddenly slammed a contract slate onto a table.

A mercenary captain stood.

Angry.

Young.

Exhausted.

And very clearly refusing an offer that would have made her rich.

Joel looked toward Anton.

Anton was already smiling.

Neither brother spoke.

They simply picked up their drinks.

The next recruitment opportunity had just entered the room.

## 3. The Bounty Hunter’s Contract

The Broken Gate became quiet before the bounty hunter said a word.

That was the first thing Joel noticed.

The door opened.

A tall man stepped inside wearing a long dark coat over reinforced field armour. The coat had been cut around the shoulders so it would not catch on his weapons.

He carried three.

A heavy pistol on his right thigh.

A compact rifle across his back.

And a short-barrelled weapon under his left arm that looked designed for fighting inside ships.

The man was perhaps fifty.

Grey hair.

Scar across the jaw.

One artificial eye.

No visible faction colours.

That alone told Joel something.

Most mercenaries wanted people to know who they worked for.

Bounty hunters wanted people to know they worked for themselves.

Anton leaned back in his chair.

“Interesting.”

Joel took another drink.

“You say that too easily.”

The bounty hunter walked straight toward the centre of the bar.

Behind him came four station security officers.

Between them walked a prisoner.

The prisoner wore restraint cuffs around both wrists.

A locking collar covered his neck.

His clothes were clean.

Too clean for a captured pirate.

He looked perhaps forty.

Thin.

Well-fed.

Educated.

And completely calm.

Joel watched his eyes.

Not frightened.

Not angry.

Calculating.

Anton noticed too.

“That one is dangerous.”

“The prisoner?”

“Yes.”

“He is in restraints.”

Anton smiled.

“That rarely makes someone less dangerous.”

The bounty hunter reached an empty table.

He placed a metal contract slate on it.

Then he looked around the room.

“Mercenaries.”

Nobody answered.

Several captains watched him carefully.

The bounty hunter raised his voice.

“My name is Garran Holt.”

That produced a reaction.

A few people whispered.

One man at the bar turned away immediately.

Anton leaned toward Joel.

“You know him?”

“By reputation.”

“Good?”

“Alive.”

Anton nodded.

“Useful measure.”

Holt continued.

“I have a transport contract.”

The prisoner smiled faintly.

Holt ignored him.

“I need to move one secured prisoner from Kestrel to the Ardent Judicial Relay.”

A captain near the bar shouted:

“Then hire station security.”

Holt looked toward him.

“Station security does not operate outside Kestrel jurisdiction.”

Someone else called:

“Hire House escort.”

Anton nearly choked on his drink.

Joel kicked him lightly under the table.

Holt answered:

“I am not paying House rates.”

Anton looked offended.

Joel whispered:

“We are technically expensive.”

“We are sitting in a cheap bar.”

“That is personal choice.”

Holt continued.

“I need ships.”

That changed the room.

Mercenaries understood ships.

“How many?” someone asked.

“Minimum three combat-capable hulls.”

Another voice:

“Destination?”

“Ardent Relay.”

“How far?”

“Two gate transits.”

A captain near the back laughed.

“That route crosses Damaris space.”

“Yes.”

“Damaris is fighting a civil war.”

“Yes.”

“And you are moving a prisoner through it.”

“Yes.”

Anton looked at Joel.

“I like him.”

Joel ignored him.

One captain stood.

“What is the prisoner worth?”

Holt looked toward the restrained man.

“Alive?”

“Yes.”

“Three million credits.”

The bar became silent.

Anton slowly put down his glass.

“There it is.”

Joel watched the room change.

Three million credits was enough to buy ships.

Enough to settle debts.

Enough to make otherwise sensible people become stupid.

Holt continued.

“Dead, he is worth nothing to me.”

A mercenary shouted:

“But worth something to someone else?”

Holt looked directly toward him.

“Yes.”

That was the answer everyone had expected.

Another captain stood.

“How much for escort?”

Holt touched the slate.

The contract terms appeared on several nearby displays.

**BASE PAYMENT: 180,000 CREDITS PER ACCEPTED SHIP.**

**COMBAT BONUS: 40,000.**

**PRISONER DELIVERY BONUS: 60,000.**

**SALVAGE RIGHTS: LIMITED.**

**CASUALTY COMPENSATION: INCLUDED.**

Anton whistled quietly.

“Respectable.”

Joel nodded.

“Too respectable.”

Anton looked at him.

“You think he expects trouble.”

“He is advertising in a bar full of armed strangers.”

“Fair.”

Holt raised one hand.

“There is one condition.”

The prisoner finally spoke.

“He means several.”

His voice was calm.

Educated.

Almost amused.

Holt looked toward him.

“Silence.”

The prisoner smiled.

The bounty hunter faced the room again.

“Any ship accepting this contract accepts full escort discipline until delivery.”

One captain frowned.

“Your command?”

“Convoy command.”

“Same thing.”

“No.”

Another captain asked:

“What happens if someone tries to claim the prisoner?”

Holt answered immediately.

“We leave them behind.”

Anton smiled.

Joel looked at him.

“No.”

“I said nothing.”

“You were thinking something.”

“I was thinking that is efficient.”

The prisoner spoke again.

“You should probably tell them who will be trying.”

Holt turned toward him.

“I said silence.”

The prisoner looked around the bar.

“You are asking strangers to die for money. They deserve accurate information.”

That produced several murmurs.

Holt’s artificial eye shifted.

The prisoner smiled wider.

Then Joel saw something interesting.

Holt did not strike him.

Did not threaten him.

Did not panic.

He simply waited.

Controlled.

Professional.

Joel approved.

A young mercenary captain near the front spoke.

“Who is he?”

Holt said nothing for a moment.

Then:

“Former Vardos Coalition intelligence director.”

Sera’s war had ended recently enough that everyone in the bar understood what that meant.

The prisoner inclined his head slightly.

“Former Acting Director Marek Soren.”

The room changed again.

Some recognised the name.

Others recognised the title.

Anton leaned forward.

“Now it is interesting.”

Joel looked at him.

“More interesting.”

Soren continued before Holt could stop him.

“I managed Coalition covert operations in six systems.”

Holt’s jaw tightened.

“I signed warrants.”

“I authorized payments.”

“I know where several disappeared ships went.”

The bounty hunter stepped closer.

“Soren.”

The prisoner looked at him.

“Yes?”

“Stop helping me increase the price.”

A few mercenaries laughed.

Even Joel smiled.

Soren continued anyway.

“And I know which Coalition officers are still alive.”

That ended the laughter.

Holt grabbed the restraint chain.

“Enough.”

Soren allowed himself to be pulled back.

But the damage was done.

The contract was no longer about transporting a prisoner.

It was about transporting information.

Anton leaned toward Joel.

“Three ships will not be enough.”

“No.”

“Five?”

“Maybe.”

“Ten?”

“Depends who wants him.”

Joel watched Soren.

“That is the better question.”

Holt looked around the bar.

“Contract remains open.”

A captain near the back raised his hand.

“I want proof you have legal custody.”

Holt touched the slate again.

A judicial warrant appeared.

Authentication verified through Kestrel.

Joel nodded.

“Clean warrant.”

Anton looked impressed.

“Very professional.”

“Mostly.”

The young captain who had asked Soren’s identity stepped closer.

“What happens if Ardent Relay refuses entry?”

Holt answered:

“Secondary destination is Terminus Spire.”

Joel looked toward Anton.

Anton immediately saw the problem.

“That makes the route worse.”

“Yes.”

The captain continued.

“And if both refuse?”

Holt looked toward Soren.

Then back at the mercenaries.

“Then I become very unhappy.”

Anton laughed.

Joel covered his face briefly.

Several captains were already opening contract copies.

Others were walking away.

A few were talking quietly with their crews.

Joel recognised the pattern.

Greed.

Fear.

Opportunity.

Calculation.

Exactly the kind of room they had come looking for.

Anton watched one table.

“Green jacket.”

Joel followed his eyes.

A woman in a worn green flight coat sat with four crew members.

She had not touched the contract display.

Instead she was watching Soren.

“Why her?” Joel asked.

“She is looking at the prisoner, not the money.”

Joel nodded.

“Good.”

Another group stood near the wall.

Five mercenaries.

Old armour.

Older weapons.

One of them was studying Holt’s convoy route.

Anton noticed them too.

“Grey team.”

“They are studying the gate approaches.”

“Also good.”

Joel looked toward another captain.

He had already accepted the contract.

Too quickly.

“Red scarf.”

Anton frowned.

“Bad?”

“He signed before asking about medical support.”

Anton considered that.

“Very bad.”

Joel took another drink.

The recruitment opportunity was becoming larger than expected.

Not one captain.

Several.

A convoy was a useful test.

People showed themselves quickly when responsible for someone valuable.

Especially when enemies started firing.

Holt raised his voice again.

“I leave in twelve hours.”

He pointed toward the slate.

“Final acceptance closes in six.”

Then he looked around the bar.

“I am not looking for heroes.”

Anton whispered:

“Shame.”

Joel kicked him again.

Holt continued.

“I want captains who follow orders, keep formation, and understand that the prisoner reaches Ardent alive.”

Soren smiled.

“Preferably.”

Holt turned toward him.

“You are beginning to annoy me.”

“I started hours ago.”

Holt looked toward station security.

“Take him back to holding.”

The guards moved.

Soren allowed himself to be led toward the door.

Then he stopped.

He looked across the bar.

His eyes passed over the captains.

The mercenaries.

The brokers.

Then paused briefly on Joel and Anton.

Joel felt it.

Recognition?

No.

Something else.

Soren studied them for perhaps one second.

Then smiled.

Not broadly.

Just enough.

Anton noticed.

“So.”

Joel remained still.

“He looked at us.”

“Yes.”

“You think he knows?”

“No.”

“You sound uncertain.”

“I am.”

Anton smiled.

“That is rare.”

Soren disappeared through the door.

The noise returned slowly.

Captains argued.

Contracts opened.

Crews calculated fuel reserves.

Someone began taking bets on how many ships would survive the first gate.

Anton finished his drink.

“So what are we doing?”

Joel looked toward the green-coated captain.

Then toward the grey-armoured crew.

Then toward Holt.

“Nothing.”

Anton stared at him.

“Nothing?”

“For six hours.”

Anton leaned back.

“You want to see who signs.”

“Yes.”

“And why.”

“Yes.”

Anton smiled.

“That sounds like betting.”

“It is evaluation.”

“That is betting with paperwork.”

Joel ignored him.

Across the bar, Garran Holt sat alone.

He ordered one drink.

Then waited to see which mercenaries valued three hundred thousand credits more than the possibility of dying before the second gate.

Joel lifted his glass.

“Now we watch.”

Anton looked around the room.

“At captains?”

“At everyone.”

Outside the bar windows, House frigates protected Kestrel.

Inside, the next recruitment trial had just announced itself in public.

## 4. The Unmarked Entry

Six hours before Garran Holt closed the contract, Joel and Anton left The Broken Gate.

Neither hurried.

The bar behind them was still full of noise.

Captains argued over fuel requirements.

Crews checked weapon inventories.

Brokers offered insurance nobody trusted.

Three ships had already signed.

Two more were considering it.

Joel walked through Kestrel’s market district with his hands inside his civilian coat.

Anton followed beside him.

“So.”

Joel kept walking.

Anton waited.

“So.”

Joel sighed.

“Yes?”

“We are doing something.”

“We are observing.”

“We observed.”

“We are still observing.”

Anton smiled.

“You have that face.”

“What face?”

“The one you make before involving the family.”

Joel stopped beside a quiet service corridor.

Anton’s smile widened.

“I knew it.”

Joel pulled a small encrypted communicator from inside his coat.

Not the civilian unit he had used at the bar.

This one carried no public identifier.

He activated it.

The device checked biometrics.

A crimson House symbol appeared.

Anton leaned against the wall.

“Christer?”

“Probably.”

“Daniel?”

“Both.”

“That seems excessive.”

Joel looked at him.

“You once called two hundred Guardsmen to a medical ward.”

“I did not call them.”

“You sent the message that made them come.”

Anton shrugged.

“Details.”

Joel opened a private family channel.

The connection took several seconds.

Then Christer appeared.

He was seated somewhere that looked suspiciously like a command deck.

“Joel.”

“Where are you?”

Christer looked to one side.

“Nearby.”

Joel frowned.

“That is not a location.”

“It is accurate.”

Anton leaned toward the communicator.

“Daniel there?”

Daniel’s voice came from somewhere off-screen.

“Unfortunately.”

Christer turned the display.

Daniel was sitting at another console, reading something.

He looked up.

“Anton.”

Anton grinned.

“Good. We have work.”

Daniel immediately looked suspicious.

Joel spoke before Anton could continue.

“We need an unmarked ship.”

Christer stared at him.

Daniel lowered the slate.

Anton smiled.

That got their attention.

Christer folded his arms.

“How unmarked?”

“No House colours.”

Daniel asked:

“No House transponder?”

“Correct.”

“No obvious military registry?”

“Correct.”

“No Guard escort?”

Joel looked toward Anton.

“No.”

Anton looked disappointed.

Christer leaned closer.

“You are undercover again.”

“Partly.”

Daniel sighed.

“You went to a bar.”

Joel said nothing.

Christer looked toward Daniel.

“They went to a bar.”

“I assumed.”

Anton joined in.

“A very good bar.”

Joel ignored him.

“There is a transport contract leaving Kestrel.”

Christer became serious.

“Target?”

“Prisoner escort.”

“Who?”

“Former Vardos Coalition Intelligence Director Marek Soren.”

That changed both men immediately.

Daniel sat forward.

“Soren?”

“Yes.”

Christer looked at Joel.

“Alive?”

“Yes.”

“Destination?”

“Ardent Judicial Relay.”

Daniel laughed once.

“That route is terrible.”

Anton nodded.

“We know.”

Joel continued.

“Bounty hunter Garran Holt is assembling a mercenary convoy. Several captains are signing publicly.”

Christer understood first.

“You want us inside the convoy.”

“Yes.”

“As mercenaries.”

“Yes.”

Daniel looked toward Christer.

“This might actually be enjoyable.”

Joel pointed toward the screen.

“Do not enjoy it too much.”

Anton looked offended on their behalf.

Joel continued.

“You take the contract under independent registry.”

Christer nodded.

“What ship?”

“You choose.”

Daniel immediately said:

“Not anything recognizable.”

“Yes.”

“No Crimson Guard.”

“Yes.”

“No family command codes.”

“Yes.”

Anton raised one finger.

“Maybe a little family command code.”

Joel looked at him.

“No.”

Christer smiled.

“What about weapons?”

Joel considered that.

“Enough to look competent.”

Daniel laughed.

“That is subjective.”

“Not enough to look like a House strike ship.”

Christer nodded slowly.

“We have something.”

Joel narrowed his eyes.

“That answer came too quickly.”

Daniel answered.

“Because we actually own useful things.”

Anton laughed.

Joel continued.

“Register as independent contractors. Take Holt’s contract. Ask normal questions. Negotiate payment.”

Christer looked almost insulted.

“I know how mercenary work functions.”

“You command banners.”

“I commanded smaller crews before that.”

Daniel smiled.

“Many years ago.”

Christer stared at him.

“You are coming too.”

“I know.”

Joel looked between them.

“One more thing.”

Both became serious.

“You do not know us.”

Anton leaned toward the communicator.

“That will be difficult.”

Christer nodded.

“Very.”

Joel ignored them.

“If we board the same convoy, we are strangers.”

Daniel smiled faintly.

“You are planning to board too.”

Joel said nothing.

Anton answered.

“Yes.”

Joel looked at him.

“We had not decided.”

“We had.”

“You decided.”

“That is often enough.”

Christer started laughing.

Joel closed his eyes.

“Just bring the ship.”

---

Three hours later, Kestrel Control received a new arrival request.

The vessel identified itself as the *Grey Nomad*.

Independent registry.

No major faction affiliation.

Medium mercenary transport-frigate.

Old hull.

Reliable engines.

Two visible missile racks.

Four point-defence clusters.

A single forward gun that looked powerful enough to matter without looking expensive enough to invite questions.

The hull carried no heraldry.

No House crest.

No crimson.

Nothing.

It looked like the sort of ship that had spent twenty years taking escort contracts nobody important remembered.

Joel watched it arrive from a market observation window.

Anton stood beside him.

“That is one of ours.”

Joel frowned.

“You recognize it?”

“No.”

“Then how do you know?”

Anton pointed.

“It is too well maintained.”

Joel looked closer.

Anton was right.

The hull looked old.

The paint looked bad.

The registration markings were deliberately boring.

But the engine alignment was perfect.

The manoeuvring thrusters fired evenly.

The ship entered Kestrel traffic with disciplined spacing.

Joel sighed.

“They are terrible at pretending to be mediocre.”

Anton smiled.

“They are trying.”

---

The *Grey Nomad* docked on the opposite side of the station from the House military berths.

Christer came off first.

Joel almost laughed.

Christer wore a worn civilian combat jacket over old armour.

No rank.

No crest.

A simple mercenary pistol on his hip.

His hair was slightly untidy.

Daniel followed.

Plain flight coat.

Old boots.

A battered data slate under one arm.

They looked convincing.

Mostly.

Anton whispered:

“Christer still walks like he owns the corridor.”

Joel nodded.

“And Daniel is watching every camera.”

“They cannot help it.”

Joel and Anton remained across the concourse.

They did not approach.

Christer and Daniel did not look at them.

Good.

The two men went directly toward The Broken Gate.

Joel smiled.

“They listened.”

Anton nodded.

“Suspicious.”

---

Garran Holt was still sitting at the same table.

Four contract slates had been accepted.

The green-coated captain had signed.

The grey-armoured team had signed.

Two other independent captains had joined.

Holt looked up when Christer and Daniel entered.

He studied them carefully.

Christer walked toward the table.

“Transport contract still open?”

Holt looked at the clock.

“One hour.”

Christer sat without invitation.

Daniel remained standing.

Holt studied them.

“Ship?”

“*Grey Nomad*.”

“Registry?”

“Independent.”

“Crew?”

“Thirty-six.”

“Combat capable?”

Christer looked toward Daniel.

Daniel answered:

“Yes.”

Holt frowned.

“That was confident.”

Daniel shrugged.

“You asked.”

Holt opened the contract.

“Escort discipline mandatory.”

Christer read.

“Salvage limitations are poor.”

Holt almost smiled.

“You expect salvage?”

“I expect someone to shoot at us.”

“Reasonable.”

Christer scrolled further.

“Combat bonus too low.”

Holt stared.

“You have not even heard who wants Soren.”

“That means you do not know.”

Holt became silent.

Christer smiled faintly.

“Which makes the risk higher.”

Joel and Anton sat three tables away.

Anton whispered:

“He is enjoying this.”

Joel nodded.

“Too much.”

Holt leaned back.

“What do you want?”

Christer named a higher figure.

Holt laughed.

Daniel said nothing.

Christer waited.

Finally Holt reduced the gap.

Christer countered.

They settled somewhere in the middle.

Anton looked toward Joel.

“He negotiated.”

“I saw.”

“He actually negotiated.”

“Yes.”

“I thought he would simply pay himself.”

Joel ignored him.

Holt rotated the contract slate.

“Captain’s name?”

Christer paused.

“Chris Heron.”

Anton lowered his head onto the table.

Joel covered his mouth.

Barely.

Holt looked toward Daniel.

“And you?”

“Dan Ward.”

Anton’s shoulders were shaking.

Joel whispered:

“Stop.”

“I cannot.”

“They will hear.”

“This is terrible.”

Holt looked suspiciously toward their table.

Joel immediately lifted his drink.

Anton pretended to cough.

Across the room, Christer signed.

Daniel added his mark.

**GREY NOMAD — CONTRACT ACCEPTED.**

Holt nodded.

“Departure in eleven hours.”

Christer stood.

“Convoy briefing?”

“Dock Control Room Twelve. Four hours before launch.”

Daniel looked toward the prisoner holding information.

“Where is Soren?”

Holt’s artificial eye focused on him.

“Secure.”

Daniel nodded.

“Good.”

Holt watched them leave.

---

Outside the bar, Christer and Daniel passed Joel and Anton.

Nobody stopped.

Nobody greeted anyone.

Daniel looked straight ahead.

Christer did the same.

Then, just as he passed Anton, Christer quietly said:

“Chris Heron.”

Anton made a choking sound.

Joel looked away.

Daniel muttered:

“He chose it himself.”

They kept walking.

Anton waited until the corridor door closed behind them.

Then he burst out laughing.

Joel finally joined him.

“They are terrible.”

Anton wiped his eyes.

“They are magnificent.”

Joel looked back toward the bar.

The convoy now had five escort ships.

A bounty hunter.

A prisoner worth three million credits.

Several captains worth evaluating.

And two senior Hjertstedt family members pretending to be mediocre independent mercenaries aboard an unmarked ship.

Anton took a breath.

“So.”

Joel knew that tone.

“What?”

“We still need passage.”

Joel looked toward the accepted contract list.

Anton smiled.

“Which ship?”

Joel studied the names.

Then he pointed toward the green-coated captain.

“The one who watched the prisoner instead of the money.”

Anton nodded.

“Good.”

Joel started walking toward her table.

Behind them, the *Grey Nomad* sat quietly in Kestrel’s docks.

No heraldry.

No House colours.

No obvious connection.

Exactly what Joel had ordered.

Almost.

## 5. Assault Crew

Joel and Anton did not approach the green-coated captain as passengers.

That would have been too easy.

They waited until she finished arguing with her engineer.

The woman sat at a corner table inside The Broken Gate with four crew members around her. The contract slate from Garran Holt remained open between them.

**PRISONER ESCORT — ACCEPTED.**

Her name appeared beneath it.

**Captain Mara Vale.**
**Independent Frigate: *Resolute Dawn*.**

Joel had already checked the public registry.

Fifty-eight crew.

Old military frigate.

Good drive.

Moderate weapons.

Excellent survival record.

Three completed civilian evacuations.

Two failed contracts.

One disciplinary complaint for refusing a client order to abandon a damaged transport.

Anton had liked that part.

Mara looked up as Joel and Anton approached.

“Table is occupied.”

Joel stopped.

“We are looking for work.”

Mara glanced over both men.

Plain clothes.

Civilian coats.

No House symbols.

Weapons concealed well enough to remain technically polite.

“You crew?”

“Sometimes,” Anton said.

Her engineer looked at him.

“That means no.”

Anton smiled.

Joel continued.

“We heard you signed Holt’s escort contract.”

“So did half the bar.”

“You need additional assault personnel.”

That got her attention.

Mara leaned back.

“Why would you think that?”

Joel pointed toward her contract display.

“Your ship has fifty-eight crew.”

Mara said nothing.

“Registry lists twelve trained security personnel.”

Still nothing.

“Three are currently on medical restriction.”

Her engineer slowly frowned.

Joel continued.

“That gives you nine effective shipboard security personnel for a convoy carrying a prisoner worth three million credits through a civil-war corridor.”

Anton pulled out a chair.

Mara stopped him with one boot.

“I did not invite you to sit.”

Anton remained standing.

“Strict captain.”

Joel almost smiled.

Mara looked at him.

“Where did you get my crew numbers?”

“Public records.”

“My medical status is not public.”

Joel paused.

Anton looked toward him.

Joel corrected himself.

“Mostly public records.”

Mara’s hand moved closer to her pistol.

“Who are you?”

“Independent contractors.”

Anton nodded.

“Expensive independent contractors.”

Joel looked at him.

“We have not discussed payment.”

“I was preparing expectations.”

Mara’s engineer laughed despite herself.

Mara did not.

“What experience?”

Joel answered simply.

“Boarding.”

Anton’s grin appeared.

“Considerable boarding.”

“How considerable?”

Anton looked toward Joel.

Joel looked back.

Neither spoke.

Mara’s suspicion increased.

One of her crew, a heavy-built woman with a shaved head, leaned forward.

“You two military?”

“Formerly,” Joel said.

Anton raised an eyebrow.

That was technically a lie.

Joel ignored him.

Mara studied them.

“You want passage?”

“No,” Joel said.

“We want to sign aboard as temporary assault crew.”

That surprised her.

Anton pulled the chair again.

This time Mara allowed him to sit.

Joel remained standing.

Mara looked between them.

“Why?”

Anton answered first.

“Because passengers are boring.”

Joel added:

“And because Holt’s convoy is going to be attacked.”

Mara nodded slowly.

“At least we agree about that.”

She turned her slate toward them.

“Three possible threat groups.”

Joel read them.

Former Coalition intelligence officers.

Damaris privateers.

Unknown third-party bounty claimants.

Anton pointed toward the last category.

“That one.”

Mara frowned.

“You know something?”

“No.”

“Then why?”

“Because everyone knows the first two.”

Joel nodded.

“The unknown actor is the threat worth planning around.”

Mara stared at them for several seconds.

Then she looked toward her security chief.

“Venn?”

The shaved-headed woman stood.

“Test them.”

Anton brightened.

Joel sighed.

---

The *Resolute Dawn* had an assault training compartment near its main airlock.

Old military construction.

Armoured walls.

Moveable cover.

Non-lethal training weapons stored along one side.

Mara stood behind the observation glass.

Her security chief, Lieutenant Venn Korda, entered with four crew members.

Joel looked at Anton.

“Control yourself.”

Anton removed his civilian coat.

Underneath was simple black body armour.

No crest.

No rank.

“I am always controlled.”

“You once punched through a blast-door mechanism because it annoyed you.”

“It opened.”

“That is not the point.”

Venn tossed Joel a training rifle.

Then Anton.

“Simple exercise.”

Anton checked the weapon.

“Good.”

“You two hold the centre.”

Joel looked around.

“Against?”

Venn smiled.

“Us.”

The lights changed.

Ten seconds later the exercise began.

It ended in forty-three.

Mara stopped the simulation herself.

Her four crew members were spread across the compartment.

Nobody was seriously hurt.

Mostly.

Anton had one man pinned beneath a training shield.

Joel had disarmed Venn without firing his weapon.

Venn stared at the empty space where her rifle had been.

Then at Joel holding it.

“How?”

Joel handed it back.

“You lead with your right shoulder before changing targets.”

She frowned.

“I do not.”

“You do.”

Anton called from the other side:

“You absolutely do.”

Venn pointed at him.

“You stay out of this.”

Anton released the man under the shield.

Mara opened the door.

“You were holding back.”

Joel answered:

“Yes.”

Anton answered:

“A little.”

Joel looked at him.

“A lot.”

Mara’s eyes narrowed.

“That is supposed to make me feel better?”

“No.”

“Good. It does not.”

She looked toward Venn.

The security chief rubbed her shoulder.

“Hire them.”

Mara glanced at her.

“You hate them.”

“Yes.”

“That was fast.”

“They are useful.”

Anton smiled.

“I like her.”

Venn pointed toward him again.

“I still hate you.”

“Excellent.”

---

They signed temporary crew agreements one hour later.

Not passenger contracts.

Crew.

Joel became registered as:

**Joel Hart — Assault Specialist.**

Anton became:

**Anton Berg — Breach Specialist.**

Anton looked at his designation.

“Breach specialist.”

“Yes.”

“I like it.”

Joel signed his slate.

“You would.”

Mara reviewed the contract.

“You follow my orders aboard the *Resolute Dawn*.”

Joel nodded.

“Yes.”

Anton nodded a second later.

“Within reason.”

Mara stared at him.

Joel kicked his boot.

“Yes,” Anton corrected.

“You do not initiate boarding without authorization.”

“Yes.”

“You do not start fights with other convoy crews.”

Anton hesitated.

Joel answered for him.

“Yes.”

Mara looked at Anton.

“I want him to say it.”

Anton sighed.

“I will not start fights with other convoy crews.”

“Good.”

“Can I finish them?”

Mara closed her eyes.

Joel looked toward the ceiling.

Venn started laughing.

Mara eventually pointed toward the armoury.

“Get your equipment inspected.”

Anton stood.

“This might be a problem.”

Mara looked at him.

“Why?”

Anton opened his civilian coat.

The heavy black pistol rested beneath it.

Mara stopped.

Venn stopped laughing.

Joel sighed.

Mara stared at the weapon.

“What is that?”

“Pistol.”

“That is not a pistol.”

“It fits in one hand.”

“That does not make it a pistol.”

Anton removed it carefully and placed it on the table.

The impact made the table move.

Mara looked at Joel.

“You have one too.”

Joel remained silent.

Mara pointed.

“Table.”

Joel removed his long-barrelled weapon.

Venn stared.

“Where did you get these?”

Joel answered:

“Family.”

Anton nodded.

“Old family.”

Mara studied them again.

“You two are becoming increasingly suspicious.”

Joel smiled.

“You hired us.”

“I am beginning to regret that.”

“You can cancel.”

Mara immediately answered:

“No.”

Anton laughed.

“There it is.”

---

Four hours before departure, the convoy assembled in Kestrel’s commercial docks.

Five escort ships.

Holt’s prisoner transport.

Independent freighters carrying fuel and supplies.

The *Resolute Dawn* rested near the centre.

Across the dock basin sat the *Grey Nomad*.

Joel saw Christer standing near its airlock.

Plain combat jacket.

Independent mercenary captain.

“Chris Heron.”

Daniel stood beside him as “Dan Ward.”

Anton noticed them.

He said nothing.

Mostly.

Mara walked past Joel.

“You know them?”

Joel looked toward the *Grey Nomad*.

“No.”

Anton coughed.

Mara stopped.

“You?”

“Never seen them before.”

Across the basin, Christer looked directly toward Anton.

His expression remained perfectly neutral.

Daniel did not look at them at all.

Professional.

Mara continued walking.

Anton waited until she was gone.

Then whispered:

“They are getting better.”

Joel nodded.

“Barely.”

---

Garran Holt entered the briefing room.

Five captains stood around the central tactical display.

Mara represented the *Resolute Dawn*.

Christer represented the *Grey Nomad*.

Joel and Anton stood behind Mara with the other assault personnel.

Nobody acknowledged anybody.

The route appeared.

Kestrel.

First gate.

Damaris transit corridor.

Second gate.

Ardent Judicial Relay.

Holt looked around the room.

“The prisoner moves aboard my transport.”

A captain frowned.

“You keep him on your ship?”

“Yes.”

“That makes you the obvious target.”

Holt nodded.

“That is intentional.”

Joel became interested.

Mara noticed.

Holt continued.

“If they know where Soren is, they do not have to guess which escort carries him.”

Anton whispered:

“I like this man.”

Mara whispered back:

“Be quiet.”

Holt expanded the formation.

“*Resolute Dawn* takes forward assault-screen position.”

Mara nodded.

“*Grey Nomad* takes close escort on my transport.”

Christer answered:

“Accepted.”

Joel nearly smiled.

Perfect.

Mara’s ship at the front.

Christer and Daniel beside the prisoner.

Joel and Anton aboard Mara’s frigate as assault crew.

The family had accidentally surrounded the contract from two directions.

Again.

Holt looked at everyone.

“Any questions?”

Anton raised his hand.

Mara grabbed his wrist and pulled it down.

“No.”

Holt stared toward them.

Anton smiled innocently.

The bounty hunter continued.

“We depart in two hours.”

“Once we leave Kestrel protection, assume we are being watched.”

He shut down the display.

“And if someone boards us…”

His artificial eye moved toward the assault crews.

“…make sure they regret it.”

Anton looked toward Joel.

Joel gave the smallest nod.

That part, at least, would not be difficult.

## 6. The Prisoner Moves

The transfer order came twenty minutes before departure.

Captain Mara Vale was already on the bridge of the *Resolute Dawn* when Garran Holt appeared on the main screen.

His artificial eye reflected the tactical display behind him.

“Change of plan.”

Mara leaned back in her chair.

“That sentence has never improved a contract.”

Holt ignored that.

“Soren transfers to your ship.”

The bridge became quiet.

Venn Korda looked toward Mara.

“Why?”

Holt answered:

“Because everyone expects him to remain aboard my transport.”

Joel stood near the rear bulkhead in plain clothes with Anton beside him.

Anton smiled.

Joel did not.

Mara folded her arms.

“You deliberately made yourself the obvious target.”

“Yes.”

“And now you are moving the prisoner.”

“Yes.”

“So your transport stays the obvious target without carrying the actual prisoner.”

“Yes.”

Mara stared at him.

“That is either clever or the beginning of an expensive disaster.”

Holt nodded.

“I have considered both.”

Joel approved.

It was a simple deception.

Not invisible.

Not perfect.

Good enough to force an attacker to confirm before committing.

Exactly the sort of thing that worked in a universe where sensor truth was always uncertain.

Mara looked toward Joel and Anton.

“You two.”

Anton brightened.

“Yes, Captain?”

“You wanted assault work.”

“Yes.”

“You are now part of prisoner security.”

Anton looked toward Joel.

Joel looked back.

Neither laughed.

That made Mara suspicious immediately.

“What?”

Joel answered carefully.

“Nothing.”

Anton added:

“Very convenient.”

Mara pointed at him.

“That tone means something.”

“It usually does.”

Venn stepped between them.

“Transfer team docks in twelve minutes.”

Mara looked toward her assault specialists.

“You escort Soren from airlock to secure compartment.”

Joel nodded.

Anton smiled.

“Now we get to meet him.”

---

Their bunks were assigned before the transfer.

The *Resolute Dawn* did not have private cabins for temporary assault crew.

Joel and Anton were placed in a six-person military bunk room near the main boarding corridor.

Two upper bunks were empty.

Two lower bunks belonged to regular ship security.

The final two became theirs.

Venn stood in the doorway.

“You get ten minutes to store your gear.”

Anton looked around.

“Comfortable.”

“It is a bunk room.”

“I have slept in worse.”

Joel set his bag on the lower bunk.

Anton dropped his beside the opposite wall.

The bag hit the deck with a metallic weight.

Venn looked at it.

“What is in there?”

Anton smiled.

“Clothes.”

The bag settled again.

Heavy.

Venn did not move.

“Open it.”

Anton looked toward Joel.

Joel sighed.

“We were going to have to show them eventually.”

“That sounds less fun.”

Venn crossed her arms.

“Open. It.”

Anton crouched.

He released two hidden catches.

The bag unfolded.

Venn stopped breathing for half a second.

Inside was armour.

Not normal mercenary armour.

Not even normal military armour.

The chest plate was dark, almost black, but lined with deep crimson reinforcement channels.

The shoulder sections were thick enough to absorb rifle fire.

The collar locked high around the neck.

Compact power-assist systems sat beneath the back plate.

Magnetic weapon mounts were built directly into the frame.

The armour looked less like something worn by infantry and more like something designed to walk through a boarding breach while everything around it tried to kill the wearer.

Venn stared.

“What in hell is that?”

Anton lifted one gauntlet.

“Custom.”

“That is not an answer.”

“It fits me very well.”

Venn looked toward Joel.

Joel’s bag remained closed.

“Yours too.”

Joel opened it.

His armour was different.

Slimmer.

More refined.

Still terrifying.

Dark command-grade plating.

Reinforced chest and abdomen.

Integrated tactical display nodes around the collar.

Left-arm interfaces built specifically around Joel’s augmented limb.

The armour carried no visible House insignia.

Those had been removed.

But the shape remained unmistakably expensive.

Purpose-built.

Personal.

Mara appeared behind Venn.

She had clearly followed the argument.

She stared at the equipment.

“You joined my ship as temporary assault crew carrying custom military armour worth more than some corvettes.”

Anton considered this.

“Maybe not a good corvette.”

Mara looked at Joel.

“Explain.”

Joel rested one hand on the armour.

“We prefer equipment we trust.”

“That armour has powered movement assistance.”

“Yes.”

“Integrated environmental sealing.”

“Yes.”

“Boarding hardpoints.”

“Yes.”

“Command-grade communications.”

Joel hesitated.

“Yes.”

Mara’s eyes narrowed.

“You are not ordinary assault crew.”

Anton smiled.

“We never claimed ordinary.”

Mara looked toward Venn.

Venn stared back.

Then at the armour.

Then again at Joel.

“You said former military.”

Joel answered:

“I said formerly.”

Anton turned toward him.

“That was terrible.”

“I know.”

Mara took a slow breath.

“Fine.”

Joel raised one eyebrow.

“Fine?”

“You are under contract.”

She pointed at both sets of armour.

“You use them only when I authorize combat load.”

Anton nodded.

“Reasonable.”

“You do not access my command network through those suits.”

Joel nodded.

“Agreed.”

“You do not override ship security.”

“Agreed.”

“You do not open sealed compartments.”

Anton looked disappointed.

Joel answered:

“Agreed.”

Mara pointed at Anton.

“I want him to say it.”

Anton sighed.

“I will not open sealed compartments without authorization.”

Mara studied him.

Then looked back toward the armour.

“What do you call it?”

Anton smiled.

“Mine.”

Joel answered more usefully.

“Custom House-pattern assault armour.”

Mara went very still.

Venn looked at Joel.

“House?”

Joel paused.

Anton closed his eyes.

Joel corrected himself.

“House-pattern. Old manufacturing line.”

Mara stared at him for several seconds.

“You are getting worse at lying.”

Joel nodded.

“Yes.”

A tone sounded through the ship.

**PRISONER TRANSFER DOCKING.**

Mara pointed toward the corridor.

“Armour stays here.”

Anton looked disappointed.

“Pistols?”

“Yes.”

His mood improved immediately.

---

Garran Holt entered through the docking airlock first.

Four armed security personnel followed.

Then Marek Soren.

The former intelligence director wore restraint cuffs and a locked collar.

He looked around the *Resolute Dawn* with quiet interest.

Not fear.

Joel stood to the right of the corridor.

Anton stood left.

Soren saw them.

He stopped.

Only for a second.

Holt pulled the restraint chain.

“Move.”

Soren continued.

But his eyes remained on Joel.

Then Anton.

Then back to Joel.

Interesting.

Mara stepped forward.

“Prisoner transfer accepted.”

Holt handed her a slate.

“Custody remains mine. Shipboard security becomes yours.”

Mara checked the document.

“Medical status?”

“Stable.”

“Implants?”

“None detected.”

Joel noticed the wording.

Detected.

Not none.

He said nothing.

Soren looked toward him.

“You are not ship security.”

Anton answered:

“Today we are.”

Soren smiled slightly.

“That seems dangerous.”

Anton smiled back.

“For someone.”

Holt pulled him forward.

“Secure compartment.”

Mara gestured.

The group moved.

The holding compartment sat two decks below command.

No windows.

Independent life support.

Mechanical locks backed by electronic seals.

Two guards outside at all times.

Venn opened the door.

Soren stepped inside.

He looked around.

Then turned.

“Captain Vale.”

Mara met his eyes.

“Yes?”

“You should know something before we depart.”

Holt’s expression hardened.

“Do not.”

Soren ignored him.

“The people hunting me are not all Coalition.”

Mara said nothing.

“Some are.”

He looked toward Joel.

“Some are not.”

Anton slowly crossed his arms.

Joel remained still.

Soren continued.

“And some will not try to kill me.”

Mara frowned.

“What will they do?”

Soren smiled without warmth.

“Take me.”

The room became colder.

Holt shoved him inside.

The door closed.

Locks engaged.

Venn checked the status.

“Secure.”

Mara looked toward Holt.

“You knew that?”

Holt answered:

“I knew it was possible.”

“That is not the same thing.”

“No.”

“Who wants him alive?”

Holt looked toward the door.

“Too many people.”

Anton leaned toward Joel.

“Better and better.”

Mara looked at him.

“You really need a new phrase.”

“No.”

---

Departure came one hour later.

The *Resolute Dawn* released from Kestrel.

The *Grey Nomad* moved into close escort near Holt’s prisoner transport.

Joel watched the ship through a side monitor.

Christer and Daniel were aboard.
