Joel looked at the Ardent marker.

“Corruption under judicial cover.”

The House commander went silent.

Then:

“Understood.”

That answer carried weight.

Mara heard it.

So did Holt.

Joel continued.

“No heraldry on approach. No open House transponders until authorized.”

“Trojan entry?”

“Yes.”

The commander sounded almost pleased.

“Very well.”

Joel ended the channel.

Mara stared at him.

“You already have a name for this kind of thing?”

Anton answered:

“We have names for many bad ideas.”

---

Six hours later, the convoy disappeared into a cold debris field beyond the next lane marker.

The *Resolute Dawn* cut engines.

The *Grey Nomad* drifted beside her.

Holt’s damaged transport remained in the centre.

The captured hostile cruiser followed under reduced power.

Then shadows appeared beyond them.

No transponders.

No running lights.

One cruiser.

Four frigates.

Two destroyers.

An assault carrier.

Sanctuary support remained farther back.

Mara watched the formation emerge from darkness.

Disciplined.

Silent.

Terrifying.

Then the lights came on.

House crests appeared briefly across the hulls.

Anton looked through the viewport.

“Home.”

Joel looked toward the Oath cruiser.

“Temporary home.”

A boarding shuttle crossed toward the *Resolute Dawn*.

When its hatch opened, House officers entered.

They saluted Joel.

Mara watched him return the salute.

No disguise now.

No Joel Hart.

No temporary assault specialist.

High Marshal Joel von Hjertstedt stood in the middle of her damaged ship.

His full command uniform had arrived aboard the shuttle.

Black.

Crimson.

House crest.

Rank clear across the collar.

Anton changed as well.

Full Breach Commander uniform.

The joking mercenary disappeared beneath military authority.

Christer and Daniel did the same aboard the *Grey Nomad*.

Mara looked at the four tactical feeds.

“This is going to become difficult to explain.”

Joel smiled.

“That is why we do not explain it yet.”

---

The operation came together in the Oath cruiser’s war room.

A hologram of Ardent filled the centre.

Joel stood at the head of the table.

Mara, Holt, Anton, Christer, Daniel and Soren surrounded it.

A Justicar joined them.

That mattered.

This was no longer revenge.

It was evidence.

Joel pointed toward Ardent.

“We do not attack first.”

Anton looked disappointed.

Joel ignored him.

“The convoy continues exactly as expected.”

Mara understood.

“*Resolute Dawn* damaged.”

“Yes.”

“Holt still angry.”

“Authentically.”

Holt looked toward him.

“That will be easy.”

Daniel continued.

“Captured cruiser?”

Joel smiled.

“That is the horse.”

Soren looked toward the model.

Now he understood.

The hostile cruiser had attacked them.

Ardent’s corrupt officials would know its identity.

If they saw it returning with the damaged convoy, they might assume the ambush had partly succeeded.

Joel expanded the plan.

“The captured cruiser transmits a false survivor report.”

Daniel nodded.

“They claim they recovered the prisoner after heavy resistance.”

“Correct.”

Holt looked toward Soren.

“And Soren?”

“Remains aboard the *Resolute Dawn* until the last moment.”

Mara nodded slowly.

“Then we transfer him visibly.”

“To the cruiser,” Joel said.

Anton smiled.

“Wrapped nicely.”

Joel continued.

“Ardent opens a secure military collar for the cruiser.”

Christer understood.

“We place assault teams inside.”

“Yes.”

“Black Guard?”

“Small element.”

“Iron?”

“Held outside.”

Anton looked wounded.

“Crimson?”

Joel stared at him.

“Controlled.”

“That is not a number.”

“Twenty.”

Anton smiled.

“Acceptable.”

The Justicar spoke.

“Objective?”

Joel answered immediately.

“Ardent judicial archives.”

“Security command.”

“Communications.”

“Warrant authority.”

“Capture everyone directly tied to the illegal seizure order.”

The Justicar nodded.

“Civilian protection?”

“Absolute.”

“Station reactor?”

“Untouched.”

“Life support?”

“Untouched.”

“Surrender terms?”

Joel looked toward Soren.

“Broadcast the moment we have command integrity.”

The House preferred crippling and seizure over destruction, especially when civilians and evidence mattered.

Mara looked toward the station model.

“And the battle group?”

Joel zoomed outward.

The House ships were positioned outside Ardent’s normal confirmation range.

Cold.

Quiet.

Waiting.

“They remain dark.”

“Until?”

Joel looked toward her.

“Until Ardent tries to stop us.”

Anton smiled.

“Then they stop being dark.”

---

Soren studied the plan.

“You assume the corrupt group controls docking.”

“Yes,” Daniel said.

“They do.”

Everyone looked at Soren.

He smiled faintly.

“Ardent Judicial Security has three command tiers.”

The station model changed.

Soren pointed.

“Public constables.”

“Judicial Guard.”

Then the central tower.

“Office of Special Custody.”

Holt’s artificial eye tightened.

“That office issued my warrant.”

“Yes.”

“Who commands it?”

Soren hesitated.

Then:

“Director Lysandra Kael.”

Mara watched Holt.

Recognition.

“You know her.”

Holt nodded.

“She hired me.”

Silence.

Anton leaned back.

“There it is.”

Soren looked toward Joel.

“Kael does not want me dead.”

Joel’s eyes narrowed.

“What does she want?”

“My memory archive.”

Mara frowned.

“You have no implants.”

Soren smiled.

“Not electronic memory.”

Nobody spoke.

Soren tapped his head.

“I know names.”

“Agents.”

“Access codes.”

“Hidden accounts.”

“Judges.”

“Military officers.”

“Corporate buyers.”

“People who survived the Coalition collapse by changing uniforms.”

Joel looked toward Ardent.

Corruption.

Not one official.

A network.

Anton saw the change in his brother.

“Oh.”

Mara looked at him.

“What?”

Anton smiled without humour.

“This just became personal.”

Joel’s crimson eyes slowly appeared.

“No.”

His voice remained quiet.

“It became professional.”

That was worse.

---

The convoy resumed course twelve hours later.

House warships remained behind.

Dark.

Silent.

The *Resolute Dawn* looked wounded.

The *Grey Nomad* looked exhausted.

Holt’s transport vented controlled coolant to make its damage appear worse.

The captured cruiser moved at the centre.

Its transponder identified it as a surviving attacker.

Ardent appeared ahead.

A silver station around a relay spine.

Clean.

Orderly.

Respectable.

Mara stared at it from her bridge.

A transmission arrived.

“Convoy, this is Ardent Judicial Control.”

Calm voice.

Professional.

“State status.”

Holt looked toward Joel.

Joel stood outside camera view, back in his assault under-suit.

He nodded.

Holt opened the channel.

“This is warrant carrier Garran Holt.”

He sounded angry.

That part required no acting.

“We were attacked after gate emergence.”

Ardent Control responded:

“Prisoner status?”

Holt looked toward Soren.

Soren smiled.

“Alive.”

A pause.

Too short for relief.

Too long for procedure.

“Transfer immediately upon docking.”

Joel’s eyes met Mara’s.

There.

Mara felt it too.

No concern for the convoy.

No casualty inquiry.

No request for attack evidence.

Only the prisoner.

Ardent Control continued.

“Captured support cruiser may dock at Special Custody Collar Seven.”

Anton smiled.

Exactly as planned.

Joel activated the hidden assault channel.

“Trojan team.”

Twenty Crimson-equipped assault troops waited inside sealed compartments aboard the captured cruiser.

Christer.

Daniel.

Anton.

All prepared.

Mara looked toward Joel.

“You are staying here?”

Joel nodded.

“I command the outside piece.”

Anton’s voice came through.

“Because he is boring.”

Joel ignored him.

“Once Collar Seven locks, nobody moves until evidence confirms Kael.”

Daniel answered.

“Understood.”

Christer:

“Understood.”

Anton paused.

Then:

“Understood.”

The captured cruiser moved toward Ardent.

Docking arms extended.

Ardent opened the gate.

Mara watched the ship disappear into the collar.

The station believed it was receiving a captured prisoner.

Instead, it was pulling a blade into its own armour.

Joel looked toward the dark tactical screen.

Beyond sensor confirmation, the Hjertstedt battle group waited.

No crest.

No signal.

No warning.

Yet.

Ardent Control transmitted:

“Collar Seven locked.”

Daniel’s whisper came through the secure channel.

“Pressure equalized.”

Christer:

“Internal doors opening.”

Anton laughed quietly.

“Horse is inside.”

Joel’s eyes burned crimson.

“Begin.”

## 14. Special Custody Falls

Collar Seven opened into white light.

Too clean.

Too bright.

Ardent had built its Special Custody wing to look lawful.

Polished walls.

Clear floor markings.

Blue judicial banners.

Security checkpoints with recorded warrant seals.

Nothing about it looked corrupt.

That was the point.

Anton stepped through first.

Plain dark armour.

No House crest.

No heraldry.

Behind him came Christer and Daniel with twenty assault troops dressed in unmarked combat gear.

The captured cruiser remained sealed behind them.

Ardent security officers waited ahead.

Twelve visible.

Probably more behind the walls.

Director Lysandra Kael was not among them.

A senior Judicial Guard officer approached.

“Prisoner?”

Christer held up the transfer slate.

“Secured aboard.”

The officer frowned.

“We ordered immediate transfer.”

Daniel looked around the concourse.

“Then perhaps you should have sent a proper receiving team.”

The guard’s expression hardened.

Anton smiled.

Christer quietly said:

“Do not.”

“I have not done anything.”

“That is what worries me.”

The senior guard touched his comm unit.

“Director Kael, receiving party has arrived.”

A woman answered.

Calm.

“Bring Soren through.”

Daniel’s eyes shifted toward the wall.

He had been listening to the security network from the moment the hatch opened.

Three hidden squads.

Two sealed doors behind them.

Automated weapons in the ceiling.

Anton saw Daniel’s small hand signal.

Three.

Christer saw it too.

They were already inside the trap.

Good.

Christer lifted the transfer slate.

“We require custody confirmation before releasing the prisoner.”

The guard frowned.

“Your contract does not authorize delay.”

Christer looked directly at him.

“Our contract ended when your people attacked the convoy.”

Silence.

The senior guard froze.

Just for half a second.

Enough.

Daniel spoke.

“Confirmed.”

Anton’s smile disappeared.

The guard reached for his weapon.

“NOW!”

The ceiling opened.

Automated guns dropped from recessed panels.

Anton moved before they finished unfolding.

His pistol fired twice.

The first mount vanished in sparks.

The second rotated toward Christer.

A House trooper put three rounds into its sensor housing.

The room exploded into motion.

Judicial Guards drew weapons.

Unmarked assault troops moved faster.

No shouting.

No hesitation.

The first security line collapsed almost instantly.

Anton hit one guard with enough force to throw him over the checkpoint desk.

Christer fired controlled bursts into weapon arms and armour joints.

Daniel did not engage the main line at all.

He turned toward the sealed side passage.

“Black team with me.”

Four troops followed.

The security door tried to close.

Daniel reached it first.

He jammed a cutting charge against the frame.

“Open.”

The blast drove the door inward.

Behind it waited eight more Judicial Guards.

They opened fire.

Daniel’s team moved through them like a machine.

Two seconds of return fire.

A shield forward.

One flank.

One stun grenade.

The guards lost formation.

Three surrendered.

Two went down wounded.

The others retreated.

Daniel looked at the captured guards.

“Weapons away. Hands visible.”

One hesitated.

Daniel raised his pistol.

The hesitation ended.

Back in the main concourse, Anton stood behind the original security desk.

A guard fired at him from less than ten metres.

The rounds hit his armour.

Anton looked down at the impact marks.

Then back at the man.

“That was disappointing.”

The guard backed away.

Anton advanced.

“Drop it.”

The man fired again.

Anton crossed the distance.

One hit.

The rifle spun away.

The guard hit the floor.

Anton looked toward Christer.

“Still reasonable?”

“Yes.”

“Fine.”

---

On the *Resolute Dawn*, Joel watched the internal assault feeds.

Captain Mara Vale stood beside him.

Garran Holt remained near the rear of the bridge.

Marek Soren sat restrained under guard.

Soren watched the same tactical display.

Mara looked at Joel.

“They are moving too fast.”

“No.”

“They just captured a judicial security checkpoint in under a minute.”

“Yes.”

“That qualifies.”

Joel ignored her.

Daniel’s signal appeared.

**SECURITY SUBNET BREACHED.**

Joel leaned closer.

“Daniel.”

His voice returned through tight-beam.

“Listening.”

“Evidence first.”

“I know.”

“Do not wipe anything.”

Daniel sounded offended.

“I know.”

Anton came over the channel.

“Kael is retreating toward Special Custody Command.”

Joel looked at the station map.

“Do not chase blindly.”

Anton laughed.

“She locked every door between us.”

Christer interrupted.

“We are not chasing blindly.”

Joel saw three House markers moving along a maintenance spine parallel to Anton.

Good.

Mara noticed too.

“They are surrounding her.”

“Yes.”

Soren smiled faintly.

“Kael likes controlled exits.”

Joel looked toward him.

“What does that mean?”

“She never builds a room with one way out.”

Mara stepped closer.

“Secondary escape?”

“Always.”

Joel looked at Daniel’s feed.

“Daniel.”

“Yes?”

“Soren says Kael will have a private exit.”

Daniel was silent for two seconds.

Then:

“Found it.”

A new route appeared.

It ran from Special Custody Command through judicial archives and into a private shuttle collar.

Joel’s expression hardened.

“She plans to run.”

Soren nodded.

“She always plans to run.”

---

Daniel reached the archive level first.

The doors were already locking.

He stopped.

Then smiled.

One of his troops looked at him.

“That bad?”

“No.”

“That good?”

“Yes.”

Daniel connected a bypass unit.

The archive system resisted.

Then failed.

Thousands of sealed warrant files appeared.

Judicial orders.

Private transfers.

Suppressed hearings.

Emergency custody authorizations.

Account movements.

Mercenary payments.

Daniel’s expression changed.

The amusement vanished.

“Joel.”

His voice was different.

Joel heard it immediately.

“What?”

“This is not one corrupt director.”

The bridge became quiet.

Daniel began opening files.

“Special Custody has been selling access.”

Holt stared.

“To whom?”

Daniel continued.

“Private intelligence groups.”

“Corporate detention services.”

“Former Coalition officers.”

“Three recognized governments.”

Mara looked toward Soren.

Soren did not smile now.

Daniel found another layer.

Then another.

“Prisoners transferred under sealed warrants.”

“Some vanish after transfer.”

“Some reappear under different identities.”

Joel’s crimson eyes slowly appeared.

Soren watched him.

Mara noticed.

Joel’s voice became quiet.

“How many?”

Daniel stopped scrolling.

“I do not know yet.”

“Estimate.”

A pause.

“Hundreds.”

Nobody spoke.

The House did not hate corruption because it was untidy.

It hated corruption because corruption turned authority into camouflage for predation.

The Charter of Steel demanded that violence be bounded, witnessed, and accountable. Ardent had built the opposite: a lawful-looking machine that could make people disappear behind valid signatures.

Joel looked toward Holt.

“You carried prisoners here before?”

Holt’s face hardened.

“Yes.”

“How many?”

He did not answer.

Joel waited.

“Eleven.”

Soren looked toward him.

Holt stared at the tactical screen.

“Three never appeared in public court records.”

Mara said quietly:

“You did not know.”

Holt looked furious.

“No.”

Joel turned back toward the assault feed.

“Then we find out where they went.”

---

Anton reached Special Custody Command.

The blast door was heavy.

Judicial armour.

Reinforced.

Anton placed one hand against it.

Christer arrived beside him.

“You are thinking about breaking it.”

“Yes.”

“No.”

“Why?”

“Evidence.”

Anton frowned.

“You say that like doors are evidence.”

“In this building, probably.”

Daniel came over the channel.

“I have control.”

The blast door unlocked.

Anton looked disappointed.

“I could have opened that.”

Christer nodded.

“We know.”

The doors slid apart.

Director Lysandra Kael stood behind the central console.

Six Judicial Guards surrounded her.

Three more officers were near the rear exit.

Kael was older than Mara expected from the intelligence photograph.

Silver hair.

White judicial coat.

No armour.

No visible fear.

Anton entered.

“Director.”

Kael looked at him.

“You are not mercenaries.”

Anton smiled.

“Depends on the day.”

Christer stepped in behind him.

Kael’s eyes narrowed.

“You are House.”

Nobody answered.

Still no name.

No crest.

No public declaration.

Not yet.

Kael touched the console.

Nothing happened.

Daniel had already taken command.

Her expression changed for the first time.

Christer looked toward the guards.

“Weapons down.”

One officer laughed nervously.

“You have no authority here.”

Anton glanced toward Christer.

Christer answered:

“We have evidence of unlawful seizure, attempted prisoner theft, fraudulent warrants, and attacks against a contracted convoy.”

Kael’s face hardened.

“That evidence was obtained illegally.”

Daniel’s voice came through the room speakers.

“No.”

Kael looked up.

Daniel continued.

“You opened your station to the vessel carrying it.”

Anton smiled.

“That was generous.”

Kael looked toward the rear door.

It opened.

Not for her.

Four unmarked House troops entered from the escape corridor.

Kael went very still.

Daniel added:

“Also, your private shuttle is now locked.”

Anton laughed.

“Very generous.”

One Judicial Guard slowly lowered his rifle.

Another followed.

The senior officer beside Kael did not.

“Director?”

Kael looked around.

Her station.

Her command.

Her escape route.

Gone.

Christer repeated:

“Weapons down.”

The remaining guards surrendered.

Kael did not.

She stood behind her console.

“You think taking this office changes Ardent?”

Christer answered:

“No.”

Anton looked toward the files appearing on every command screen.

“But this might.”

The archive doors opened throughout the room.

Daniel had released the sealed index.

Names began appearing.

Payments.

Transfers.

Judges.

Security commanders.

Shipping companies.

Government intermediaries.

Kael’s face went pale.

Anton saw it.

“There.”

Christer looked at him.

“What?”

“The first honest thing she has done.”

Kael glared.

Anton smiled.

“Fear.”

---

Joel opened a station-wide channel.

Mara looked toward him.

“You are revealing yourself.”

“Yes.”

The House battle group remained dark outside Ardent.

For now.

Joel stood in front of the communications console.

The station feed connected.

His voice went to docks.

Courtrooms.

Habitation rings.

Security posts.

Civil offices.

Thousands of people heard him.

“This is Joel von Hjertstedt.”

Across Ardent, people stopped.

Mara looked toward Soren.

Soren closed his eyes.

The cover was gone.

Completely.

Joel continued.

“House von Hjertstedt has seized Ardent Special Custody under witnessed evidence of fraudulent warrants, unlawful detention, attempted prisoner seizure, and coordinated attacks against a civilian-contracted convoy.”

Security channels erupted immediately.

Joel continued over them.

“This is not an occupation.”

Mara looked toward him.

“This is not a claim against Ardent’s civilian population.”

The House doctrine was clear: seal the predatory authority, declare the warrant, preserve life support, accept surrender, then take the command structure and evidence intact.

“Station personnel not involved in the criminal network remain protected.”

“Judges, security officers, and administrators who surrender records will be treated as witnesses until evidence proves otherwise.”

“Anyone destroying files, harming prisoners, or attacking civilians will be treated as actively resisting a lawful investigation.”

Ardent security tried to cut the transmission.

Failed.

Daniel owned the network now.

Joel finished:

“Director Lysandra Kael is in custody.”

“Special Custody Command is secure.”

“Judicial archives are preserved.”

“Ardent will now decide whether it protects its law…”

His crimson eyes brightened.

“…or the people who corrupted it.”

He closed the channel.

For five seconds, nothing happened.

Then Ardent began answering.

One judicial office opened its sealed records.

Then another.

A security captain transmitted surrender.

A dock commander declared neutrality.

Three judges demanded independent review.

A habitation administrator released a list of missing detainees.

Then a constable unit arrested its own superior.

Mara watched the tactical display change.

No fleet battle.

No bombardment.

No station-wide assault.

The corruption network was beginning to collapse from the inside.

Soren looked toward Joel.

“You knew that would happen?”

Joel shook his head.

“I hoped.”

Holt stood near the door.

“And if it had not?”

Anton’s voice came through the channel.

“Then we had the fleet.”

Mara laughed once.

Joel sighed.

“Anton.”

“What?”

“It was true.”

---

Director Kael was escorted into the archive chamber.

Anton walked beside her.

Christer remained behind with the captured security command.

Daniel stood in front of the main archive display.

Names continued appearing.

The network extended farther than anyone had expected.

Kael looked toward Daniel.

“You have no idea what you opened.”

Daniel answered:

“We rarely do.”

Anton nodded.

“It keeps things interesting.”

Daniel ignored him.

Then one file appeared.

Different encryption.

Older.

Higher classification.

Soren saw the identifier through the remote feed.

His expression changed instantly.

Joel noticed.

“What is that?”

Soren leaned toward the screen.

“No.”

Joel looked at him.

Soren’s face had lost all colour.

“No what?”

Daniel opened the outer index.

A title appeared.

**PROJECT SHEPHERD**

Below it:

**EXTERNAL CUSTODY PARTNERS**

The first listed entry was corrupted.

The second was hidden.

The third contained a symbol none of them recognized immediately.

Except Soren.

He whispered:

“Close it.”

Joel’s eyes narrowed.

“Why?”

Soren looked toward him.

For the first time since Kestrel, the former intelligence director looked genuinely afraid.

“Because Ardent was not selling prisoners only to human buyers.”

The room went silent.

Anton stopped smiling.

Daniel looked at the symbol again.

Joel stood very still aboard the *Resolute Dawn*.

Outside, the hidden Hjertstedt battle group remained dark.

Inside Ardent, a corruption case had just become something much larger.

# Chapter 3: The Excision of Ardent

## 1. Beneath Special Custody

Once the receiving corridor was secure, Joel transferred from the Resolute Dawn to Special Custody with Mara and Holt. Soren followed under guard. Their shuttle crossed a dock still crowded with security teams surrendering their weapons; by the time they reached Daniel in the archive chamber, more of the sealed files were opening.

The first Shepherd files were bad enough.

Prisoner manifests.

Transfer orders.

Genetic screening tags.

Seraph custody marks.

Medical notes written in cold administrative language beside human names.

Captain Mara Vale stood beside Daniel as lines of archive data rolled across the display.

“Prime host stock.”

“Functional labor.”

“Cultural utility.”

“Resistance disposal.”

Holt read over her shoulder and looked sick.

Marek Soren said nothing at all.

Joel stood at the center of the chamber, silent and still, crimson eyes faint in the reflected light of the archive screens.

Then Daniel stopped.

“There is more.”

Everyone looked at him.

“What?” Joel asked.

Daniel expanded a buried maintenance layer hidden under Special Custody.

A new structure appeared beneath Ardent’s official station grid.

Three levels.

No public listing.

No judicial registry.

No civilian utility map.

The files labeled them only as:

SUBLEVEL BLACK

SUBLEVEL CHAPEL

SUBLEVEL SHEPHERD

Anton stared at the display.

“Well.”

Christer folded his arms.

“That is rarely good.”

Daniel opened the access logs.

“Private lift only. Restricted to Kael’s office, archive command, and an isolated cargo spine.”

Soren leaned closer.

“I never knew about those levels.”

Joel looked at him.

“I believe you.”

Mara turned toward Director Lysandra Kael, who stood restrained under House guard.

“What are they?”

Kael said nothing.

Anton smiled without warmth.

“That was your chance.”

Joel stepped closer to her.

“Answer.”

Kael looked at him, then at the archive display, then finally down.

“Research.”

No one spoke.

Holt was first.

“Research on what?”

Kael’s voice was dry.

“Selection.”

Daniel opened another folder.

His jaw tightened.

“Human compatibility studies.”

Another.

“Host survivability.”

Another.

“Memory retention.”

Mara stared.

“Memory retention?”

Daniel read from the file.

“They were trying to measure how much of the original person could remain functional after Seraph implantation.”

Silence settled over the room like a weight.

That was worse than simple murder.

Joel’s expression became harder.

“Open the lifts.”

Kael looked up sharply.

“No.”

Anton laughed once.

“That sounded like a request to her.”

Daniel was already inside the control grid.

“It was not.”

The private lift locks broke.

Security doors opened.

A maintenance map appeared.

Three red levels beneath the station.

Ardent had been running a hidden laboratory under its own courts.

Joel turned to Mara, Holt, Christer and Anton.

“With me.”

He looked toward Soren.

“You stay under guard.”

Soren nodded once.

For the first time, he looked relieved not to be included.

The lift descended too far for normal station architecture.

No music.

No announcements.

No public lighting.

Only emergency strips and the hum of isolated power.

Anton checked his weapon.

“Who designs a legal station with secret torture floors?”

Holt answered quietly.

“People who stop believing law applies to them.”

The lift doors opened onto Sublevel Black.

The corridor beyond was white.

Sterile.

Cold.

Medical, not judicial.

Rows of sealed rooms lined both sides.

Most were empty.

Some were not.

The first occupied chamber held surgical frames and restraints.

The second held a row of suspended tanks.

Three were shattered.

Two still contained bodies.

Human.

Thin.

Scarred.

Skin marked by repeated invasive procedures.

One still had part of a medical label attached to the glass.

SUBJECT 4412 — CONVERSION FAILURE

Mara stopped moving.

Anton’s smile had vanished completely.

Daniel moved to a side console.

“These are not just holding cells.”

“No,” Joel said.

“They are laboratories.”

They advanced deeper.

The horrors became more specific.

Rooms full of bloodwork and tissue records.

Compatibility charts.

Neural scans.

A chamber where failed host tissue had been preserved for reference.

A restraint room with a speaker system designed to replay familiar voices into sealed cells.

Another with rows of implants and surgical instruments arranged like sacred tools.

Then they reached Sublevel Chapel.

The name fit badly.

It was not holy.

It was ceremonial.

Seraph symbols had been cut into the metal walls beneath removable House-neutral panels.

Here the experiments changed.

Not simply survival.

Control.

Voice imitation.

Behavior pattern retention.

Command phrase testing.

There was a chamber marked:

RETURNED SUBJECT STABILITY REVIEW

Holt read it and looked toward Joel.

“They sent some of them back.”

“Yes,” Joel said.

“That was always the worst possibility.”

Christer checked the next passage.

“Movement ahead.”

Everyone raised weapons.

A locked bulkhead stood at the end of the corridor.

Behind it, faint impacts sounded from the far side.

Not machine noise.

Someone striking metal.

Joel nodded.

Anton placed the breaching charge.

The door blew inward.

Beyond it lay Sublevel Shepherd.

This level was different from the others.

Not active laboratories.

Storage.

Rows of sealed cryo-vaults stretched into the dimness.

Some were empty.

Some were broken.

Some still glowed with low-power status lights.

Daniel moved to the nearest terminal.

“Power reserve is failing. Many pods are dead.”

He pulled up the occupant registry.

His face changed.

“What?”

Daniel turned the screen toward Joel.

The names were old.

Too old.

Missing House personnel.

Agents.

Captured officers.

Names marked dead in House records decades earlier.

Mara saw Joel go still.

Anton stepped closer.

Then swore quietly.

Christer stared at the list.

“No.”

Daniel scrolled again.

The file flagged a special category.

ANOMALOUS STOCK — HJERTSTEDT LINE

That was bad enough.

Then another cluster of names appeared under restricted preservation orders.

Not soldiers.

Not officers.

Elders.

Ancient House names.

People from old campaigns.

Figures recorded as lost, executed, or dead in transit long ago.

Joel reached for the nearest pod as if he did not trust his eyes.

Inside lay an old man with a pale face, long white hair, and the hard features of House blood.

Scars crossed his throat and scalp.

His chest still rose.

Slowly.

A faded data plate on the pod read:

Hakon von Hjertstedt

Status: Host Rejection / Retained for Study

Anton stepped back.

“Hakon?”

Mara looked between them.

“You know him?”

Christer answered, voice flat.

“He died forty-three years ago.”

Joel stared through the frost on the pod glass.

“No.”

He looked at the rest of the row.

“House was told he died forty-three years ago.”

Daniel opened another pod record.

Then another.

Three more names.

All House elders.

All believed dead.

All preserved.

All marked as failed Seraph conversion subjects.

Mara finally understood.

“The blood.”

Joel looked at her.

She saw the warning in his eyes.

Enough.

No more.

The secret remained the House’s.

But the evidence was clear enough.

The Seraphs had tried to take House blood.

Failed.

And kept the survivors alive for study.

Kael had not just sold prisoners.

She had fed ancient enemies one of the most protected bloodlines in human space.

Behind them, one of the older pods alarmed.

Power failure.

Daniel moved fast.

“This one is losing support.”

Anton was already at the manual release.

Joel checked the registry.

Name:

Elder Rurik von Hjertstedt

Status:

Viable

Christer looked at Joel.

“What now?”

Joel’s face was stone.

“We wake them.”

Behind him, in the darkness of Sublevel Shepherd, more dormant pods waited.

Some bore House names.

Some belonged to other factions.

Some had no names at all.

And on the final sealed vault door at the far end of the chamber, a red warning burned through centuries of dust:

SHEPHERD PRIORITY VAULT — AUTHORIZED FLESH MINISTRY ACCESS ONLY

Anton looked at it and smiled like a wolf.

“Now it gets interesting.”

---

## 2. The Scale of Ardent

The House-only signal left Ardent under maximum encryption.

Joel stood beside the cryo-vault containing Hakon von Hjertstedt.

His crimson eyes stayed fixed on the old man's face.

Forty-three years declared dead.

Forty-three years inside a Shepherd laboratory.

Around them, House medics connected emergency support to the surviving elders.

Anton stood near the sealed Priority Vault.

Christer and Daniel watched the archive feeds.

Nobody joked now.

Joel opened the command channel.

“Crimson emergency.”

The reply came immediately.

“Authentication confirmed.”

“Seraph involvement verified,” Joel said. “Living House blood recovered from experimental custody. Multiple elders previously declared dead.”

Silence followed.

Then the House officer answered differently.

“Repeat.”

Joel did.

This time his voice was harder.

“Seraph research. House blood. Living elders.”

A pause.

“Understood.”

Joel continued.

“Notify Justicar High Command. Medical command. Senior family authority.”

Anton looked toward him.

Joel added:

“And begin Excision readiness.”

That changed everything.

Daniel suddenly turned from the archive terminal.

“Joel.”

“What?”

“Ardent is larger than we thought.”

He expanded a recovered strategic file.

The station around them appeared first.

ARDENT JUDICIAL RELAY — CENTRAL.

Then two more major installations appeared.

One around the neighbouring gas giant.

Another guarding the system’s primary gate complex.

Mara stared at the display.

“Three stations?”

Daniel nodded.

“Three major stations.”

Soren looked over his shoulder.

“Ardent Central. Ardent Bastion. Ardent Gatehold.”

Holt frowned.

“I thought Gatehold was only customs.”

Soren gave him a tired look.

“That is what Ardent tells outsiders.”

Daniel opened another layer.

Planets appeared.

Several inhabited.

One agricultural world.

One mining world.

A small industrial planet with large orbital yards.

Another colony listed as a restricted administrative territory.

Mara slowly turned toward Joel.

“This is not a station government.”

“No,” Joel said.

“It is a small state.”

Daniel opened the military ledger.

Then stopped.

Christer noticed.

“What?”

Daniel enlarged the fleet count.

Everyone went quiet.

Ardent's declared warfleet should have been modest.

Patrol frigates.

Customs ships.

A few destroyers.

Perhaps several cruisers protecting the relay network.

Instead the hidden ledger showed almost three times the expected combat strength.

Reserve ships.

Unregistered escorts.

Assault transports.

Missile barges.

Several heavy cruisers.

At least two capital hulls operating under false commercial registries.

Anton stared at the numbers.

“Well.”

Christer folded his arms.

“That explains the money.”

Mara looked toward Soren.

“You knew?”

“Not the full count.”

“You were intelligence director.”

“Ardent was very careful.”

Joel studied the fleet structure.

This was not random corruption.

Someone had been building military power.

Quietly.

For years.

Holt looked toward the Shepherd archive.

“With prisoner money?”

Daniel shook his head.

“Not enough.”

Joel pointed toward several transfers.

“External funding.”

Soren looked pale.

“Seraph.”

Nobody answered.

They did not need to.

The Seraph Throne was known for building influence before exposing fleets. It preferred command seizure, host capture, and control of infrastructure over simple destruction.

Ardent had become useful.

Judicial authority.

Three major stations.

Planets.

Shipyards.

A hidden warfleet.

And Shepherd access to thousands of people.

Anton looked toward Joel.

“This is no longer an investigation.”

“No.”

“A raid?”

“No.”

Christer already understood.

“Campaign.”

Joel nodded.

House von Hjertstedt normally avoided conquest. Its Charter favored protection, limited intervention, surrender, capture, and bounded war.

Seraph involvement was one of the exceptions.

Joel opened another encrypted channel.

“Nearest Banner commands.”

Several acknowledgements appeared.

“High Marshal.”

“Listening.”

“Ready.”

Joel looked toward the map of Ardent territory.

“New operational assumption.”

He highlighted all three stations.

Then the planets.

Then the hidden fleet.

“Ardent is considered strategically compromised until proven otherwise.”

Mara looked at him.

“You are claiming the whole state?”

Joel shook his head.

“Not yet.”

Anton smiled without humour.

“That means probably.”

Joel continued.

“Seal outbound military traffic.”

“Do not interfere with civilian evacuation.”

“Sanctuary groups prepare quarantine capacity.”

“Vigil groups move toward all known gates.”

“Justicars begin legal review of Ardent authority.”

Christer stepped closer.

“And their fleet?”

Joel looked at the hidden force numbers.

“Observe.”

Anton frowned.

“Only observe?”

“Until they choose.”

Daniel nodded.

“If they surrender their records and submit to inspection, we do not need a war.”

“And if they refuse?” Mara asked.

Joel looked toward the cryo-pods.

Toward Hakon.

Toward the elders the House had mourned decades earlier.

His eyes turned fully crimson.

“Then Ardent stops being independent.”

Nobody spoke.

Joel opened the House-wide command circuit.

“This is High Marshal Joel von Hjertstedt.”

Every active House command listening went silent.

“Confirmed Seraph involvement inside Ardent territory.”

“Project Shepherd is active.”

“House blood has been used in conversion research.”

The words were enough.

Across distant ships, officers would already be changing course.

Joel continued.

“Ardent controls three major stations, several inhabited worlds, and a concealed warfleet roughly three times its declared strength.”

Anton smiled.

The kind of smile he wore before battles.

Joel finished:

“Prepare for Excision Campaign authority.”

Outside the station, the first Ardent warships began changing formation.

Their drives lit.

Weapons systems woke.

On Joel’s display, the changing formation gave him no answer yet. He kept the inspection offer open and watched their weapons.

---

## 3. The Primarch Approves

The message reached Vintergard under Crimson priority.

It did not go through ordinary fleet command.

It passed the Winter Road relay network, crossed the Shield around Hjarta, and reached the restricted command chambers beneath Vinterkrona.

Primarch Lars von Hjertstedt read it once.

Then again.

Around him, the old strategic chamber remained silent.

PROJECT SHEPHERD CONFIRMED.

SERAPH INVOLVEMENT VERIFIED.

MULTIPLE HOUSE ELDERS RECOVERED ALIVE.

HJERTSTEDT BLOOD USED IN HOST RESEARCH.

Lars stopped at that line.

His eyes turned crimson.

The officers around the table noticed.

Nobody spoke.

Lars was Joel and Anton's father.

He had commanded House fleets before either son had been trusted with a pistol, survived campaigns now taught as doctrine, and watched too many names enter the family memorial halls.

Several of those names had just been found breathing inside Seraph cryogenic vaults.

“Hakon?” Lars asked.

A medical officer answered carefully.

“Alive.”

Lars looked up.

“Confirmed?”

“Three independent scans. House genetic verification. He remains under suspended revival until Joel authorizes full extraction.”

Lars's jaw tightened.

“Hakon died before Anton had command authority.”

“Yes, Primarch.”

“He did not die.”

“No.”

Lars looked back toward the Ardent strategic map.

Three stations.

Several inhabited worlds.

Hidden yards.

A warfleet almost three times the strength Ardent publicly admitted.

And beneath all of it—

Project Shepherd.

Lars placed both hands on the table.

“Joel requested Excision readiness.”

“Yes.”

“Approved.”

Nobody moved.

The senior Justicar beside him leaned forward.

“Full authority?”

Lars looked at him.

“Seraph custody facilities. House blood experimentation. Living elders stolen from our dead rolls.”

His voice stayed calm.

That made it worse.

“If Ardent cannot prove command integrity, then yes.”

The Justicar nodded.

“Excision authority will be prepared.”

“Not announced.”

“Understood.”

Lars pointed toward the Ardent systems.

“Joel still gives them the chance to surrender.”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

A faint smile appeared.

“He remembers what I taught him.”

Then Lars looked toward another display.

Sol.

The neutral heart of human diplomacy.

The system where rival human powers could meet without bringing fleets into the same room.

“Prepare my ship.”

One officer hesitated.

“Destination?”

“Sol.”

That earned several looks.

Lars noticed.

“If this becomes an Excision Campaign, every major human power will hear about it within hours.”

He pointed toward Ardent.

“A judicial state with three stations and a hidden fleet does not disappear quietly.”

The Justicar understood.

“You want recognition before the war expands.”

“I want witnesses.”

Lars travelled aboard a House command cruiser rather than a full battle formation.

Even so, Sol Control noticed the ship immediately.

The vessel emerged beyond Neptune under full legal authentication and transmitted diplomatic status before advancing.

No fleet followed.

That was deliberate.

Sol was neutral ground.

House von Hjertstedt respected that neutrality.

Lars stood on the bridge in full Primarch uniform.

Black.

Deep crimson.

Old campaign markings at the collar.

No unnecessary decoration.

His officers stood behind him.

“Sol Control confirms diplomatic corridor.”

“Escort?”

“Terran neutral-security frigates.”

Lars nodded.

“Accept.”

The cruiser moved inward.

Neptune fell behind.

Then Saturn.

Jupiter's defensive bastions appeared across the tactical screens.

Earth remained distant.

Blue.

Small.

Important.

Lars watched it without speaking.

The emergency briefing was called on Luna.

Not Earth.

Too many civilians.

Too much symbolism.

Luna's Concordat chambers were built for exactly this kind of crisis.

Terran representatives arrived first.

Then observers from the Throne Imperium.

The Hiigaran Restoration sent a gate-security delegation.

Several smaller human states joined remotely.

Nobody had yet been told everything.

Lars entered last.

Conversation stopped.

The Terran chairwoman stood.

“Primarch von Hjertstedt.”

“Chairwoman.”

“You requested emergency human-security status.”

“Yes.”

“For Seraph involvement?”

Lars looked around the chamber.

Imperial uniforms.

Hiigaran lineage officers.

Terran security.

Neutral judges.

Everyone knew what the word Seraph meant.

Nobody needed a history lesson.

“Yes.”

The room became much quieter.

Lars activated the central display.

Ardent appeared.

“Ardent Judicial Authority operates three major stations, several settled worlds, and a warfleet approximately three times its declared legal strength.”

The Imperial representative frowned.

“That alone is not grounds for intervention.”

“No.”

Lars changed the display.

Project Shepherd appeared.

Prisoner transfers.

False warrants.

Missing detainees.

Genetic classifications.

Then Seraph custody markings.

Nobody interrupted now.

Lars continued.

“Ardent officials have transferred human prisoners into Seraph hands.”

The Terran chairwoman looked sick.

“Knowingly?”

“At senior levels, yes.”

Another file appeared.

Conversion experiments.

Host-retention studies.

Failed implantation records.

Lars did not show everything.

Some horrors did not belong on a diplomatic screen.

Then he displayed the cryogenic vault.

House names appeared.

Names recorded dead decades earlier.

The Hiigaran delegate leaned forward.

“They kept them alive?”

“For research.”

Lars's voice hardened.

“My son found living members of my family beneath Ardent Special Custody.”

The Throne Imperial representative finally understood why Lars himself had come.

“And High Marshal Joel von Hjertstedt?”

“Currently inside Ardent territory.”

“Anton?”

“With him.”

Lars almost smiled.

“Which means Ardent's situation is already deteriorating.”

A few people around the chamber understood that remark differently than others.

Lars changed the display again.

Three Ardent stations.

The hidden fleet.

House banners beginning to reposition outside the sector.

“This is the decision before us.”

The room waited.

“If Ardent submits to inspection, releases prisoners, opens its records and purges Seraph influence, the House will support restoration of lawful authority.”

Then the red marks appeared.

Gatehold.

Bastion.

Central.

“If they resist…”

Lars looked around the chamber.

“…House von Hjertstedt will declare an Excision Campaign.”

The Terran chairwoman asked quietly:

“And seize Ardent territory?”

“Yes.”

The answer came without hesitation.

“Until the Seraph presence is dead.”

The Imperial delegate stiffened.

“You are openly claiming the right of conquest.”

Lars looked at him.

“Against the Seraph?”

Silence.

That question had an old answer.

Every major human power had burned fallen systems before.

Every one of them carried records they preferred not to discuss.

Lars continued.

“The House normally leaves territory when the contract ends.”

He looked toward Ardent.

“This is not a normal contract.”

The tactical display showed Joel's latest signal.

ARDENT WARFLEET MOBILIZING.

Lars read it.

Then smiled.

Coldly.

“Apparently they are making the decision for us.”

---

## 4. The Rose of Judgment

The secure channel opened from Luna.

Joel stood inside Ardent Special Custody Command.

Anton leaned against the tactical table beside him.

Christer and Daniel were still below with the recovered elders and the Shepherd archives.

The image stabilized.

Primarch Lars von Hjertstedt appeared first.

Joel straightened.

“Father.”

Anton smiled.

“You still look angry.”

“I am.”

“That is reassuring.”

Lars ignored him.

Then someone stepped into view beside the Primarch.

Joel’s expression changed immediately.

Annette von Hjertstedt wore the black and crimson uniform of the Head Justicar.

Joel’s wife.

Christer and Daniel’s sister.

Fleet strategist.

And one of the few people inside House von Hjertstedt who could make senior officers go quiet simply by entering a room.

Her long peach-coloured hair fell around her shoulders and down her back.

Most of the time, it hid the two small horns at the sides of her head.

Those horns were not ornamental.

Inside them lived two advanced AI systems.

Kira.

Lucy.

But what drew everyone’s attention was not the horns.

It was the flower in Annette’s hair.

A small pink rose pin sat above her left temple.

Elegant.

Delicate.

Harmless-looking.

Inside the House, almost nobody saw it that way.

The rose was the Justicar Shield.

When Annette wore it openly, it meant she was not merely attending as family.

She was acting under full judicial authority.

Evidence would be preserved.

Commands would be examined.

Corruption would be dragged into the light.

And rank would not protect anyone.

Anton saw it.

His smile weakened.

“Oh.”

Joel looked at him.

“Yes.”

“You noticed.”

“Yes.”

“That is unfortunate.”

Annette heard him.

“Good evening, Anton.”

Anton straightened slightly.

“Head Justicar.”

Joel almost laughed.

Annette looked toward him.

“And you.”

Joel’s voice softened.

“Annette.”

“You found Seraph laboratories without me.”

“I did not arrange them.”

“That is not an excuse.”

Lars looked between them.

“Can the family discussion wait until after the war?”

Annette smiled.

“Of course, Primarch.”

She stepped toward the Luna tactical table.

Ardent appeared between them.

Three major stations.

Several settled worlds.

Hidden yards.

A warfleet almost three times its declared size.

Joel’s present force remained concentrated near Central.

Ardent ships were moving.

Annette reached up and moved part of her peach hair behind one ear.

The first horn became visible.

A thin line of light ran through it.

A cool female voice entered the channel.

“Kira active.”

Annette moved the hair on the opposite side.

The second horn appeared.

Another voice followed.

Warmer.

“Lucy active. Strategic model synchronized.”

Anton smiled.

“Hello, girls.”

Lucy replied:

“Hello, Anton. Your probability of making this operation more difficult than necessary remains high.”

Daniel’s voice came over the lower-level channel.

“Accurate.”

Anton frowned.

“This family has no loyalty.”

Annette ignored them.

Kira reorganized the Ardent map.

Fleet contacts separated.

Central Defence Fleet.

Gatehold Fleet.

Bastion Reserve.

Then more contacts appeared.

Commercial hulls.

Customs ships.

Cargo vessels.

Several changed classification.

Hidden warships.

Mara stepped closer to Joel’s display.

“How did she identify those?”

Annette touched the first horn.

“Kira tracks military logistics.”

Kira answered:

“Fuel consumption exceeds Ardent’s declared military requirement by 214 percent.”

Lucy added:

“They hid hulls. They failed to hide what the hulls consume.”

Joel smiled faintly.

“That sounds like you.”

Annette looked at him.

“It sounds like logistics.”

Lars pointed toward the three major stations.

“Joel.”

“Yes?”

“You are not attacking all three with the force currently at Central.”

“I know.”

“Good.”

Lars continued.

“You hold Central.”

“Secure the elders.”

“Preserve Project Shepherd evidence.”

“Prevent military traffic from leaving with Seraph material, prisoners, or archive cores.”

Joel nodded.

“And if they attack?”

Lars smiled.

“Then stop asking questions you already know the answer to.”

Anton laughed.

There was the Primarch Joel remembered.

Annette leaned closer to the display.

Her expression changed.

Less wife.

More Head Justicar.

“Joel.”

He looked toward her.

“Seraph involvement means we assume the entire Ardent command chain may contain compromised elements.”

“Yes.”

“That does not mean we declare the entire population infected.”

“Agreed.”

“We separate negligence, corruption, collaboration, and conversion.”

Joel nodded.

“Agreed.”

“People who took money face trial.”

“Yes.”

“People forced into cooperation are investigated.”

“Yes.”

“Confirmed Seraph hosts go into quarantine.”

Joel’s expression hardened.

“And active Seraph facilities?”

Annette touched the pink rose pin.

The tiny movement was enough to make Anton stop smiling again.

“Destroyed.”

The word was quiet.

Final.

Mara looked from Annette to Joel.

She understood now why the flower mattered.

Annette continued.

“The Rose is open.”

Joel stared at her.

“Full Justicar authority?”

“Yes.”

Even Lars looked toward her.

That meant every House unit involved would preserve records.

Every prisoner transfer.

Every shot.

Every warrant.

Every Ardent official who tried to destroy evidence would become part of the case.

The pink rose in Annette’s peach hair had just become more dangerous than some battleships.

Anton muttered:

“Someone in Ardent is going to have a very bad day.”

Lucy replied:

“Statistically, several thousand people.”

“Thank you, Lucy.”

“You are welcome.”

Annette changed the tactical display.

“Joel, I want Project Shepherd intact.”

Anton frowned.

“I thought we were destroying it.”

“The organization, yes.”

She pointed toward the files.

“The evidence, no.”

Her voice became colder.

“I want every false warrant.”

“Every payment.”

“Every prisoner.”

“Every transfer route.”

“Every official who signed.”

“Every intermediary who profited.”

“Every Seraph contact.”

“We follow the chain until it stops.”

Joel watched her.

“That could cross several states.”

“I know.”

“Possibly major factions.”

“I know.”

Lars spoke then.

“If it reaches a major faction, evidence comes to Sol before escalation.”

Annette nodded.

“The House does not start a human war on suspicion.”

Joel looked toward the Ardent fleet.

“No.”

Anton smiled faintly.

“But if the evidence is real?”

The room became still.

Lars looked directly at him.

“Then we deal with reality.”

An alarm sounded in Ardent Command.

Mara turned.

“High Marshal. Central Defence Fleet is accelerating.”

Kira answered at once:

“Weapons-range transition projected.”

Lucy followed:

“Probability of hostile opening fire now exceeds seventy-two percent.”

Annette moved another lock of peach hair aside.

Both horns were visible now.

The pink rose remained between them.

Beautiful.

Terrible.

“Joel.”

“Yes?”

“Try not to destroy everything before I arrive.”

Anton laughed.

Joel looked toward the tactical display.

Ardent warships were moving into Hammer Range.

His crimson eyes brightened.

“No promises.”

---

## 5. Three Blades

Annette reached Ardent before the fleets opened fire.

Her command cruiser emerged under full House authentication, followed by a strategic escort and two Sanctuary tenders.

Joel met her in Ardent Central Command.

Annette entered wearing full Head Justicar black.

Her long peach hair flowed behind her.

The pink rose of the Justicar Shield sat above her left temple.

House officers who had been talking stopped.

Anton noticed.

He leaned toward Christer.

“Everyone suddenly remembers their paperwork.”

Christer nodded.

“Healthy instinct.”

Annette looked toward them.

Both became silent.

Joel smiled.

“Welcome.”

Annette kissed him once.

Then she turned toward the tactical table.

“War first.”

Her peach hair shifted.

Two small horns became visible.

Kira activated first.

“Strategic network synchronized.”

Lucy followed.

“Three primary objectives loaded.”

The Ardent state appeared above the table.

Three stations dominated the map.

Ardent Central.

The judicial and administrative capital.

Ardent Bastion.

The heavy military station protecting the industrial worlds.

Ardent Gatehold.

The fortified gate complex controlling the main routes in and out.

Around them sat several smaller worlds and Ardent’s hidden warfleet.

Annette touched the table.

The map divided into three operational zones.

“We do not attack them one after another.”

Anton smiled.

“Good.”

Annette looked at him.

“We attack them at the same time.”

His smile became wider.

“Better.”

Joel studied the projection.

“Three fleets.”

“Yes.”

Kira highlighted Central.

Annette pointed.

“First Fleet holds Central.”

The House formation appeared.

An Oath command core.

Heavy cruisers.

Vigil screens.

Assault ships.

Tenders.

Joel’s existing forces became the centre of it.

“You command this one,” Annette said.

Joel nodded.

“Objective?”

“Keep Central isolated. Preserve Shepherd records. Secure the elders. Break any fleet attempting to relieve the other stations.”

Joel looked toward the civilian sections.

“And avoid turning the capital into a battlefield.”

“Exactly.”

Lucy highlighted Bastion.

The second fleet appeared.

Heavier.

More assault-oriented.

Anton looked interested immediately.

Annette looked at him.

“No.”

“I did not say anything.”

“You were thinking loudly.”

Christer laughed.

Annette continued.

“Second Fleet takes Bastion.”

Anton looked toward her.

“Who commands?”

Annette displayed the command structure.

Anton.

Supported by Christer.

A heavy Banner capital sat at the centre.

Two assault carriers.

Destroyers.

Boarding cruisers.

Iron Marines and Crimson Marines prepared for station seizure.

Christer studied the approach.

“Bastion has the largest concentration of Ardent fleet mass.”

Annette nodded.

“That is why you are going there.”

Anton laughed.

“Now I feel appreciated.”

“You are going there because Bastion requires someone comfortable with ugly close-range problems.”

“Still appreciated.”

Then Gatehold appeared.

The third fleet.

Daniel leaned closer.

Annette looked toward him.

“You get Gatehold.”

Daniel studied the fortress.

Three gate-defense rings.

Missile platforms.

Authentication stations.

A large customs fleet that had somehow acquired military-grade armour.

“And if they try to shut the gate?”

“You stop them.”

“If they destroy the relay?”

“You take it before they can.”

“If the whole thing is compromised?”

Annette’s fingers touched the pink rose.

“Then you hold the gate until I decide whether it remains open.”

Daniel nodded.

No joke.

Gatehold mattered.

Whoever controlled it controlled whether Ardent could escape, reinforce, or call outside help.

Three blades now pointed into Ardent: Central, Bastion and Gatehold.

Joel looked toward Annette.

“And you?”

She smiled.

“I command all three.”

Anton sighed dramatically.

“Of course.”

Kira began updating movement windows.

“Central Fleet ready in forty-three minutes.”

Lucy followed.

“Bastion Fleet can begin covert departure under commercial traffic masking.”

Kira:

“Gatehold Fleet requires two additional fuel tenders before full campaign endurance is confirmed.”

Annette looked toward Daniel.

“You wait for the tenders.”

Daniel nodded.

Anton frowned.

“We could move now.”

Annette looked at him.

“No.”

“We have fuel.”

“You have enough fuel to arrive.”

“Yes.”

“You also require enough fuel to fight, capture ships, tow damaged hulls, support civilians and return.”

Anton looked toward Joel.

Joel smiled.

“She wins.”

“She always wins logistics arguments.”

Annette ignored them.

The fleets began moving.

Not charging.

Positioning.

Vigil frigates slipped into outer sensor space.

Tenders shifted behind protected lanes.

Assault carriers moved under low emissions.

House witness drones seeded approaches.

Ardent watched three fronts slowly close around it.

But they were not the only ones watching.

Kira suddenly marked new contacts outside Ardent territory.

Different colours.

Not hostile.

Not House.

Annette’s eyes narrowed.

“Interesting.”

Joel looked toward the map.

A Throne Imperium observation group had moved closer to Ardent’s outer lanes.

Hiigaran vessels had appeared near a secondary relay.

Terran diplomatic traffic had increased around a neighbouring neutral system.

Anton stared.

“They are gathering.”

Lucy answered:

“Public explanation: humanitarian observation.”

Kira added:

“Probability that this is the sole purpose: low.”

Annette zoomed out.

The pattern became clearer.

Other major powers were positioning around Ardent.

Not to save it.

To benefit from what happened after.

The Imperium could claim responsibility for restoring lawful government.

The Hiigarans could demand gate-access concessions.

Terran negotiators could offer reconstruction loans and military advisers.

Everyone saw the same thing:

House von Hjertstedt was about to spend blood and ships breaking Ardent.

Someone else might try to collect the political reward.

Joel smiled faintly.

“There it is.”

Annette looked toward him.

“What?”

“The part where everyone waits for us to do the dangerous work.”

Anton laughed.

“And then arrives with paperwork.”

Annette touched the pink rose in her hair.

“Then we make certain our paperwork is better.”

That silenced even Anton.

Outside, three Hjertstedt fleets began their approach.

Central tightened.

Bastion disappeared into silent transit formation.

Gatehold received its final tenders.

Ardent’s warfleet reacted.

Drives lit.

Weapons warmed.

And beyond the coming battle, the major powers of human space quietly moved pieces toward the board.

Not yet enemies.

Not yet allies.

Just waiting to see what House von Hjertstedt would win for them.

---

## 6. The Fleet Comes for Central

Ardent Central was already taken.

The command decks were secure.

Special Custody was under House control.

The Shepherd archives were being copied under Justicar seal.

Below, House medics prepared Hakon and the recovered elders for transfer.

Joel no longer needed to remain on the station.

That did not mean Ardent Central was left leaderless.

The House never worked that way.

Several senior officers had already arrived from the rapid-response elements.

Banner commanders.

Justicars.

Marine colonels.

Logistics chiefs.

Medical officers.

Men and women whose names meant little outside House circles, but who could run a captured station without waiting for Joel to tell them where to stand.

Senior Justicar Elin Varberg took legal control of Special Custody.

Banner-Captain Tomas Rydell assumed military responsibility for the station perimeter.

Colonel Freja Sorn took command of the internal marine defence.

Chaplain-Mechanist Oskar Vale took control of power, life support, reactor security and damage control.

None of them asked whether they were important enough.

They simply took their stations.

Joel preferred it that way.

Then every tactical display turned red.

“Contact bloom!”

The sensor officer’s voice cut across Central Command.

Ardent warships were burning toward the station.

Not patrol craft.

Cruisers formed the centre.

Destroyers and frigates spread ahead.

Assault transports came behind them.

The Central Defence Fleet had committed.

Banner-Captain Rydell stepped toward the tactical table.

“They intend to retake Central.”

Justicar Varberg looked toward the Shepherd vault status.

“Then they are too late.”

The first missile wave launched.

Joel was already moving.

“Rydell.”

“High Marshal.”

“Central is yours.”

Rydell nodded once.

“Understood.”

“Protect the civilians.”

“Yes.”

“Protect the elders.”

“Yes.”

“Protect the archive.”

Rydell’s expression hardened.

“They will need to kill us first.”

Joel smiled faintly.

“Good answer.”

The evacuation corridor became controlled chaos.

Medical shuttles moved elders toward Sanctuary tenders.

Justicars carried archive cores under armed guard.

Station civilians were being evacuated away from exposed hull sections.

Joel ran through it all toward Dock Three.

No command coat.

No ceremony.

Combat uniform.

Sidearm.

Crimson eyes.

His cutter waited under emergency launch status.

The pilot shouted as he entered.

“High Marshal, Ardent fleet is already entering Hammer Range!”

“Launch.”

The cutter broke free from Central.

Space outside was already burning.

Point-defence fire flashed around the station.

Missile trails crossed the dark.

House Vigil frigates moved between the enemy fleet and Ardent Central.

One destroyer rolled away trailing armour fragments.

Ahead waited Joel’s capital ship.

Dark.

Massive.

House heraldry lit across its command spine.

Its docking bay was already open.

The cutter accelerated.

A hostile salvo crossed its route.

“Brace!”

One missile vanished under point defence.

Then another.

A third passed close enough to throw the cutter sideways.

Joel barely moved.

“Keep going.”

The cutter entered the capital ship’s bay at dangerous speed.

Magnetic arrestors caught it.

Before the hull had fully settled, Joel was out.

Crew lined the corridor.

“High Marshal aboard!”

The bridge doors opened.

Joel entered.

The tactical officer turned.

“Central Banner forming.”

“Sanctuary tenders?”

“Behind station shadow.”

“Good.”

Joel took the command position.

The enemy fleet filled the forward display.

The investigation was finished.

Now they held what they had taken.

Three fast cutters launched from Central’s opposite military collar.

Anton’s went first.

Destination: Bastion Fleet.

Christer followed on the same front.

Daniel broke away toward Gatehold.

Anton opened the family channel.

“I dislike leaving before the shooting starts.”

Joel answered from his bridge.

“You are flying toward more shooting.”

Anton laughed.

“Excellent.”

Christer’s cutter rolled past a House destroyer.

“Bastion reports heavy Ardent fleet activation.”

Anton answered:

“Even better.”

Daniel’s cutter accelerated for the outer corridor.

“Gatehold is sealing civilian traffic.”

Joel’s expression hardened.

“Do not let them close the route.”

“I know.”

Annette’s voice entered the channel.

Calm.

Precise.

“Three fronts remain unchanged.”

Kira projected fleet routes.

Lucy updated hostile concentrations.

“Joel holds Central.”

“Anton and Christer take Bastion.”

“Daniel isolates Gatehold.”

The cutters separated.

For a few moments the brothers could see each other only as tactical icons.

Then Anton and Christer vanished into Bastion Fleet traffic.

Daniel disappeared toward Gatehold.

Joel remained at Central.

The first Ardent cruisers fired.

Heavy rounds crossed Hammer Range.

Joel’s capital ship rolled.

“Spinal charge ready.”

“Lead cruiser,” Joel ordered.

“Drive section only.”

“Confirmed.”

Joel’s eyes turned crimson.

“Fire.”

The spinal weapon discharged.

The bridge trembled.

Across the dark, the lead Ardent cruiser lost its engine bloom.

Still alive.

Still capturable.

Then Central transmitted another warning.

“High Marshal! Additional hostile launches!”

Joel turned.

Boarding cutters.

Assault pods.

Dozens.

Then hundreds.

Not aimed at the fleet.

Aimed at the station.

At Shepherd.

At the elders.

Joel opened fleet-wide communications.

“Central Banner.”

Every House ship answered.

“Listening.”

Joel stared at the swarm.

“Nothing reaches Central.”

Inside Ardent Central, Colonel Freja Sorn watched the same contacts multiply.

Around her waited some of the most difficult troops the House possessed.

Elite marines.

Experienced boarders.

Veterans who had been disciplined repeatedly for shouting over command channels, unauthorized wagering, unnecessary challenges, and treating dangerous operations like competitive sport.

They were also terrifyingly effective.

One marine stared at the tactical display.

Then slowly smiled.

“Boarders?”

Another leaned closer.

“How many?”

The sensor officer answered.

“Several hundred incoming.”

The room erupted.

“Yes!”

“FINALLY!”

“WHO HAS CIVILIAN RESCUES?”

“I CALL BREACH COUNT!”

“NO, KILLS AND CAPTURES ARE SEPARATE!”

A marine raised both hands.

“Commander! We need categories!”

Colonel Sorn stared at them.

“Categories.”

“Yes, ma’am.”

“For what?”

The marine started counting on armored fingers.

“Most confirmed hostiles neutralized.”

“Most prisoners taken alive.”

“Most civilians evacuated.”

“Most wounded recovered.”

Someone shouted from the back:

“BONUS FOR SAVING AN ELDER!”

Another answered:

“That cannot be a category! There are only a few elders!”

“That makes them worth more points!”

Sorn closed her eyes.

A veteran sergeant leaned toward her.

“They are motivated.”

“I can see that.”

One marine shouted:

“WHAT ARE THE PRIZES?”

That triggered another wave.

“Liquor!”

“Leave!”

“First choice of salvage!”

“NO SALVAGE, JUSTICAR WILL KILL US!”

Someone pointed toward the pink Justicar seal displayed on the wall.

Everyone became slightly quieter.

Then:

“Fine! Expensive liquor!”

Colonel Sorn looked toward Banner-Captain Rydell.

He stood at the rear of the chamber, trying very hard not to laugh.

“Captain.”

“Yes?”

“They are asking leadership for prizes.”

Rydell considered the incoming boarding wave.

Then the marines.

Then the civilian evacuation map.

“Fine.”

The room went silent.

“Official categories.”

The marines leaned forward.

“Most civilians saved.”

Cheers.

“Most prisoners taken alive.”

More cheers.

“Most wounded recovered under fire.”

Louder.

“And…”

Rydell paused.

“…most confirmed boarders stopped before reaching Shepherd.”

The entire chamber erupted.

Colonel Sorn stared at him.

“You have made this worse.”

Rydell smiled.

“Probably.”

A marine shouted:

“PRIZES?”

Rydell opened a direct channel to Joel’s flagship.

“High Marshal.”

Joel answered.

“Report.”

Rydell looked at the screaming elite marines.

“I require authorization for motivational expenditures.”

Joel went silent.

Then:

“How expensive?”

Rydell smiled.

“Potentially very.”

Joel moved the reply to a private command circuit.

“Your own contribution?”

“The bottle you have been trying to win off me for nine years.”

Joel glanced at the interception plot. Rydell's reserves were already moving toward the threatened civilian corridors.

“You chose your terms before calling.”

“You do it to everyone else. I thought you would appreciate the courtesy.”

A fresh alarm sounded. Rydell gave Sorn the reserve assignments without another word about prizes.

From the background, hundreds of marines started cheering as the first enemy cutters approached the hull.

The battle for Ardent Central had not begun yet.

But the betting already had.

---

## 7. The Maniacs of the Grey Nomad

The first Ardent boarding cutter struck Central hard enough to shake dust from the old ceiling panels.

Then came six more.

Colonel Freja Sorn watched the tactical display fill with breach alarms.

“Dock Four.”

“Maintenance Spine Two.”

“Habitation Access Seven.”

“Outer Medical.”

Her officers began issuing orders.

The elite marines behind them began cheering.

Not disciplined cheering.

Not ceremonial cheering.

The kind heard before a bar fight.

“THAT ONE IS MINE!”

“YOU SAID THAT LAST TIME!”

“I STILL WON!”

“YOU CHEATED!”

Sorn looked toward Banner-Captain Rydell.

“They are getting worse.”

Rydell smiled faintly.

“They came from the Grey Nomad.”

That explained far too much.

The forty-eight assault veterans Christer and Daniel had brought into the convoy had never really gone away.

They had expanded.

Picked up friends.

Recruited other loud specialists.

Passed on bad habits.

The result was now several elite marine teams who combined terrifying combat skill with almost no respect for silence, ceremony, or reasonable behavior.

Sorn opened the marine channel.

“All teams. Stop shouting.”

A voice answered immediately.

“WE ARE NOT SHOUTING!”

Another:

“THIS IS NORMAL VOLUME!”

Sorn closed her eyes.

“Grey Nomad people.”

The channel became quiet.

Mostly.

“You are responsible for this,” she said.

Someone answered:

“Technically Heron and Ward brought us.”

Another voice:

“YES! BLAME CHRISTER!”

A third:

“AND DANIEL!”

Rydell laughed.

“Accurate.”

Then the first breach door exploded.

The laughter stopped.

The marines moved.

Fast.

Team One hit Dock Four.

Ardent boarders came through under heavy shields.

The House marines met them before they cleared the smoke.

One marine slammed a boarding shield into the lead attacker and drove him back into his own line.

Another jumped onto the overturned docking frame and fired down from above.

A third shouted:

“ALIVE COUNTS DOUBLE!”

“WHO SAID?”

“ME!”

“That is not authority!”

“It is now!”

The fight lasted less than two minutes.

Twelve boarders surrendered.

Four more were dragged out wounded.

Seven never left the breach alive.

The marines immediately started arguing.

“TWELVE PRISONERS!”

“Shared capture!”

“No!”

“I touched six first!”

“YOU CANNOT CLAIM PEOPLE BY TOUCHING THEM!”

At Habitation Access Seven, another team found civilians trapped behind a jammed pressure door.

Their sergeant looked at the incoming hostile markers.

Then at the civilians.

“Rescue category.”

Everyone suddenly moved even faster.

One marine ripped open an emergency panel.

Another crawled into the maintenance trunk.

Two others carried an elderly couple out before the Ardent boarders reached the corner.

“THAT IS TWO!”

“Children count individually!”

“Obviously!”

A medic shouted:

“Stop turning civilians into points!”

The sergeant looked offended.

“We are saving them!”

“You are also counting them!”

“Motivation!”

“Then carry the stretcher.”

The sergeant took the heavier end. He stopped counting and handed the stretcher to the waiting medical team beyond the door. His rear pair stayed at the junction until the last civilian was through.

The first Ardent soldier appeared.

The sergeant raised his rifle.

“Wrong corridor.”

On Joel’s flagship, the family channel was open.

Joel stood over the tactical table while reports from Central rolled in.

Anton had already reached his fleet.

Christer was transferring to Bastion command.

Daniel was approaching Gatehold.

Annette remained at strategic command with Kira and Lucy.

Rydell’s request came through again.

Motivational prize structure required.

Anton started laughing.

Joel stared at the message.

“No.”

Christer answered from his cutter.

“We created them.”

Daniel corrected him.

“You brought them.”

“You were there.”

“So were you.”

Anton cut in.

“The Grey Nomad teams were excellent.”

Joel looked toward the screen.

“They turned prisoner recovery into a competitive event.”

“Yes.”

“That is not normal.”

Anton paused.

“It is effective.”

Annette entered the channel.

Her peach hair was pulled back from one horn.

The pink rose pin remained visible.

“We need prizes that reward the right behavior.”

Joel looked toward her.

“You are taking this seriously.”

“Yes.”

Anton sounded delighted.

“Good.”

Annette began listing categories.

“Most civilians rescued.”

Christer nodded.

“Prize should be large.”

“Most prisoners taken alive,” Daniel added.

Anton groaned.

“Boring.”

Annette looked directly into his feed.

“Most prisoners alive.”

Anton immediately corrected himself.

“Excellent category.”

Joel smiled.

“Most wounded recovered under fire.”

Christer added:

“Best breach team.”

Daniel:

“Best captured intelligence.”

Anton:

“Highest confirmed kill count.”

Silence.

Annette looked at him.

Anton shrugged.

“They are going to track it anyway.”

Joel sighed.

“He is right.”

Annette considered.

“Fine. But kill count only applies to confirmed hostile combatants actively resisting.”

Anton grinned.

“There is my Justicar.”

Annette ignored him.

Then came the important question.

“The prizes.”

Christer smiled.

“I have an old pre-Fall pistol.”

Daniel answered:

“No. They will fight each other for that.”

“That is the point.”

“No.”

Anton leaned toward his display.

“I have a bottle.”

Joel immediately said:

“No.”

“You do not know which bottle.”

“I know you.”

“It is from the southern reserve.”

Joel became quiet.

Christer did too.

Daniel stared.

Annette raised one eyebrow.

“That bottle?”

Anton smiled.

“Yes.”

Joel finally spoke.

“That is worth more than some small ships.”

“Which makes it motivating.”

Annette considered.

Then smiled.

“Good.”

Joel looked betrayed.

She continued.

“Each category winner gets something different.”

Annette attached Sorn's operational conditions before releasing the notice. Teams kept their assigned sectors; medical priority decided rescue order. No extra exposure for a score, no claim over a prisoner, and no prize for disobeying an order. The sealed case would belong to the winning team's table, served by a host in small measures after relief from duty. Ordinary House marines did not inherit the family's tolerance with their uniforms.

The tactical display updated.

CIVILIAN RESCUE: one sealed case of Vintergard Winter Reserve.

PRISONERS TAKEN ALIVE: priority selection from captured personal equipment and ceremonial salvage, under Justicar approval.

WOUNDED RECOVERED: one month paid leave at Vinterholm plus family transport.

BEST INTELLIGENCE CAPTURE: direct commendation and personal dinner with senior House command.

Anton looked offended.

“That is a prize?”

Christer laughed.

“For some people.”

Then Annette added the final category.

CONFIRMED COMBAT SCORE: one bottle from the Primarch’s restricted feast reserve.

Everyone went silent.

Joel stared.

“You cannot offer Father’s reserve.”

Annette smiled.

“I can ask.”

The authorization returned through the command network before the prize notice went out.

Lars had added one line beneath his approval.

RYDELL STILL OWES ME A BOTTLE. DO NOT ACCEPT IT AS HIS CONTRIBUTION.

Rydell read it on Central's display.

“That debt was old before Joel took command,” he told Sorn. “He can afford to wait another watch.”

Anton started laughing.

Daniel joined him.

Christer shook his head.

“Now they will fight like demons.”

Joel looked toward Central’s live tactical feed.

The marine channel had received the prize notice.

For three seconds there was silence.

Then Ardent Central exploded with cheering.

“PRIMARCH RESERVE!”

“MOVE!”

“GET THE CIVILIANS FIRST!”

“NO KILLING SURRENDERING TARGETS, YOU IDIOTS!”

“INTELLIGENCE TEAM, FIND ME AN OFFICER!”

“MEDICS! I WANT THAT MONTH OFF!”

Colonel Sorn stared at the tactical display.

The marines had doubled their movement speed.

Rydell leaned beside her.

“Motivated.”

Sorn looked at him.

“I hate all of you.”

Another breach alarm sounded.

The elite teams turned toward it.

Weapons came up.

Someone shouted:

“GREY NOMAD RULES!”

Another answered:

“WE ARE NOT ON THE GREY NOMAD!”

“SPIRITUAL CONDITION!”

Then they charged.

Across the station, Ardent boarders discovered something worse than disciplined defenders.

They had found disciplined defenders with prizes.

---

## 8. Bastion Ignites

Anton’s cutter reached the Bastion Fleet just as the first warning changed from yellow to red.

Christer’s cutter was thirty seconds behind.

Ahead, Ardent Bastion filled the tactical display.

The station was not elegant like Central.

It was built for war.

Armoured rings.

Heavy docks.

Missile towers.

Repair yards.

Fleet anchorages hidden behind reinforced orbital structures.

And now those anchorages were opening.

Anton leaned forward in his seat.

“Oh, that is beautiful.”

The pilot glanced back.

“Sir?”

“Warships.”

Dozens of drive blooms appeared around Bastion.

Cruisers.

Destroyers.

Assault carriers.

Missile barges.

Then two capital hulls began moving from behind the station’s armour shadow.

Anton started laughing.

“Christer.”

His brother answered over the family channel.

“I see them.”

“They hid capitals.”

“Yes.”

“Two.”

“Yes.”

Anton’s crimson eyes brightened.

“This is already better than Central.”

Christer sighed.

“You are impossible.”

Their cutters split.

Anton’s went toward the assault flagship.

Christer headed for the fleet command capital.

House ships were already forming around them.

Vigil frigates spread into screens.

Destroyers moved forward.

Oath cruisers began building firing solutions.

Assault carriers opened their launch bays.

Behind everything came Sanctuary tenders.

Not glamorous.

Necessary.

The fleet had learned that long wars were won by ships carrying welders, medics, fuel and ammunition.

Anton’s cutter entered the assault flagship.

Magnetic clamps caught it.

He was out before the hatch fully opened.

Marines lined the passage.

Several were former Grey Nomad maniacs.

One shouted:

“BREACH COMMANDER!”

Another:

“YOU HEAR ABOUT CENTRAL?”

Anton stopped.

“What?”

“They have prizes!”

Anton stared.

“For what?”

“Everything!”

A marine began counting.

“Civilians!”

“Prisoners!”

“Wounded!”

“Kill count!”

Anton’s expression changed.

“They started without us.”

The marine looked offended.

“We need Bastion prizes.”

Anton smiled slowly.

“Oh, yes.”

Christer reached fleet command seconds later.

His bridge was already alive with reports.

“Bastion fleet departing anchorages!”

“Enemy missile barges moving to rear line!”

“Capital signatures confirmed!”

Christer stepped into command.

“Range?”

“Outer Hammer transition in seven minutes.”

“Witness net?”

“Live.”

“Civilian traffic?”

“Still moving from two commercial collars.”

Christer frowned.

“Ardent is launching a fleet while civilians are still in the battlespace.”

His tactical officer nodded.

“They may be using traffic clutter.”

Christer opened a channel to Anton.

“No mass fire toward the station-facing side.”

Anton answered immediately.

“I know.”

“Do you?”

“Yes.”

A pause.

“Probably.”

Christer closed his eyes.

Then Annette entered both command feeds.

Her face appeared beside the tactical map.

Peach hair.

Pink rose Justicar pin.

Both horn-mounted AIs active.

Kira spoke first.

“Bastion warfleet strength exceeds previous estimate by eighteen percent.”

Lucy followed.

“Several civilian transponders are masking military heat signatures.”

Christer’s jaw tightened.

“There.”

The map changed.

Three cargo ships detached from civilian traffic.

Armour panels opened.

Missile racks extended.

Anton laughed.

“They really are corrupt.”

Annette’s expression went cold.

“Witnessed.”

The pink rose caught the bridge light.

That mattered.

“Military vessels hiding among civilian traffic are now authenticated hostile contacts.”

Christer nodded.

“Understood.”

Anton added:

“Permission to make them regret the creativity?”

Annette looked at him.

“Disable.”

Anton smiled.

“Still good.”

The first missile wave came from Ardent.

Not small.

Hundreds.

“CONTACT BLOOM!”

The House screens reacted.

Vigil frigates moved first.

Point defence lit space.

Drones flooded the approach.

Decoy clouds opened.

The battle crossed fully into Hammer Range.

Missiles died in storms of light.

Others came through.

One House frigate took a hit across the bow and rolled away.

Another lost a sensor mast.

A destroyer’s engine bloom vanished for three seconds before restarting.

Anton watched from his assault flagship.

“Return fire.”

The gunner looked toward him.

“Targets?”

Anton pointed at the disguised missile ships.

“Those three first.”

“Drive kill?”

Anton smiled.
