“Make them stop being clever.”

House missiles launched.

Fewer than Ardent’s.

Better targeted.

The first disguised freighter lost manoeuvring control.

The second had both missile racks destroyed.

The third tried to retreat behind civilian traffic.

Christer’s voice came over the channel.

“Do not fire through the civilian line.”

Anton stared at the tactical display.

“They know that.”

“Yes.”

“They are using it.”

“Yes.”

Anton’s smile disappeared.

“Fine.”

He changed orders.

“Vigil Two and Three. Go around.”

The frigates accelerated.

Longer route.

Cleaner shot.

More dangerous.

But lawful.

That was House doctrine.

Then Bastion’s two capital ships fired.

The first spinal shot crossed space like a white scar.

It struck a House destroyer near the drive.

The ship spun.

The second shot hit an Oath cruiser’s forward armour.

The cruiser stayed in formation.

Barely.

Christer gripped the command rail.

“Damage?”

“Severe armour loss. Reactor stable.”

“Keep them in line.”

Anton’s voice came through.

“Those capitals are mine.”

Christer looked toward his display.

“No.”

“Half?”

“No.”

“One?”

“Anton.”

“You take one, I take one.”

Christer stared at the enemy formation.

Then smiled.

“Fine.”

Anton laughed like a madman.

The bridge crew around him started cheering.

Then one marine shouted:

“PRIZE FOR FIRST CAPITAL CAPTURE!”

Another:

“YES!”

Christer heard it over the shared channel.

“No.”

Anton leaned toward his microphone.

“We need motivation.”

“You are already motivated.”

“More motivation.”

Annette entered the channel.

“No personal destruction competitions.”

The marines groaned.

Then Lucy spoke:

“Alternative category proposed: intact capital capture.”

Everything became quiet.

Anton stared.

Christer slowly smiled.

Annette looked toward the tactical map.

“That I will allow.”

Kira added:

“Secondary categories: civilians protected during engagement, prisoners recovered, and salvage value preserved.”

Anton looked delighted.

“Now we are talking.”

Christer shook his head.

“Prize?”

Anton answered immediately.

“Something expensive.”

Annette looked thoughtful.

“Winner of intact capital capture gets first selection from the next Vintergard feast reserve shipment.”

Anton froze.

Christer did too.

One marine whispered:

“Feast reserve?”

Another:

“Is that the good stuff?”

Anton opened the fleet-wide assault channel.

“All boarding teams.”

Every marine listened.

“New category.”

The bridge erupted.

Christer shouted:

“Do not encourage them!”

Too late.

“INTACT CAPITAL!”

“NO REACTOR SHOTS!”

“SAVE THE BRIDGE!”

“PRISONERS COUNT!”

“WHOever scratches the engines buys drinks!”

Anton leaned back.

His crimson eyes burned.

Ahead, Bastion’s warfleet continued advancing.

House formations tightened.

Assault carriers moved forward.

Boarding pods began warming.

Christer looked at the first Ardent capital ship.

Anton looked at the second.

Annette watched both from strategic command.

Three fronts were becoming one family argument conducted with fleets.

Then Kira reported:

“Bastion station opening additional launch bays.”

More drive blooms appeared.

Hidden ships.

More than expected.

Lucy added:

“Enemy reserve force entering battle.”

Anton smiled.

“Good.”

Christer looked toward him.

“You keep saying that.”

Anton raised his heavy pistol from beside the command chair and locked it to his armour.

“Because they keep improving the prize.”

Outside, Hammer Range became fire.

Inside both fleets, boarding crews began arguing about who would claim the first capital alive.

---

## 9. First Capital Boarding

Bastion’s orbit became a furnace.

Two Ardent capital ships drove straight into Hammer Range, their escorts spreading around them in layered screens. House cruisers answered with disciplined lines, but the battle no longer looked like a skirmish around a station.

It looked like a warfleet duel.

Heavy guns opened first.

One Ardent capital ship fired a spinal mass driver that crossed the void like a white spear and hit a House Oath cruiser square across the forward armour. The impact rolled the cruiser half over, tore away plating, and sent burning fragments out behind it.

Christer’s command crew shouted damage reports.

“Winter Oath still in formation!”

“Forward batteries reduced!”

“Reactor stable!”

Christer nodded once. “Hold her in line.”

Then the House answered.

Three cruisers fired together. Their return volley struck the nearer Ardent capital along its port side. Armour flared, cracked, and vented a river of glowing debris. The enemy ship kept coming.

Anton watched from the assault flagship and grinned.

“There. That one.”

The tactical officer did not need to ask which.

The nearer capital had committed too hard. It wanted the centre broken. It wanted House ships to scatter so Bastion’s carriers could launch boarding waves into the confusion.

Anton preferred enemies who came forward.

Another exchange hit both lines.

Missiles crossed between the battle groups in clouds. Point defence burned so hot that space seemed full of white rain. A House destroyer vanished inside a strike bloom and emerged with one flank burning. An Ardent heavy cruiser lost its bow battery to a precise House salvo and drifted backward, alive but crippled.

Then the true slugfest began.

The two capital lines entered close Hammer Range and exchanged repeated heavy broadsides. Great shells smashed armour. Lance fire scarred hulls. Sensor ghosts and decoy fields burned away under saturation fire until the capital ships could see one another clearly and still refused to turn aside.

Christer’s bridge shook under another near hit.

“Enemy capital two adjusting angle!”

“Trying to cross our line!”

Christer opened a direct channel to the left wing. “Destroyers One through Three. Cut inside. Force her to turn.”

The House destroyers obeyed instantly, sprinting into the gap under a storm of defensive fire. One lost shields entirely. Another took a hit along its dorsal line. But the move worked. The Ardent capital shifted to face them, and in doing so exposed its damaged quarter to the House guns.

Anton barely listened.

He was already focused on the first capital.

Its escorts had drifted wider after the last exchange. Its point-defence envelope had gaps. Its port launch bay was damaged. Its captain was still pushing forward instead of withdrawing to a cleaner line.

Perfect.

“Assault carriers forward,” Anton ordered.

His bridge crew looked up.

“Now?”

“Yes.”

Christer’s voice hit the family channel. “You are doing this in the middle of a capital exchange.”

“Yes.”

“That is reckless.”

“Yes.”

Annette entered before the argument could grow.

“Anton.”

“Yes?”

“You have one opening.”

He smiled. “Then I only need one.”

Kira marked the path across the tactical display. Lucy overlaid the enemy point-defence blind angles.

“Window duration: ninety-one seconds.”

Anton was already moving toward the launch deck.

“More than enough.”

The assault carrier shook around him as it accelerated.

Anton stood inside the foremost boarding cutter with two full marine squads behind him. Former Grey Nomad maniacs filled the compartment in heavy assault plate, grinning like criminals promised good alcohol.

One of them raised a hand.

“Does intact capital capture still count if we break several very important doors?”

Anton considered. “Yes.”

The cutter bay launched.

At once the void became violence.

House capital ships kept hammering the Ardent line, not just to win the slugfest but to cover Anton’s approach. One Oath cruiser fired repeated broadsides into the target capital’s starboard batteries, forcing defensive guns away from the boarding vector. A destroyer group rushed its screen frigates, losing one ship but breaking the escort ring open.

Anton’s cutter raced through the gap.

Behind him, four more cutters and a wave of breaching pods followed.

Two pods died in point-defence fire.

Another spun away venting atmosphere.

The rest kept coming.

Anton’s cutter struck the enemy capital near the damaged launch bay. Magnetic claws bit hard. The breaching arm started cutting before the hull fully settled.

“Ready,” Anton said.

The marines behind him started chanting.

Not a battle hymn.

A prize list.

“Capital first!”

“Bridge alive!”

“Prisoners count!”

“Do not shoot the reactor crew!”

Anton laughed.

The hull opened.

They went in.

The first defenders waited behind a half-collapsed internal blast door. Ardent naval marines, disciplined and heavily armed. They fired into the breach the moment smoke rolled through.

Anton led anyway.

His heavy shield took the first burst. His return shot broke the lead defender’s position apart. Two Grey Nomad veterans went over him and into the gap. One shouted, “LEFT CORRIDOR IS MINE!” Another answered, “NO CLAIMS BEFORE COMMAND!”

The fighting turned immediate and brutal.

Anton’s team drove through the launch section and into the internal troop corridor. The enemy had numbers. The House had momentum.

Outside the hull, the capital slugfest kept raging. Every few seconds the entire enemy ship shuddered under impacts from House heavy guns or returned its own thunder across the void. Bulkheads sparked. Lighting died and reappeared blood-red. The ship was fighting for its life while Anton fought through its spine.

He loved it.

“Status,” he barked.

A marine answered, “Launch section secured!”

Another shouted, “Fifteen prisoners!”

A third called, “Bridge route marked!”

Anton opened the family channel. “I am inside.”

Christer’s dry voice answered through static and distant gunfire. “I had guessed.”

“And my ship is still alive?”

“Yes.”

“Excellent.”

Christer cut his own line of battle across the second Ardent capital and spoke to his gunners.

“Keep the pressure. Anton’s target does not get relief.”

More House broadsides crashed into the first capital’s outer armour. The ship staggered in formation, suddenly forced to fight two battles—one in its hull, one across the void.

Anton reached an internal junction and looked up the tactical feed. The route to the bridge was open but tightening. More defenders were moving down from command.

He smiled behind his visor.

“Good. Save me the walk.”

---

## 10. The Second Capital

Christer watched Anton’s boarding icons crawl through the first Ardent capital ship.

Then he looked toward the second.

It was bigger.

Better protected.

And still firing.

“Range?” Christer asked.

“Hammer-close,” his tactical officer answered. “Enemy capital is holding line with six cruisers and nine escorts.”

“Damage?”

“Moderate. Port armour weakened. Drives intact.”

Christer smiled.

“Good.”

His bridge officer looked toward him.

“Good?”

“I do not want an easy prize.”

The House command capital rolled.

Heavy batteries aligned.

Across space, the Ardent ship did the same.

Both fired.

The exchange hit like two buildings colliding.

Christer’s bridge shook violently.

Warning lights flashed.

“Forward armour breach!”

“Secondary communications offline!”

“Two point-defence clusters lost!”

Christer gripped the command rail.

“Return fire.”

House guns answered.

Mass-driver rounds tore through the Ardent capital’s outer plating.

One strike ripped open a missile bay.

Another shattered a broadside mount.

The enemy ship stayed in line.

“Again.”

The next salvo crossed at almost the same moment.

Ardent shells hit Christer’s starboard armour.

House fire struck the enemy’s port quarter.

Neither capital withdrew.

This was no elegant duel.

It was a slugfest.

Two massive ships trading armour, heat, ammunition and crew lives for position.

Around them, cruisers and destroyers fought their own battles. Missiles crossed through clouds of debris. Point defence filled the darkness with violent flashes. Damaged ships drifted away while tenders waited behind the line for any hull that could still be saved.

Annette’s voice entered the fleet channel.

“Christer.”

“Listening.”

“Kira predicts their captain will turn inward after the next broadside.”

Christer studied the enemy vector.

“Why?”

Lucy answered:

“Their starboard heat sinks are failing. They need to expose the opposite side.”

Christer smiled.

“Thank you.”

He waited.

The Ardent capital fired.

House armour screamed.

Then the enemy began turning.

Exactly as predicted.

Christer pointed.

“Now.”

Three House cruisers fired together.

Not at the reactor.

At the drive housings.

The first shot broke armour.

The second tore open external cooling.

The third struck the manoeuvring cluster.

The Ardent capital lurched.

Its formation collapsed around it.

Christer leaned forward.

“There.”

Then the sensor officer shouted:

“Unknown contacts!”

The tactical display changed.

New signatures appeared beyond Bastion.

Cold.

Large.

Moving strangely.

Christer’s smile vanished.

“How many?”

“Unclear. Four confirmed. More possible.”

“Ardent?”

The officer hesitated.

“No.”

Annette came onto the channel immediately.

Kira began analyzing the contacts.

“Hull profiles inconsistent with Ardent construction.”

Lucy added:

“Drive behavior also inconsistent.”

Christer stared toward the distant darkness.

One contact finally crossed confirmation threshold.

Its silhouette appeared.

Long.

Narrow.

Ancient-looking.

Layered armour curved around the hull like ribs.

Gold-black surfaces reflected Bastion’s distant light.

Christer knew the shape.

Everyone did.

“Seraph.”

The bridge went silent.

The Seraph Throne was not an unknown enemy. Every major human military knew what those ships meant: command seizures, host capture, conversion facilities, and battles where surviving the first attack did not guarantee the person returning home was still themselves.

Annette’s voice became colder.

“Do not engage until intent is confirmed.”

Christer nodded.

“Understood.”

Then Bastion opened new docking bays.

Not ship bays.

Troop bays.

Small assault craft launched.

At first Christer assumed they were Ardent reinforcements.

Then one of the House observation drones got close enough to transmit imagery.

The bridge crew stopped talking.

The first boarding craft contained figures in sealed armour.

Too tall.

Too thin in some places.

Too broad in others.

Bodies reshaped around machinery.

Some still had human proportions.

Others did not.

One tactical officer whispered:

“Husk Guard.”

The Seraph faction was infamous for turning failed or ruined hosts into sealed warforms used for boarding, palace seizure, and terror assaults.

But these looked worse.

Experimental.

One moved on four reinforced limbs with a human torso locked above them.

Another had one arm replaced by a heavy weapon mount while surgical braces held the chest closed.

A third still had part of a human face visible behind transparent armour.

Its mouth moved.

Nobody could hear what it was saying.

The next image showed more.

Humans with spinal support frames.

Bodies held upright by external machinery.

Helmets fused into surgical collars.

Some walked normally.

Some twitched.

Some moved with horrible precision.

The bridge officer beside Christer looked away.

“That is not an army.”

Christer stared at the screen.

“No.”

“It is a laboratory that learned to walk.”

The Ardent capital ahead suddenly changed its identification signal.

Old Ardent codes disappeared.

A different command layer appeared underneath.

Seraph authentication.

Christer’s crimson eyes lit.

There it was.

Proof.

Not corruption anymore.

Not suspicion.

Bastion was compromised.

Annette opened all three House command channels.

Joel appeared from Central.

Daniel from Gatehold.

Anton from inside the first enemy capital.

Annette stood between their tactical feeds, peach hair framing the pink rose of the Justicar Shield.

“The Rose is open.”

Nobody joked.

“Confirmed Seraph military presence at Bastion.”

Joel’s expression hardened.

Anton stopped smiling.

Daniel became completely still.

Annette continued:

“Excision threshold met.”

The tactical displays changed.

Bastion.

Its warships.

Its military docks.

Its hidden troop facilities.

All became red objectives.

Christer looked toward the crippled Ardent capital in front of him.

Then toward the Seraph ships emerging behind Bastion.

“Orders?”

Annette touched the rose pin.

“Take the capital.”

“Preserve evidence.”

“Then isolate Bastion.”

Anton’s voice came from inside his own captured target.

“And the Husk Guard?”

Annette’s expression did not change.

“Do not let them reach civilian sections.”

Anton’s eyes turned crimson.

“Understood.”

Outside, another Seraph ship entered confirmation range.

Then another.

The battle around Bastion had just become something much worse than an Ardent civil war.

---

## 11. Excision War

Annette did not raise her voice.

She did not need to.

Her peach hair framed the pink rose of the Justicar Shield as she stood between the three fleet feeds. Kira and Lucy burned tactical light across her small horn-mounted housings. Behind her, Bastion still burned. Ahead of Christer, the second capital ship limped under House fire. Behind Anton, Ardent decks were filling with Husk Guard and experimental soldiers.

Annette touched the rose pin.

“The Rose is open.”

Every House command channel went silent.

“Confirmed Seraph military presence. Confirmed Seraph host experimentation. Confirmed human transfer and conversion infrastructure. Confirmed compromised Ardent military command.”

She looked first to Joel, then to Daniel, then to Christer, and finally to Anton.

“By authority of the Head Justicar and witnessed under House command, I invoke full Excision Campaign authority across all Ardent territory.”

No one joked.

Not even Anton.

Kira changed the tactical color coding. Ardent Central, Bastion, Gatehold, the hidden yards, and the military worlds all shifted from red suspicion to black war priority.

Lucy followed.

“Excision War status active.”

At once another channel opened.

Primarch Lars von Hjertstedt stood on the Luna feed.

His face was harder than stone.

“Accepted.”

That single word carried the weight of Vintergard behind it.

Lars looked at the three battlefronts and then at the horror-images coming from Bastion: Husk Guard moving from launch bays, stitched soldiers in sealed harnesses, boarding craft marked with Seraph sigils beneath Ardent paint.

“I have seen enough.”

He enlarged the Bastion imagery until the whole channel could see it.

The transparent faceplate with the moving human mouth.

The quadrupedal armored thing with a human torso fixed above mechanical limbs.

The sealed combat shells made from failed men.

Mara, still with Joel at Central, turned away from one image.

Lars did not.

“They built that under judicial protection,” he said. “They used law to feed monsters.”

His crimson eyes brightened.

“No more.”

He opened a House-only command layer beneath the family feed.

“Order to Shield Command. Order to Skold. Order to the Black Vaults beneath Hjarta.”

One by one acknowledgements appeared.

Lars continued.

“Authorize Special House Deployment.”

Then he spoke the next words with old hatred.

“Release the Seraph Hunters.”

Even Anton went quiet.

These were not normal boarding troops.

Every House child knew the stories, though few had ever seen them. The Seraph Hunters were the House’s oldest anti-Seraph formations, deployed only when infection, host-recovery, or extermination could no longer be handled by ordinary marines. They wore massive Proctor-class hunter armour: layered bronze-black plate covered in wards, null projectors, reliquary seals and cognition systems. Each suit was a walking shrine of war, built to hunt hosts, break Husk Guard, and burn corrupted flesh from steel corridors without surrendering the dead or the living to Seraph capture.

Lars kept speaking.

“Deploy three Hunter Lances immediately.”

Kira identified the units as they came online.

Lance Duskbringer — anti-boarding and host termination.

Lance Exterminatus Seal — ship-clearing and vault recovery.

Lance Purifier — Seraph laboratory purge and contaminated infrastructure breach.

Lucy overlaid the destination arrows.

“Duskbringer to Bastion.”

“Exterminatus Seal to Central.”

“Purifier to Gatehold reserve.”

Joel understood at once.

Central needed protection for the elders, the archives, and the Shepherd vaults.

Bastion needed something that could cut through Husk Guard and experimental shock troops.

Gatehold might yet hide its own black levels.

Lars looked toward Joel.

“Your ordinary marines hold the station. The Hunters secure the nightmare.”

Joel nodded once.

“Understood.”

Then Lars looked toward Anton and Christer.

“You two will receive Duskbringer.”

Anton smiled again, but there was nothing cheerful in it.

“Good.”

Christer asked the real question.

“How many?”

Lars answered.

“Twelve suits.”

Mara stared.

“Only twelve?”

Annette replied before Lars could.

“Twelve is enough.”

The House did not use Seraph Hunters like line infantry. They were for breach points, sealed vaults, host nests, corrupted command decks and places normal troops should never have to enter. The reference image of their armour lived in old House archives for a reason: giant warded shields, great swords edged for anti-symbiote work, incinerator cannon systems, reinforced thrusters, null filters, redundant optics, sanctified munition feeds. They were not elegant.

They were final.

Lars opened another fleet layer.

“Additional order. Any Ardent force confirmed to be carrying Seraph personnel, Husk Guard, or conversion stock is now subject to immediate disabling fire and enforced boarding.”

Annette added:

“Preserve witnesses where possible. Preserve human subjects whenever possible. Burn the Seraph infrastructure.”

Daniel spoke from Gatehold.

“And if the infrastructure is fused into the station?”

Annette touched the rose.

“Then cut carefully.”

Anton laughed once.

“Head Justicar mercy.”

“Do not test it.”

The battle map updated again. New House signatures emerged from deep transit behind Bastion and Central: fast transport carriers bearing the Hunter Lances. They were not large, but every bridge watching them understood what their arrival meant.

Bastion was no longer a political crisis.

It was now a sanctioned purge.

Lars gave the final order.

“All fronts advance.”

“Joel, Central remains the anchor.”

“Daniel, the road does not close.”

“Christer, break the second capital.”

“Anton, take the first.”

“And when the Hunters arrive…”

His voice went cold.

“…show the Seraph what House von Hjertstedt forged in secret for wars like this.”

Then the Primarch cut the channel.

Across all three fronts, House crews watched the Hunter transports burn toward battle.

And on Bastion, the first Husk Guard boarding craft began turning toward Anton’s captured capital.

---

## 12. Everyone Wants a Piece

The declaration reached Sol before the battle reports did.

HOUSE VON HJERTSTEDT — EXCISION CAMPAIGN ACTIVE.

The emergency chamber on Luna went silent.

Primarch Lars von Hjertstedt stood before the central display while Ardent territory burned red across it.

Three stations.

Several settled worlds.

A hidden warfleet.

Confirmed Seraph military presence.

Project Shepherd.

House blood experimentation.

The Throne Imperium representative was first to speak.

“You have invoked territorial authority.”

Lars looked at him.

“Temporary Excision authority.”

“Across an entire state.”

“An entire state was feeding prisoners to the Seraph Throne.”

No one challenged that.

The Seraph were not a theoretical enemy.

Human history contained too many dead systems for that.

The Terran delegation exchanged quiet looks.

The Hiigaran representatives were already studying Gatehold.

Of course they were.

Lars noticed.

“You are all thinking about what happens after.”

The Terran chairwoman answered carefully.

“We are thinking about containment.”

Lars smiled faintly.

“So am I.”

The Hiigaran admiral enlarged the gate map.

“Gatehold cannot remain uncontrolled after this campaign.”

“Agreed,” Lars said.

“It should therefore be transferred to an established gate authority.”

There it was.

The first claim.

Lars looked at him.

“You mean yours.”

“The Restoration has more experience securing ancient gate infrastructure.”

“Convenient.”

The Imperial representative entered next.

“Ardent Central is a judicial nexus. Once cleansed, lawful administration should fall under recognized imperial supervision.”

The Terran chairwoman almost laughed.

“So Gatehold goes to the Hiigarans and Central goes to the Imperium?”

The Imperial representative remained calm.

“That would be preferable to permanent mercenary occupation.”

Lars’s crimson eyes slowly brightened.

The room noticed.

He remained polite.

“House von Hjertstedt has not requested territory.”

“Yet.”

“No.”

Lars pointed toward Ardent Bastion.

“My children are currently fighting Seraph-controlled warships while this chamber is dividing territory nobody here has taken.”

Silence.

That helped.

For approximately ten seconds.

Then Terra moved.

The Terran chairwoman highlighted Ardent’s industrial worlds.

“Civilian reconstruction will require food, medicine, engineers and administrative support.”

Lars looked toward her.

“And Terra can provide all of that.”

“Yes.”

“For access.”

“Preferential contracts.”

Lars laughed.

Not loudly.

That made everyone more uncomfortable.

“There it is.”

The chairwoman frowned.

“What?”

“The vultures finally landed.”

At Ardent strategic command, Annette received the same diplomatic traffic.

Her peach hair was tied back now.

The pink rose remained clearly visible.

Kira analyzed military movement.

Lucy analyzed diplomacy.

The results were almost identical.

Throne Imperium expeditionary group approaching Ardent outer corridor.

Hiigaran relay-security force moving toward secondary gate access.

Terran humanitarian convoy assembling under armed escort.

Annette stared at the tactical display.

“Humanitarian convoy.”

Lucy answered:

“It contains three reconstruction ships, two hospital vessels, four escorts, and twelve commercial representatives.”

Anton’s voice came from Bastion.

“Commercial representatives?”

“Yes.”

“During an Excision War?”

Lucy replied:

“Opportunity appears highly motivating.”

Christer laughed over the channel.

Annette did not.

“Joel.”

His image appeared from Central.

“Listening.”

“The major powers are moving.”

“I expected that.”

“The Imperium wants Central.”

Joel smiled.

“Of course.”

“The Hiigarans want Gatehold.”

“Obviously.”

“Terra wants reconstruction access.”

Anton interrupted.

“I want a drink.”

Annette ignored him.

Daniel joined from Gatehold.

“They can all want things.”

Annette looked toward him.

“That is exactly the correct attitude.”

She enlarged the three-front map.

“None of them enters Ardent military space without coordination.”

Joel nodded.

“Agreed.”

“None of them lands troops.”

“Agreed.”

“Humanitarian vessels may approach designated Sanctuary corridors after contamination screening.”

Joel smiled.

“You already wrote the rules.”

Annette touched the pink rose.

“I am a Justicar.”

At Bastion, the first Seraph Hunter transport crossed Anton’s fleet line.

Behind it, Ardent capital ships still exchanged heavy fire with Christer’s formation.

Nobody there cared what diplomats wanted.

Not yet.

But the political battle had already begun.

The Imperium transmitted an offer:

IMPERIAL SUPPORT AVAILABLE FOR STABILIZATION.

The Hiigarans followed:

RESTORATION GATE COMMAND READY TO ASSIST.

Terra:

FEDERAL HUMANITARIAN CORRIDOR REQUESTED.

Joel read all three.

Then sent one reply to everyone.

ASSISTANCE WELCOME UNDER HOUSE EXCISION COMMAND.

NO TERRITORIAL CLAIMS WILL BE RECOGNIZED UNTIL SERAPH CONTAMINATION IS REMOVED AND CIVILIAN REPRESENTATION IS RESTORED.

Anton read it.

Then laughed.

“You told three major powers to wait outside.”

“Yes.”

Christer added:

“They will hate that.”

“Yes.”

Daniel smiled.

“Good.”

Annette’s expression remained colder.

“They will obey while the Seraph threat is visible.”

Joel understood the rest.

“And after?”

Kira answered:

“After victory, probability of political competition rises sharply.”

Lucy added:

“Estimated probability that at least one faction attempts to convert assistance into permanent influence: ninety-one percent.”

Anton whistled.

“Optimistic.”

Joel looked across Ardent.

Central.

Bastion.

Gatehold.

House forces were paying for every kilometre with ammunition, armour and lives.

The other great powers were already planning the settlement.

Annette looked directly at Joel.

“Then we make sure Ardent survives enough to choose for itself.”

Joel nodded.

That was the only answer consistent with the Charter.

And perhaps the most difficult one.

Outside Bastion, Seraph ships moved closer.

Around Ardent, human fleets gathered.

The House was now fighting two wars.

One with guns.

One with promises.

---

## 13. The Hunters of Bastion

The Seraph Hunter transport hit Bastion’s battlespace like a falling blade.

It was not elegant.

It was compact, armoured, and ugly in the way only purpose-built warcraft could be. Heavy warding seals covered the hull. Null exhaust bled pale light behind it. Two escort cutters stayed close while Bastion’s guns and Ardent missiles tried to kill it before it reached the fight.

They failed.

Anton watched from the bridge of his assault flagship as the transport burned through crossfire toward the half-secured Ardent capital he was boarding.

“Duskbringer arriving,” his tactical officer said.

Anton smiled.

“Finally.”

Christer’s voice came through the shared command net, strained by the ongoing slugfest with the second capital.

“Do not let them waste momentum. Bastion is launching more assault craft.”

Anton checked the plot.

He saw them.

Ardent troop carriers were pouring from the station’s lower military collars, surrounded by smaller black-gold craft that no longer pretended to be anything except Seraph.

And inside those craft were the things House marines had already seen on the observation feed.

Husk Guard.

Experimental soldiers.

Walking surgery.

The first Hunter transport cut across the void and locked onto Anton’s captured capital just aft of the launch section. Magnetic clamps bit. Breach charges fired. The hull opened.

Then the Seraph Hunters came out.

Twelve suits.

Each one massive.

Bronze-black Proctor-class armour layered in plates thick enough to turn corridor walls into obstacles rather than cover. Great shields carried wards, skull-reliefs and anti-symbiote seals. Long swords burned with red-lit edge script. Some hunters carried incinerator cannons slung beneath one arm, built to purge sealed compartments without letting contamination spread. Their helmets showed no faces. Only dark optics and machine-red eyes.

The marines on Anton’s deck went silent.

One of the Grey Nomad veterans finally whispered, “That is what they look like.”

Anton keyed the internal command line.

“Hunter Lance Duskbringer.”

A deeper voice answered from inside the lead suit.

“Lance-Captain Helle Voss.”

“Anton von Hjertstedt.”

“We know.”

Of course they did.

Anton almost laughed.

“Enemy Husk Guard and experimental combat stock are entering this hull and launching from Bastion. We need the lower military decks locked down.”

“Understood.”

No flourish.

No speech.

The Hunters moved.

The first clash happened three decks below the launch spine.

Anton and two marine squads were pushing toward the bridge when a bulkhead alarm screamed. The side corridor blew open. Smoke poured through. Then a Husk Guard came through the gap.

It had once been human.

Now it was a sealed war-shell with a fused torso, reinforced armature legs and one oversized gauntlet ending in a cutting claw. Red fluid lines pulsed beneath its armour seams. Behind it came three more, followed by human-shaped experimental soldiers wearing support braces bolted into their spines and shoulders.

One still had its original face.

Or part of it.

Its mouth was stitched at one side. One eye was gone. It held a rifle perfectly steady.

The marine beside Anton swore.

Then the Hunters met them.

Lance-Captain Voss stepped in front of the first Husk Guard and took its cutting strike on her shield. Sparks exploded across engraved wards. Another Hunter drove an incinerator burst into the second warform, flooding the corridor with white-orange flame. A third brought a great sword down through an experimental soldier’s weapon and chest rig in one stroke.

No dramatic motion.

No wasted movement.

Just total, brutal certainty.

Anton watched for half a second.

Then grinned.

“Good. Now kill the rest.”

His marines surged past the Hunters’ flanks and hit the human troops behind the warforms. One shouted, “PRISONERS STILL COUNT!” Another yelled back, “THEN AIM BETTER!”

The corridor became a slaughterhouse.

The Husk Guard fought with terrifying strength, but the Hunters had been built for exactly this. Their shields caught claws and heavy rounds. Their blades bit through reinforced host armour. Their incinerators burned symbiote tissue without setting the whole section alight. When one experimental soldier tried to grapple a Hunter from behind, the suit’s null projector flared and the thing dropped, screaming as black filaments writhed out of its neck seal.

Anton fired twice into another soldier’s legs and shoulder.

“Alive if possible,” he ordered.

A marine looked at the twitching thing on the deck.

“Possible is a very flexible word, sir.”

“Use a less flexible interpretation.”

Outside, Bastion still burned.

Christer’s capital duel had become savage.

His ship and the second Ardent capital were nearly broadside to one another now, each trying to cripple the other before boarding became necessary. Heavy shells crossed the void in visible streaks. A House cruiser died under concentrated Bastion fire. In return, one of Ardent’s escort destroyers split open from stem to stern under a mass-driver hit.

Then the second Seraph craft entered his battlespace.

Not a boarding shuttle.

A warship.

Small by fleet standards, but unmistakably Seraph in form and heat signature.

Christer saw it at the same moment Annette did.

“More coming,” he said.

Annette’s voice answered over command. “Hold your line. The Hunters are buying us time.”

Back aboard Anton’s target capital, Lance-Captain Voss reported as her Hunters cleared the lower decks.

“Two Husk Guard teams neutralized.”

“Seven experimental combatants dead.”

“Three secured alive.”

“Seraph biomatter present in engineering access.”

Anton stopped.

“Engineering?”

“Yes.”

That was wrong.

Or worse than wrong.

Why would Seraph combat stock already be staged inside an Ardent capital unless the ship had become more than a warship?

He looked toward the deck map.

There were sealed compartments below engineering that did not match standard military layout.

Medical.

Containment.

Storage.

He smiled without humour.

“Of course.”

The marines around him noticed.

That smile meant he had found another problem.

Lance-Captain Voss looked at him through her dark visor.

“You have identified a new objective.”

“Yes.”

“State it.”

Anton keyed the bridge-route team to continue upward without him and turned toward the lower-seal passage.

“There is a laboratory inside this ship.”

The Hunters did not react.

They simply began moving that way.

---

## 14. The Duel Ends

Christer’s capital ship shuddered under another direct hit.

The bridge lights failed.

For three seconds, only emergency red remained.

Then tactical screens returned one by one.

“Forward armour down to thirty-eight percent!”

“Starboard batteries still firing!”

“Two destroyers lost from screen!”

“Enemy capital maintaining pressure!”

Christer stood behind the command rail, one hand locked around it as the deck rolled beneath him.

Ahead, the second Ardent capital ship filled the main display.

It was wounded.

So were they.

Both ships had crossed beyond the point where a careful commander would normally disengage.

Neither captain intended to be careful.

“Enemy spinal gun charging,” tactical warned.

Christer looked at the range.

Close Hammer.

Too close.

“Turn twenty degrees.”

The helmsman looked back.

“That exposes our damaged port side.”

“I know.”

The ship began turning.

One officer stared at Christer.

“Sir, if that spinal shot hits—”

“It will not.”

The Ardent gun fired.

White light crossed the void.

Christer’s ship continued turning.

The shot missed the command spine by less than a kilometre and tore through the wreck of a drifting frigate behind them.

The bridge crew went silent.

Christer pointed toward the enemy.

“Now.”

His own capital ship fired everything that still worked.

Spinal gun.

Heavy batteries.

Missile racks.

Cruisers around him joined the volley.

The Ardent capital disappeared inside impacts.

Its armour split across the forward quarter.

One engine died.

Then another.

But the ship remained alive.

Still turning.

Still fighting.

Christer smiled.

“Hard bastard.”

Then Kira’s voice entered the command net.

“New Seraph contacts.”

Christer’s expression changed.

The tactical display expanded.

Three black-gold signatures were moving from behind Bastion.

Then five.

Small cruisers.

Boarding carriers.

One larger hull still hidden behind sensor interference.

Lucy followed.

“Enemy Seraph force is committing toward the Ardent line.”

Christer frowned.

“Toward us?”

Annette answered.

“Toward everyone.”

The first Seraph cruiser opened fire.

Not at House ships.

At the crippled Ardent capital.

Christer stared.

A lance struck the Ardent vessel near its command section.

Another hit followed.

“They are destroying their own allies,” his tactical officer said.

Annette understood first.

“No.”

Her pink rose caught the light on the strategic feed.

“They are destroying evidence.”

Christer looked toward the damaged capital.

The Ardent ship had fought beside Seraph forces.

Its crew knew things.

Its computers knew more.

And now the Seraphs wanted it dead before the House could take it.

“Boarding status?” Christer demanded.

“None launched yet.”

“Then launch now.”

The assault officer looked toward him.

“Sir, point defence is still active.”

“Not for long.”

Christer opened fleet command.

“All available guns. Suppress enemy point defence.”

House destroyers accelerated.

Cruisers shifted fire.

The damaged Ardent capital found itself caught between two enemies.

House weapons stripped turrets and tracking arrays.

Seraph lances tried to kill the entire ship.

Then the Ardent captain opened a channel.

His face appeared through smoke.

“House vessel.”

Christer looked at him.

“You finally want to talk.”

The man coughed.

“Seraph ships are firing on us.”

“Yes.”

“They were supposed to reinforce Bastion.”

Christer leaned closer.

“And now?”

The captain looked toward his own bridge.

Several crew were wounded.

One console burned behind him.

“Now I think they intend to leave no witnesses.”

Christer’s expression hardened.

“Cut engines.”

The captain hesitated.

“Lower weapons.”

Another hesitation.

Christer raised his voice.

“You have two choices. Surrender to me or die proving the Seraphs never cared about you.”

A Seraph lance struck the capital’s aft section.

The bridge feed shook violently.

Then the Ardent captain looked back.

“Weapons cold.”

Across the tactical display, the great ship’s batteries stopped firing.

Its remaining drive bloom faded.

Christer opened the House-wide channel.

“White Warrant. Enemy capital surrendering.”

Annette responded immediately.

“Accepted.”

“Protect the surrendered vessel.”

That order changed the battle.

House destroyers moved around the crippled Ardent capital.

Not to finish it.

To shield it.

Vigil frigates filled the space between it and the incoming Seraph cruisers.

Point defence turned outward.

Christer watched Seraph missiles begin crossing the dark.

“Now they are shooting at us for saving their allies.”

Annette’s voice went cold.

“That tells us exactly what those allies know.”

A House assault cutter launched toward the surrendered capital.

Then another.

Then ten more.

Christer checked his armour.

His bridge officer looked toward him.

“You are going personally.”

“Yes.”

“You just fought a capital duel.”

“Yes.”

“The ship needs you here.”

Christer looked toward the surrendered Ardent vessel.

“That captain just chose surrender over Seraph execution.”

He pointed toward the Seraph cruisers closing behind it.

“If they reach him first, we lose the crew and the evidence.”

The officer sighed.

“I knew that was going to be your answer.”

Christer smiled.

“Good.”

He turned toward tactical.

“You have command until I transfer.”

Then Annette cut in.

“Christer.”

He stopped.

“Yes?”

“The larger Seraph contact just crossed confirmation range.”

The tactical screen changed.

The hidden silhouette appeared.

A heavy Seraph carrier.

Its lower hull opened.

Hundreds of small launch signatures began appearing.

Boarding craft.

Coffin barges.

Husk Guard transports.

Christer stared.

Annette continued.

“Bastion is becoming a full Seraph engagement.”

Christer’s crimson eyes brightened.

“Then we finish this quickly.”

He left the bridge at a run.

Behind him, House capital ships turned their guns away from the surrendered Ardent vessel and toward the approaching Seraph force.

The duel was over.

The real battle had just arrived.

---

## 15. Central Becomes House Ground

Ardent Central was quiet.

Not peaceful.

Quiet.

There was a difference.

The last organized Ardent resistance had surrendered six hours earlier. Special Custody remained sealed under Justicar authority. Shepherd’s archive cores were already duplicated and moved into armored storage aboard two Sanctuary tenders. The surviving elders were under medical guard. Civilian districts had been searched, cleared, and reopened one by one.

No more boarding alarms.

No more hostile launches.

No more hidden security teams trying to burn records.

For the first time since Project Shepherd had been uncovered, Joel could look at the tactical map of Central and see green.

All of it.

Captain Mara Vale stood beside him on the command deck of his capital ship.

“Station command confirms full control.”

Joel nodded.

“Civilian sectors?”

“Stable.”

“Life support?”

“Stable.”

“Medical?”

“Overloaded, but functioning.”

“Seraph contamination?”

Mara looked toward the Justicar report.

“No active biological signatures outside sealed evidence zones.”

Joel exhaled slowly.

Central was secure.

That meant he could finally move.

The tactical display widened.

Bastion was burning.

Gatehold was under pressure.

The Seraph carrier at Bastion had turned that front into something far larger than originally planned.

Daniel’s Gatehold force was still fighting to prevent route denial.

Joel looked at his fleet.

Central Banner had taken losses, but not enough to pin it in place.

Two cruisers damaged.

Three destroyers out of line.

Several Vigil frigates carrying armor scars and heat damage.

But the force remained combat capable.

Behind them, Ardent Central itself could now act as an anchor.

That changed the campaign.

“Split the fleet,” Joel ordered.

His operations officer looked up.

“High Marshal?”

“Central no longer needs the full Banner.”

Joel began marking groups.

“Leave one Oath cruiser.”

“Four Vigils.”

“Two destroyers.”

“Sanctuary support.”

“Enough marines to hold internal security.”

Mara studied the remaining force.

“And the rest?”

Joel pointed toward Bastion.

“Bastion gets the heavy group.”

Then Gatehold.

“Gatehold gets fast reinforcement.”

His tactical officer nodded.

“Two detachments.”

“Yes.”

Annette entered the command channel.

Her face appeared beside the battle map.

Peach hair.

Pink rose clearly visible.

Kira and Lucy active.

“You are moving already.”

“Yes.”

“Central status?”

“Secure.”

Annette checked the same reports through Kira.

“Confirmed. Station-wide hostile resistance below operational threshold.”

Lucy added:

“Civilian confidence indicators improving.”

Joel smiled faintly.

“Then we stop treating Central like disputed ground.”

Annette looked at him.

That sentence had weight.

Mara noticed too.

“What does that mean?”

Joel stared at Ardent Central.

The station had hidden Shepherd beneath its courts.

It had trafficked prisoners.

It had preserved House elders as laboratory stock.

It had invited Seraph access into human space.

And now its old government was gone.

There was no intact authority he trusted enough to hand it back to.

Not yet.

Joel opened the station-wide broadcast.

Every deck received it.

Courts.

Docks.

Habitation.

Engineering.

Medical.

Security.

House troops came to attention as his voice passed through the station.

“This is High Marshal Joel von Hjertstedt.”

The remaining Ardent civilians stopped to listen.

“Ardent Central is secure.”

A pause.

“Project Shepherd has been seized.”

“Special Custody is under Justicar control.”

“Seraph facilities are being dismantled.”

“Civilian life-support, food, medicine, housing, and local law will continue.”

Then Joel’s tone changed.

Stronger.

Final.

“The government that permitted Shepherd no longer holds authority here.”

Mara looked toward him.

Annette did not interrupt.

Joel continued.

“Under Excision Campaign authority, Ardent Central is hereby placed under direct protection and military governance of House von Hjertstedt.”

The command deck became very still.

“Until contamination is eliminated.”

“Until the surviving population is secure.”

“And until a lawful future government can be built without Seraph influence.”

He paused again.

Then removed the uncertainty completely.

“From this moment, Ardent Central belongs to House von Hjertstedt.”

Not conquered for profit.

Not claimed because it was rich.

Claimed because the House could not safely leave it behind.

Across the station, House heraldry replaced the dead Special Custody authority seals.

Black.

Crimson.

The crown and blade.

Joel continued:

“Anyone who lives here and obeys civilian law remains protected.”

“Anyone who surrenders receives due process.”

“Anyone who attempts to restore Shepherd…”

His crimson eyes brightened.

“…will discover why this station changed hands.”

The transmission ended.

Mara looked at him.

“You just annexed a capital station.”

Joel nodded.

“Yes.”

“You people really do make exceptions for the Seraph.”

“Yes.”

Annette finally spoke.

“The claim is lawful under Excision authority.”

Her fingers touched the pink rose.

“And witnessed.”

That mattered.

The House was not pretending the seizure had not happened.

It was recording it.

Owning it.

Accepting responsibility for everything that came after.

Joel looked toward his officers.

“Raise House colors.”

Outside, lights changed along the command spine of Ardent Central.

The old Ardent emblem dimmed.

A massive House insignia came alive across the primary station tower.

Black field.

Crimson crest.

The mark could be seen from orbit.

There would be no confusion now.

Joel returned his attention to the war.

“Bastion reinforcement.”

His operations officer highlighted the heavy detachment.

“One capital ship, two Oath cruisers, three destroyers, six Vigil frigates.”

“Add an assault carrier.”

Mara raised an eyebrow.

“That is not a reinforcement.”

“It is from Central.”

“For most people, that is a fleet.”

Joel ignored her.

“Send it to Christer.”

Annette nodded.

“Agreed.”

“And Gatehold?”

Joel marked the second group.

“Fast force only.”

“Vigils, destroyers, one cruiser.”

Annette looked toward Daniel’s front.

“Kira agrees. Gatehold needs speed more than mass.”

Kira confirmed:

“Probability of gate-core destruction increases with delay.”

Joel’s jaw tightened.

“Send them.”

The orders went out.

House ships began breaking from Central formation.

Heavy hulls turned toward Bastion.

Fast escorts accelerated toward Gatehold.

Behind them, Ardent Central remained under the crown and blade.

A conquered station.

A protected population.

A new House responsibility.

And the first permanent territorial claim of the Excision War.

Annette watched the movement.

“Joel.”

“Yes?”

“You understand what this does politically.”

“Yes.”

“The Imperium will object.”

“Yes.”

“The Hiigarans will object.”

“Yes.”

“Terra will negotiate.”

Joel smiled.

“Of course.”

Mara looked between them.

“And you are still doing it.”

Joel looked toward the newly raised House colors over Ardent Central.

“Yes.”

Then the Bastion feed flashed.

Seraph launch signatures multiplied again.

Gatehold transmitted a new warning.

Both fronts needed help.

Joel pointed toward the moving detachments.

“Central is ours.”

His voice hardened.

“Now reinforce the others.”

---

# Chapter 4: The Gate Must Hold

## 1. The Gate Must Hold

Gatehold was dying by procedure.

That was what made it dangerous.

There was no wild panic on Daniel’s tactical display.

No uncontrolled evacuation.

No random reactor failures.

Ardent Gatehold was being dismantled in sequence.

Authentication archives were being erased.

Relay keys were being pulled from live servers.

Navigation libraries were being burned.

One gate-control chamber after another was going dark.

Daniel stood on the bridge of his command cruiser and watched red sectors spread across the station schematic.

“How long until they reach the primary authentication core?”

His operations officer answered:

“Twenty-three minutes.”

Daniel’s eyes narrowed.

“Too fast.”

Another officer looked up.

“High Marshal’s reinforcement group is inbound.”

“Time?”

“Thirty-one minutes.”

Daniel stared at the numbers.

Eight minutes too late.

That was enough to lose the entire route.

Gatehold controlled more than traffic.

It held approach credentials, relay handshakes, emergency lane closures, and the route records needed to move safely through the sector. In this universe, losing a gate library could isolate worlds almost as effectively as destroying the gate itself.

Daniel opened the family channel.

“Joel.”

His brother answered from Central.

“Listening.”

“They are ahead of schedule.”

“How far?”

“Primary core in twenty-three.”

“Reinforcements thirty-one.”

Joel went silent.

Annette joined immediately.

Kira was already calculating.

“Eight-minute deficit confirmed.”

Lucy added:

“Probability Gatehold successfully destroys core before reinforcement arrival: seventy-eight percent.”

Daniel smiled without humour.

“Unacceptable.”

Annette looked toward him.

“You have what locally?”

“One cruiser group, four Vigils, three destroyers, and boarding capability.”

Joel answered before she could.

“Enough.”

Daniel looked at him.

“For what?”

“For eight minutes.”

Anton came over the channel from Bastion.

“That sounds like Daniel’s kind of problem.”

Christer added:

“Usually because Daniel creates eight-minute problems.”

Daniel ignored both.

Joel continued:

“Go.”

Annette raised one finger.

“Controlled entry.”

Daniel’s smile returned.

“Of course.”

Anton laughed.

Nobody believed him.

Gatehold filled the main screen.

A fortress wrapped around a Varanthi gate structure.

Defence rings.

Customs docks.

Military platforms.

Relay towers.

And beneath everything, the ancient gate itself.

Daniel pointed toward the inner command spine.

“That is the target.”

His captain frowned.

“Direct approach crosses two defence belts.”

“Yes.”

“Missile platforms are still active.”

“Yes.”

“Station fleet?”

Daniel looked toward the tactical display.

Ardent escorts were forming close to the gate.

“They want us outside.”

“Exactly.”

Daniel opened fleet command.

“All Vigils forward.”

His tactical officer turned sharply.

“Sir?”

“Make noise.”

Then:

“Destroyers behind them.”

“Cruiser stays dark until their guns commit.”

The formation moved.

Vigil frigates accelerated toward Gatehold.

Ardent platforms reacted immediately.

Targeting beams appeared.

Missile doors opened.

Daniel watched.

“Wait.”

The first Ardent missiles launched.

“Now.”

House Vigils broke formation in every direction.

Decoys flooded space.

Point defence flashed.

The Ardent platforms began tracking the fast targets.

Daniel’s cruiser changed vector.

Straight toward the gap.

His captain looked at him.

“You are taking the command ship through?”

“Yes.”

“That is normally considered poor flagship doctrine.”

Daniel smiled.

“I am not using it as a flagship.”

Another officer understood first.

“You are using it as a boarding transport.”

Daniel nodded.

“Much better.”

Hammer Range collapsed quickly.

Ardent destroyers fired on the Vigils.

House destroyers returned with drive-kill shots.

One Ardent escort lost engines.

Another spun away after losing fire control.

Gatehold’s platforms kept firing.

Then Daniel’s cruiser entered the gap.

“Missile warning!”

“Point defence full!”

The hull shook.

One missile struck the outer armour.

Another detonated against the forward screen.

Daniel remained standing.

“Dock target?”

“Military Collar Twelve.”

“Occupied?”

“Unknown.”

“Good.”

The cruiser burned harder.

Gatehold grew across the screen.

The station’s own guns were now firing almost point-blank.

Knife Range.

Exactly where Daniel wanted to be.

A defence platform tried to rotate toward him.

One House destroyer fired across its weapon mount.

The turret vanished.

Daniel opened the assault channel.

“All boarding teams ready.”

A familiar voice answered.

One of the former Grey Nomad maniacs.

“READY!”

Another shouted:

“CORE SAVE COUNTS?”

Daniel closed his eyes.

“Yes.”

The channel erupted.

“INTELLIGENCE CATEGORY!”

“GATE CORE IS WORTH DOUBLE!”

“WHO DECIDED?”

“ME!”

Daniel cut across them.

“Listen.”

Silence.

Mostly.

“Primary objective is authentication preservation.”

“No explosives near the core.”

“No shooting control technicians unless they resist.”

“Capture engineers alive.”

A marine asked:

“Prize?”

Daniel looked toward the countdown.

Nineteen minutes.

“If you save the core intact, I will personally argue for something from the Vintergard reserve.”

The channel exploded.

Daniel sighed.

He had become part of the problem.

The cruiser struck Collar Twelve.

Magnetic clamps locked.

The docking tube did not extend.

Ardent had disabled it.

Daniel smiled.

“Breaching teams.”

The first charges blew.

Marines flooded across.

Daniel went with them.

His officers protested.

He ignored them.

The corridor beyond was already filled with smoke.

Ardent security fired from barricades.

House marines hit them hard.

Fast.

One team pinned the defenders.

Another went through maintenance.

Daniel moved down the centre with Black Guard specialists.

“Core level?”

“Six decks below.”

“Lift?”

“Disabled.”

“Stairs.”

The marine beside him laughed.

“Traditional.”

They descended.

Above them, Gatehold continued deleting itself.

SECONDARY AUTHENTICATION VAULT — DESTROYED.

MILITARY ROUTE ARCHIVE — PURGE ACTIVE.

Daniel heard every report through his helmet.

“Time?”

“Twelve minutes.”

Too close.

Then Kira entered his feed.

“High Marshal reinforcement group has increased burn.”

Lucy followed:

“Updated arrival: seven minutes.”

Daniel smiled.

Joel had pushed them hard.

Good.

Then another warning appeared.

PRIMARY AUTHENTICATION CORE — MANUAL DESTRUCTION AUTHORIZED.

Daniel stopped.

“By whom?”

His Black Guard officer checked.

“Gatehold Commandant.”

“Location?”

The map flashed.

Same level.

Two corridors away.

Daniel’s eyes turned crimson.

“Change objective.”

The marines around him looked over.

“Commandant first.”

A Grey Nomad veteran shouted:

“CAPTURE CATEGORY!”

Daniel pointed at him.

“Alive.”

“Even better!”

They ran.

At Central, Joel watched the reinforcement group close on Gatehold.

Five minutes.

Then four.

His operations officer looked toward him.

“They are overheating engines.”

Joel nodded.

“Let them.”

“Repair bill will be severe.”

Joel looked toward the gate marker.

“A repair bill is cheaper than losing the road.”

Annette appeared beside him on tactical feed.

“That sounded almost romantic.”

“It is logistics.”

She smiled.

“Exactly.”

Then Daniel’s signal returned.

Gunfire.

Heavy breathing.

Shouting.

“We have reached core level.”

Joel leaned toward the display.

“Status?”

Daniel answered:

“Commandant is trying to destroy it manually.”

“How long?”

A pause.

Then Anton’s voice from Bastion:

“Daniel?”

“Yes?”

“Do not lose the gate.”

Daniel laughed.

“Thank you, Anton.”

Then gunfire erupted again.

The feed cut.

Joel stared at the countdown.

Two minutes until reinforcement arrival.

Gatehold’s core status flashed.

DESTRUCTION SEQUENCE ACTIVE.

Then:

MANUAL ACCESS CONTESTED.

Joel’s reinforcement group emerged into Gatehold’s outer battlespace at maximum burn.

Vigils first.

Destroyers behind.

One Oath cruiser at the centre.

The fast force opened fire immediately—not on the station.

On every ship trying to stop Daniel.

The road was still alive.

Barely.

And somewhere six decks beneath Gatehold’s armour, Daniel was racing the final seconds.

---

## 2. The Gatehold Breakthrough

Joel’s reinforcement group entered Gatehold’s battlespace at full burn.

The lead Vigil frigates did not slow.

They cut straight through the outer Ardent screen while destroyers followed behind them, firing into exposed drive sections and weapons clusters. The Oath cruiser at the center of the formation came in last, heavy guns already tracking.

Gatehold’s defenders had been focused inward.

On Daniel.

Now they had a second problem.

A very large one.

“Enemy formation turning!” Joel’s tactical officer reported from Central.

Joel watched the live feed.

“Good.”

One Ardent cruiser broke away from Gatehold and tried to intercept the incoming Oath cruiser.

Joel pointed at it.

“Disable.”

The Oath cruiser fired.

Its spinal shot struck the Ardent ship beneath the engines.

The cruiser’s drive flare vanished.

It began tumbling.

Joel gave the next order.

“Open Daniel’s corridor.”

House destroyers surged forward.

Missiles crossed at Knife Range.

A Vigil frigate took a direct hit and lost half its dorsal armour, but stayed between the enemy and the Oath cruiser.

Then the reinforcement force punched through.

Deep inside Gatehold, Daniel heard the distant impacts through the station structure.

House guns.

Reinforcements.

He smiled.

“Joel made it.”

The marine beside him shouted:

“THEN MOVE!”

They were less than three hundred metres from the primary authentication chamber.

Then the walls opened.

Not doors.

Walls.

Hidden armour panels slid apart along both sides of the corridor.

Daniel stopped.

For one second, nothing moved.

Then the first Husk Guard stepped out.

It was huge.

A human body had been rebuilt into something almost twice its original mass. Reinforced legs bent backward beneath an armoured torso. One arm ended in a cutting claw. The other carried a weapon fused into the forearm.

Behind it came another.

Then six more.

And behind them—

experimental soldiers.

Thin human shapes held upright by spinal frames.

Heads partially enclosed in surgical armour.

Tubes disappeared beneath skin.

One still wore an Ardent security uniform beneath the metal grafts.

A Grey Nomad veteran stared.

“Oh.”

Another answered:

“That is disgusting.”

Then the Husk Guard charged.

Daniel raised his weapon.

“CONTACT!”

The corridor exploded.

House marines fired into the first warform.

Rounds struck armour.

It kept coming.

The Husk Guard hit the front line like a vehicle.

One marine went into the wall.

Another lost his shield.

Daniel fired twice into the creature’s knee joint.

“LOW!”

The marines adjusted immediately.

Concentrated fire shattered the reinforced leg.

The creature fell.

It kept crawling.

A second Husk Guard came over it.

Then a third hidden compartment opened behind the House formation.

Daniel heard the warning too late.

“REAR CONTACT!”

Experimental soldiers poured into the corridor behind them.

Daniel swore.

They were trapped between two Seraph formations.

The Grey Nomad teams reacted exactly as expected.

Loudly.

“FRONT IS MINE!”

“THEN I HAVE REAR!”

“THAT IS NOT HOW COMMAND WORKS!”

Daniel shouted:

“IT IS NOW!”

The marines split.

Half turned forward.

Half turned back.

The corridor became a brutal two-direction fight.

Daniel checked the countdown.

PRIMARY CORE DESTRUCTION: FOUR MINUTES.

“Perfect.”

Joel watched Daniel’s telemetry collapse into overlapping hostile markers.

“What happened?”

Kira answered through Annette’s tactical link.

“Previously shielded compartments have opened throughout Gatehold core levels.”

Lucy identified dozens of new contacts.

“Husk Guard signatures confirmed.”

Annette’s face hardened.

“They were stored inside the station.”

Joel looked toward the Oath cruiser approaching Gatehold.

“Special force status?”

His operations officer checked.

“Onboard.”

Joel did not hesitate.

“Send them.”

Annette looked at him.

“The Hunters?”

“Yes.”

The Oath cruiser carried a small anti-Seraph detachment originally intended as Gatehold reserve.

They were no longer reserve.

Joel opened the cruiser’s command channel.

“Oath command.”

“High Marshal.”

“Prepare direct insertion.”

The cruiser captain hesitated.

“Into the station?”

“Into Daniel’s position.”

“We do not have a secure docking collar.”

Joel enlarged Gatehold’s schematic.

“Then do not use one.”

Silence.

Joel marked an emergency maintenance aperture less than a hundred metres from Daniel’s trapped formation.

“Breach there.”

The captain understood.

“That will put the insertion craft inside station defensive range.”

“Yes.”

“Very inside.”

Joel’s crimson eyes brightened.

“Daniel does not have four minutes.”

The response came immediately.

“Understood.”

The Oath cruiser rolled toward Gatehold.

Its heavy batteries fired continuously, suppressing nearby defensive platforms.

Then a small armored insertion craft launched from beneath its hull.

It carried eight Seraph Hunters and sixteen Black Guard specialists.

The craft accelerated directly toward the station.

Gatehold opened fire.

House Vigils crossed the firing lines.

One absorbed a missile meant for the insertion craft.

Another dumped decoys between it and the station.

The craft never slowed.

Inside, Hunter shields locked.

Weapons armed.

The pilot shouted:

“IMPACT IN TEN!”

Nobody answered.

“FIVE!”

The craft hit Gatehold.

Its reinforced nose punched through the maintenance aperture and buried itself halfway into the station.

The front ramp blew outward.

Smoke filled the passage.

The first Seraph Hunter stepped through.

Then the others.

Daniel heard the impact.

Every Husk Guard in the corridor seemed to pause.

A new transmission entered his helmet.

“Special force to Daniel.”

He laughed.

“About time.”

The Hunter commander answered:

“High Marshal sent us.”

Daniel fired into an experimental soldier advancing through smoke.

“I owe him a drink.”

Then the Hunters hit the Husk Guard from the flank.

Great shields slammed into Seraph warforms.

Heavy blades came down.

One Hunter drove an incinerator burst through an opened compartment and forced three experimental soldiers back into the chamber they had emerged from.

The pressure on Daniel’s formation broke instantly.

Not disappeared.

Broke.

That was enough.

Daniel pointed toward the core chamber.

“Grey Nomad teams with me!”

A marine shouted:

“HUNTERS GET THE MONSTERS?”

“Yes!”

“BEST PLAN ALL DAY!”

They ran.

Behind them, the special force held the corridor against the emerging Husk Guard.

Ahead, the authentication chamber door was closing.

Daniel saw the countdown.

ONE MINUTE FORTY-TWO.

He raised his weapon.

“Breach!”

The marines accelerated.

Above them, Joel’s reinforcement fleet had broken Gatehold’s outer defence line.

Outside, House ships now controlled the approach.

Inside, the Hunters were fighting things hidden inside the station walls.

And Daniel was less than two minutes from either saving the road—

or watching an entire sector lose it.

---

## 3. Dennis Has Arrived

Daniel hit the authentication chamber door at a run.

“Breach!”

The Grey Nomad veterans placed charges.

One marine looked at the countdown above the door.

00:54

“That is unpleasant.”

Daniel raised his weapon.

“Do it.”

The charges fired.

The door folded inward.

Daniel went through first.

Ardent security opened fire immediately.

Rounds struck his shield and armour. House marines poured through behind him, splitting left and right.

At the far end of the chamber stood Gatehold Commandant Oren Vale, one hand pressed against a manual control pedestal.

Behind him, the primary authentication core glowed inside layers of armored crystal and ancient machinery.

And above everything—

the gate status changed.

UNAUTHORIZED ACTIVATION DETECTED.

Daniel stopped.

“No.”

The commandant smiled.

The vast gate outside Gatehold began to wake.

Ancient structures around the aperture lit one after another.

Power surged through the station.

Kira’s voice entered Daniel’s helmet.

“Gate activation is not using standard Ardent authentication.”

Lucy followed.

“Handshake structure contains confirmed Seraph command architecture.”

Daniel stared at the commandant.

“What did you open?”

Vale answered quietly.

“A future.”

Daniel shot him in the leg.

The commandant collapsed screaming.

“Wrong answer.”

Outside Gatehold, space distorted.

Joel watched from Central command.

The gate aperture filled with pale light.

His operations officer shouted:

“Transit field forming!”

“Mass estimate?”

“Unknown. Rising.”

Annette appeared beside the tactical feed.

“Kira?”

“Transit object exceeds cruiser mass.”

Lucy:

“Estimate increasing.”

Joel’s expression hardened.

“Capital?”

“Possible.”

Then another transmission entered the command network.

Not Daniel.

Not Annette.

A deep voice.

Very calm.

“High Marshal.”

Joel froze for half a second.

Annette noticed.

“Who is that?”

Joel looked toward the incoming Hunter telemetry.

A name appeared.

DENNIS RASK.

Joel closed his eyes.

“Oh, no.”

Inside Gatehold, Daniel heard the same voice.

“Daniel.”

He stopped firing.

Slowly.

Very slowly.

He opened the special-force identification.

Then stared at it.

“No.”

A marine beside him glanced over.

“What?”

Daniel looked back toward the corridor where the Seraph Hunters were fighting.

“No, no, no.”

The voice came again.

“I am approaching your position.”

Daniel immediately opened the family channel.

“JOEL!”

Joel answered.

“Yes.”

“You sent Dennis?”

There was a pause.

“That was the available anti-Seraph commander.”

“You sent Dennis Rask onto a station containing an ancient gate?”

“Yes.”

Daniel stared at nothing.

“You absolute idiot.”

Anton immediately joined from Bastion.

“Dennis is there?”

Christer came next.

“You sent Dennis?”

Annette looked between all three feeds.

“I assume there is context.”

Daniel shouted while firing into an Ardent guard position.

“CONTEXT?”

A Grey Nomad veteran ducked beside him.

Daniel continued.

“Dennis does not clear Seraph infestations!”

Joel sighed.

Daniel pointed furiously at his helmet display as though Joel could see the gesture.

“He removes the geographical area containing the infestation!”

Dennis’s voice entered the same channel.

“That is an exaggeration.”

Daniel snapped:

“You destroyed half a refinery moon!”

“It was contaminated.”

“You blew up the refinery!”

“It was also contaminated.”

Anton began laughing.

Christer followed.

Daniel shouted:

“THIS IS NOT FUNNY!”

Dennis answered:

“The moon survived.”

Daniel nearly lost control.

“THAT IS YOUR DEFENCE?”

A massive figure appeared through the smoke behind Daniel’s formation.

Dennis Rask did not look like the other Hunters.

He was broader.

Older armor.

Blackened plates covered in repairs from campaigns Daniel had only heard about. His helmet carried no decoration except a row of old kill-marks and a crimson House seal personally granted decades earlier.

A huge anti-Seraph blade rested across one shoulder.

An incinerator cannon was attached to his other arm.

He walked through the remains of a Husk Guard without looking down.

Dennis had been one of Joel’s closest friends long before Joel became High Marshal.

He had survived campaigns that had killed entire commands.

He had personally dragged Joel out of at least two battles everyone else considered lost.

He was loyal.

Terrifyingly competent.

And completely unreasonable once Seraph contamination was confirmed.

Daniel pointed at him.

“You.”

Dennis stopped.

“Yes?”

“You are not allowed to destroy the station.”

Dennis looked around.

“I had not planned to.”

Daniel stared.

Dennis added:

“Yet.”

Daniel opened the family channel again.

“JOEL!”

Joel was openly laughing now.

“I heard him.”

“I hate you.”

“Save the core.”

“I am trying!”

The countdown hit:

00:19

Daniel turned.

Commandant Vale was crawling toward the destruction pedestal.

One Grey Nomad marine kicked him away.

“Not today.”

Daniel reached the console.

“Stop destruction!”

A technician shouted:

“Manual authority is locked!”

Dennis stepped beside him.

“Move.”

Daniel looked at the enormous anti-Seraph weapon.

“No.”

Dennis tilted his helmet.

“No?”

“You are not solving an authentication problem with explosives.”

“I was going to cut the power feed.”

“That is almost worse.”

Kira suddenly interrupted.

“Alternative available. Separate destruction command from gate activation.”

Daniel worked the controls.

“Do it.”

00:08

Lucy began injecting override sequences.

00:05

The gate aperture outside reached full illumination.

Something enormous became visible inside the transit distortion.

Long.

Black-gold.

Seraph.

00:03

Daniel slammed the final authorization.

PRIMARY CORE DESTRUCTION ABORTED.

He exhaled.

Then the entire station shook.

Not from an explosion.

From transit.

Outside Gatehold, the nose of a massive Seraph warship began emerging through the ancient gate.

Daniel slowly looked at Dennis.

Dennis looked at the tactical projection.

For the first time, Daniel heard amusement in his voice.

“Well.”

Daniel pointed a finger directly at him.

“No.”

“I have not said anything.”

“You are thinking about destroying the gate.”

Dennis considered.

“Yes.”

Daniel screamed into the family channel:

“JOEL, GET YOUR FRIEND AWAY FROM MY GATE!”

Joel answered immediately.

“Dennis.”

“Yes?”

“Do not destroy Daniel’s station.”

Dennis looked toward the Seraph capital ship forcing its way through the gate.

“Can I destroy what is coming through it?”

Joel smiled.

“That is why I sent you.”

Dennis raised his blade.

“Good.”

Dennis switched to Daniel's private channel.

“Something for you to measure. I clear the approach before you finish locking the core.”

“Without damaging anything I need.”

“That does narrow the field.”

“Do it and I buy your supper.”

Dennis checked the approach lanes on Daniel's map.

“Make it a good one. I dislike winning on an empty stomach.”

Daniel muttered:

“I still hate all of you.”

Outside, the Seraph capital continued emerging.

And Gatehold’s defenders began turning every surviving weapon toward it.

---

## 4. The Gate Is Not Alone

The first Seraph capital was still only halfway through the gate when Kira spoke again.

Her voice cut across Daniel’s chamber feed, Joel’s command bridge, Annette’s war room, and the surviving Gatehold tactical network.

“Additional transit handshakes detected.”

Daniel looked up from the authentication console.

“No.”

Lucy followed immediately.

“Correction: multiple additional transit handshakes.”

The chamber went still.

Even the Grey Nomad maniacs stopped shouting for half a second.

Daniel pulled the gate queue open.

One hostile command architecture sat behind the emerging capital ship.

Then another.

Then three more.

Different mass signatures.

Cruiser scale.

Boarding carrier scale.

One was larger than all the others.

He stared at the list.

“They stacked a convoy.”

Outside Gatehold, the pale transit field kept burning. The nose of the first Seraph capital pushed farther into normal space while ancient structures around the gate screamed under strain.

Joel saw the same queue from Central and swore quietly.

Annette’s expression became colder.

“How many?”

Kira answered:

“Five confirmed queued handshakes behind the leading capital. Probability of additional masked signals remains high.”

Anton’s voice entered from Bastion.

“That seems rude.”

Christer answered dryly:

“They are invading.”

Dennis Rask looked at the tactical image through his dark visor.

“Then we should be impolite back.”

Daniel pointed at him.

“No destruction.”

“I heard you the first time.”

“You were thinking about it again.”

“Yes.”

Then Dennis turned his helmet slightly toward the right-side corridor wall.

A Grey Nomad veteran blinked.

“What?”

Dennis raised his incineration cannon.

Daniel’s eyes widened.

“Dennis, do not—”

The cannon fired.

White-orange flame punched through the wall in a blinding sheet.

The corridor beyond vanished in fire.

A second later the hidden compartment behind that wall detonated outward. Husk Guards stumbled through the breach already burning, black fluid boiling out of their armour seams. Behind them, something taller reeled into view.

A Seraph commander.

Not a human warform.

A real one.

Tall, thin, draped in black-gold command armour with a human-derived face stretched too elegantly across an inhuman skull. Neural filaments hung behind its neck like a ceremonial veil.

The incineration blast had caught it full in the chest.

It tried to speak.

Then Dennis stepped forward and put a second burst through it.

The creature collapsed in flame.

Silence hit the chamber.

One Grey Nomad marine finally whispered:

“He shot the wall.”

Dennis lowered the cannon.

“There was a commander behind it.”

Daniel stared at the dead Seraph and the burning hidden compartment.

“How did you know?”

Dennis tilted his helmet.

“They hide like officers.”

That was not an answer.

It was somehow worse.

Kira confirmed the kill.

“Hidden compartment breached. Four Husk Guards eliminated. One Seraph command organism terminated.”

Anton immediately laughed over the family channel.

“I like him.”

Daniel answered without looking away from the corpse.

“I do not.”

Outside Gatehold, the battle expanded.

Joel’s fast reinforcement group had already broken the local Ardent screen, but now every surviving sensor cluster in the system was turning toward the gate.

Because the gate was not the only problem anymore.

New contacts entered the outer battlespace from the spinward approach.

Transponders bright.

Weapons hot.

Two human fleets.

One identified itself in blunt, iron-coded declaration.

IMPERIUM OF MAN RELIEF GROUP SIGMA.

The other came under colder, cleaner Zenith signatures.

ZENITH IMPERIUM EXPEDITIONARY SPEAR.

Mara, still assisting at Central command, looked toward Joel.

“They came fast.”

Joel nodded.

“Too fast.”

Annette already understood.

“They were waiting nearby.”

Not for altruism.

Not exactly.

The major powers had been circling Ardent, hoping to gain position once House von Hjertstedt broke the state open. Now two of them had committed military forces at once.

The Iron-bound Imperium group entered first: brutal-looking battleships, escort destroyers, and dense macro-battery cruisers built for direct punishment. Behind them the Zenith formation arrived with cleaner lines, disciplined arrowhead geometry, and bright sensor lattices built for command warfare and long-range strike control.

For three seconds, it looked as though Gatehold might suddenly have help.

Then hidden Seraph ships attacked them.

Black-gold signatures burst from cold shadow above the gate plane and below the ecliptic at the same time. They did not announce themselves. They simply opened fire.

The leading Imperium cruiser took a lance strike across the bow before it could finish its challenge code. Armour blew outward in molten sheets.

A Zenith escort vanished under a missile bloom less than two breaths later.

Joel watched the whole thing unfold.

“They went for the newcomers immediately.”

Annette nodded.

“Because those fleets still arrived in formation.”

The Seraphs did not want organized human reinforcement reaching Gatehold.

On the external channel, the Imperium commander bellowed in fury.

“UNKNOWN HOSTILES IDENTIFIED. RETURN FIRE. FORWARD LINE ADVANCE.”

A Zenith voice came a moment later, cold and furious.

“Combat authority accepted. All batteries engage Seraph contacts.”

Just like that, Gatehold became larger than House versus Ardent.

Human fleets were now entering the kill zone and being blooded on arrival.

Back inside the core chamber, Daniel dragged the commandant away from the console while Dennis and the Hunters sealed the burning hidden compartment.

“Report,” Joel demanded over command.

Daniel checked the gate queue again.

“Five additional hostile transit handshakes confirmed. First capital still emerging. Outer system now contains Imperium and Zenith reinforcements under Seraph attack.”

Anton whistled.

“So everyone gets a war.”

Annette looked toward the tactical spread.

“Joel, Gatehold has become the main axis.”

“Yes.”

“Bastion remains critical, but if Gatehold falls, Ardent becomes an open door.”

Daniel stared at the queue.

“And if that large mass is what I think it is, this door gets much worse.”

Dennis stepped beside him.

“Can the gate be interrupted without destroying it?”

Daniel looked at him suspiciously.

“Why?”

“So I know what I am forbidden from doing.”

Daniel opened the family channel.

“Joel, I am insulting you again.”

Joel almost smiled.

“Noted.”

The station shook as the Seraph capital forced another kilometer of its hull into real space.

Five more enemy handshakes waited behind it.

Outside, the Imperium of Man and Zenith warfleets were already exchanging fire with Seraph ambushers.

Inside, Dennis Rask had just burned a hidden Seraph commander out of a wall.

Gatehold was no longer a battle to save a route.

It was becoming the first true front of a much larger war.

---

## 5. Remove the Limiters

Joel watched Gatehold become the center of the war.

Seraph ships were attacking the newly arrived Imperium and Zenith fleets.

A Seraph capital ship was still forcing itself through the gate.

Five more transit handshakes waited behind it.

Daniel was fighting inside the station with Dennis.

That was enough.

Joel opened the family command channel.

“Anton. Christer.”

Both answered almost immediately.

Anton was still aboard the first captured Ardent capital.

Christer was overseeing the surrendered second capital while House ships exchanged fire with the growing Seraph force around Bastion.

Joel did not waste time.

“Finish Bastion.”

Anton smiled.

“How immediately?”

Joel’s crimson eyes brightened.

“Immediately.”

Christer understood first.

“You want both fleets at Gatehold.”

“Yes.”

“Bastion still has active Seraph ships.”

“Then break them.”

Anton laughed.

“That is a much better order.”

Joel leaned toward the tactical display.

“Take the command centers. Kill or capture every Seraph command organism. Secure the laboratories. Leave enough force to hold the station.”

Then his tone hardened.

“The rest comes to Gatehold.”

Christer nodded.

“Understood.”

Anton reached for his weapon.

“Finally.”

Annette entered the channel.

“Do not destroy evidence.”

Anton stopped.

“You always ruin the simple version.”

“Good.”

Then another signal forced itself into the command layer.

Highest House authority.

Everyone went silent.
