Everything Kestrel had lacked when she first arrived.

Lars spoke.

“If you accept, House von Hjertstedt does not simply protect Kestrel.”

“We invest in it.”

“We reinforce it.”

“We train with it.”

“We connect it.”

“And if someone comes to destroy it…”

His eyes hardened.

“…they discover they attacked part of us.”

Sera looked at Joel.

“You knew I would say yes.”

Joel shook his head.

“I hoped.”

“That is worse.”

She looked toward Lars.

Then toward the huge tactical chamber.

“Give Kestrel the seat.”

Lars nodded once.

The map changed.

Kestrel’s marker became crimson-ringed.

STRATEGIC AFFILIATE — ACCEPTED

Then the reinforcement plans unlocked.

Thousands of logistics tasks populated across the display.

Engineering teams.

Convoys.

Construction schedules.

Defense installations.

Training deployments.

Mara’s face appeared on the secure channel.

She stared at the suddenly exploding workload.

“Sera.”

“Yes?”

“What did you just agree to?”

Sera smiled.

“A future.”

---

## 9. The Open March

Months passed.

Not quietly.

But without another war.

That alone felt like victory.

What had begun as two separate crises—Kestrel’s survival and the Excision of Ardent—slowly became something no strategist had expected when Joel first boarded the Wayward Star pretending to be a passenger.

Territory.

Openly held territory.

For centuries, House von Hjertstedt had possessed Vintergard but avoided building a visible empire beyond it. Its public strength came from contracts, protected corridors, warrants, allied stations, and obligations rather than conquest.

Ardent changed that.

Kestrel confirmed it.

Together, they became the first region beyond the Winter Road where House banners did not disappear when the contract ended.

They stayed.

Kestrel changed first.

The old military station was almost unrecognizable.

The support complex beside it had grown from construction frames into a functioning station of its own.

Its name was Kestrel Shield.

Fleet docks occupied one side.

Civilian repair yards another.

Sanctuary medical berths sat behind the armored core.

Automated farms filled several protected modules.

Training halls, mercenary offices, refugee processing, cargo depots, and Justicar courts operated around the clock.

Sera stood inside Kestrel Command watching traffic move between both stations.

Mara Vale stood beside her.

“Forty-seven arrivals waiting for docking assignment.”

Sera looked at her.

“Military?”

“Eleven.”

“Mercenary?”

“Nineteen.”

“Civilian?”

“The rest.”

Sera smiled.

Months ago they had worried whether anyone would come.

Now they worried where to park them.

Outside, House banners flew openly beside Kestrel colors.

Not above them.

Beside them.

That distinction had become policy.

Kestrel remained Sera’s station.

But everyone knew whose protection stood behind it.

Ardent was different.

Ardent had been conquered.

Central had become the administrative heart of the new territory.

The old Special Custody insignia had been removed completely.

In its place stood the crown and blade of House von Hjertstedt.

Justicar courts operated openly.

Former Ardent civil servants had been screened and returned to duty where possible.

Others were awaiting trial.

Project Shepherd officials occupied hardened prison blocks.

Central’s markets reopened.

Schools reopened.

Traffic resumed.

Bastion became the military center.

Its ruined fleet yards had been rebuilt.

Its defense rings expanded.

Seraph Hunters now maintained permanent quarters there.

Captured Ardent ships had either been returned to vetted crews, absorbed into local defense forces, or dismantled.

Gatehold became something even larger.

The second station beside it was now operational enough to accept warships.

Massive mobile defense platforms surrounded the gate.

Authentication control had been completely rewritten.

No ship crossed without layered verification.

The same gate that had nearly released a Seraph invasion fleet was now becoming one of the hardest roads in human space to force.

The political map changed with it.

Foreign charts began using a new designation:

HJERTSTEDT ARDENT MARCH

House officials hated the word March at first.

Then everyone kept using it.

Eventually Lars stopped objecting.

Kestrel was not formally part of the Ardent March.

But strategically, it sat on the same network.

Together they formed House von Hjertstedt’s first open frontier territory beyond Vintergard.

Not hidden.

Not temporary.

Not merely contracted.

Open.

House patrol ships moved between them.

House logistics schedules connected them.

House Justicars shared warrant systems.

Kestrel mercenaries trained at Bastion.

Ardent engineers worked at Kestrel Shield.

Refugees from damaged Ardent settlements received new homes around Kestrel.

Young House officers began requesting postings there because the frontier had become the place where careers were made.

Something was forming.

Not an empire.

Not yet.

But something larger than a collection of contracts.

Joel returned several months later.

This time he came without secrecy.

His command cruiser entered Kestrel space under full House identification.

No false name.

No traveller coat.

The High Marshal stood on the bridge in black and crimson while Kestrel Shield filled the main display.

Anton stood beside him.

Christer and Daniel watched through remote tactical links.

Annette joined from Central, peach hair loose around her shoulders, the pink rose visible.

Joel stared at the traffic.

“So.”

Anton waited.

Joel continued.

“We accidentally acquired a country.”

Annette answered immediately.

“No.”

Daniel laughed.

Christer added:

“Several stations.”

Anton nodded.

“Some planets.”

Annette frowned.

“A protected territorial administration.”

Joel smiled.

“That sounds suspiciously like a country.”

Lucy entered through Annette’s channel.

“Politically, the distinction is becoming increasingly difficult to maintain.”

Kira added:

“Foreign diplomatic correspondence now treats the Ardent March and Kestrel network as a single Hjertstedt strategic bloc in sixty-three percent of analyzed cases.”

Anton looked delighted.

“See?”

Annette touched the rose.

“Do not encourage him.”

The cruiser docked.

Sera waited at the airlock.

She wore Kestrel command grey with the House affiliation badge beneath her own crest.

Joel stepped through.

Sera looked at him.

“You have seen the reports.”

“Yes.”

“And?”

Joel looked through the observation windows toward Kestrel Shield.

Fresh ships.

Construction lights.

Civilian traffic.

House banners.

Kestrel banners.

People.

“What do your people call all this?” he asked.

Sera smiled.

“Home.”

Joel became quiet.

That answer mattered more than the fleet reports.

More than foreign recognition.

More than territory.

He looked toward Anton.

Anton nodded once.

No joke.

Joel turned back toward Sera.

“Then we keep it that way.”

That evening, the crews relieved from duty filled one of Kestrel's rebuilt mess halls. The next watch ate before taking their stations. Afterward, someone started a song, someone else improved it beyond recognition, and an old admiral produced a betting book with a split leather spine.

A Banner Captain across the table groaned.

“You brought that thing.”

“It says you owe me a bottle.”

“That action was forty-six years ago.”

“And yet you remember which action.”

Anton leaned over to inspect the entry. Within minutes he was explaining why the captain's boarding maneuver deserved a better score.

The admiral listened with professional interest.

“Were you there?”

“No.”

“Then you have finally found a boarding action in which you cannot claim first breach.”

The laughter reached the next table. Even Anton joined it, then demanded the recordings.

Joel set a sealed bottle beside his plate. The price of another like it would have paid for months of a small ship's repairs; another from that batch did not exist. The host poured Winter Reserve into the little acceptance cups, offering milder drinks without comment. One small serving, no second challenge. Anyone still under a medical restriction had water instead; nobody made anything of it.

They toasted the fallen before the music resumed. For the crews still searching the wrecks and the friends still in treatment, Joel's bottle remained unopened.

By the second course, the admiral had found witnesses to an even older dispute. Nobody at the table intended to let him forget the first one.

Outside, patrol ships moved between Kestrel and its new companion station.

Farther away, the fortified road toward Ardent remained open under House protection.

Vintergard was still hidden behind the Winter Road, a self-sufficient civilization outsiders could not freely enter.

But now House von Hjertstedt had something new.

A frontier everyone could see.

A place where outsiders could trade, live, serve, negotiate, and look directly at House banners without ever approaching the secrets of home.

For the first time, the House was no longer merely operating in the Known Reach.

It had begun putting down roots in it.

---

# Chapter 6: The Forgotten Road

## 1. Sightseeing

Kestrel had become busy enough that Joel could disappear in plain sight.

He preferred it that way.

Two days into another visit to Kestrel, he found Anton standing on an observation deck overlooking Kestrel Shield.

The new support station filled half the viewport.

Construction was still active around its outer sections. Tugs carried armor modules toward unfinished docking arms. Vigil frigates moved between patrol corridors. Civilian freighters queued beneath House-controlled traffic beacons.

Beyond them, the original Kestrel station glowed with habitation lights.

Anton held a cup of something unpleasant.

Joel walked beside him.

“We have been responsible for too many meetings recently.”

Anton looked at him.

“That sounds dangerous.”

Joel watched a mercenary destroyer leave one of the docks.

“Time for some sightseeing.”

Anton smiled immediately.

“Now that sounds better.”

Joel brought up a sector map.

Not the strategic command version.

A civilian navigation layer.

Kestrel sat at the center of a rapidly growing web of trade and mercenary traffic.

House protection had changed the routes.

Places that had once been too dangerous for regular shipping were reopening.

Minor stations were advertising contracts.

Old colonies were requesting survey crews.

Salvagers were returning to wreck fields abandoned years ago.

Young House officers were arriving from Vintergard looking for experience.

Independent mercenaries were coming because Kestrel had something rare:

work, repair facilities, reliable courts, and enough guns to make being cheated less likely.

That suited the House. Its wider identity had always been built around contracts, protection, arbitration, and controlled violence rather than ordinary territorial conquest.

Anton studied the map.

“What exactly are we sightseeing?”

Joel expanded the surrounding frontier.

“Everything.”

“That is not a destination.”

“It is a philosophy.”

Anton took another drink.

“You have been spending too much time with Lars.”

They entered Kestrel’s mercenary concourse without uniforms.

Not disguises.

Just ordinary clothes.

Joel wore a dark coat.

Anton wore something that failed completely at making him look harmless.

His size alone ruined the attempt.

They passed contract boards filled with offers.

Convoy escort.

Missing freighter investigation.

Anti-piracy patrol.

Mining claim protection.

Station security.

Survey expeditions.

Recovery work.

One contract offered an absurd payment for retrieving a navigation core from a wreck field.

Anton stopped.

“That one.”

Joel read it.

“No.”

“You did not finish reading.”

“It says seventy-three percent structural instability.”

“So?”

“It also says suspected reactor contamination.”

Anton nodded thoughtfully.

“Interesting.”

“No.”

They kept walking.

The biggest change was the people.

House cadets.

Young sworn-family officers.

Independent crews.

Former Ardent sailors.

Kestrel locals.

Vardos refugees who had become mechanics, dock workers, security officers, traders, and ship crews.

Even several minor foreign military observers were present openly now.

Nobody treated Kestrel like an abandoned station anymore.

It was becoming a frontier capital.

Not politically.

Culturally.

A place where people came because things happened there.

Joel noticed a group of young House officers near one of the contract boards.

They were arguing.

Loudly.

Anton noticed them too.

One wore a Winge family insignia.

Another carried a Skar branch badge.

Two more belonged to families Joel did not recognize immediately.

Anton leaned closer.

“Fresh ones.”

Joel nodded.

“Very.”

“They look confident.”

“They have never been shot at.”

Anton smiled.

“We can fix that.”

Joel looked at him.

“Professionally.”

“Of course.”

The young officers eventually noticed them.

Conversation stopped.

One of them straightened.

“High Marshal.”

Joel sighed.

“So much for sightseeing.”

The Winge cadet looked between Joel and Anton.

“Breach Commander.”

Anton looked offended.

“You said mine with less enthusiasm.”

The cadet immediately became terrified.

Joel rescued him.

“What are you doing?”

“Reviewing available contracts, sir.”

“Which one?”

The cadet pointed toward a small frontier job.

A mining settlement had lost contact with two cargo ships along a minor lane approach.

Nothing major.

No confirmed enemy.

No strategic importance.

Exactly the kind of work House von Hjertstedt had built its reputation on before Ardent made everyone think only in fleets.

Joel read the details.

“You want it?”

“Yes, sir.”

“Why?”

The cadet hesitated.

“For experience.”

Joel shook his head.

“Wrong answer.”

The group became uncomfortable.

Joel pointed toward the contract.

“People are missing.”

The cadet looked again.

Then understood.

“To find them.”

“And?”

“Bring them home if possible.”

Joel nodded.

“Better.”

House doctrine required proof, bounded escalation, and protection of civilian life rather than violence for its own sake. That remained true whether the House deployed three fleets or one frightened cadet crew.

Anton leaned toward Joel.

“You are ruining the fun.”

“I am preventing paperwork.”

“Same thing.”

Sera found them ten minutes later.

Mara was with her.

Sera looked at Joel.

“You said sightseeing.”

“Yes.”

“You are assigning missions.”

“No.”

The Winge cadet quietly said:

“He gave advice.”

Sera stared at Joel.

Joel pointed toward the cadet.

“See?”

Mara looked at Anton.

“And what did you do?”

Anton answered:

“Encouragement.”

One cadet muttered:

“He suggested boarding anything suspicious.”

Anton pointed at him.

“Context matters.”

Mara closed her eyes.

Sera looked at Joel.

“So where are you actually going?”

Joel brought the map up again.

He expanded beyond Kestrel’s immediate patrol zone.

Dozens of minor systems and stations appeared.

Some stable.

Some barely mapped.

Some outside meaningful major-power control.

The larger states claimed influence over many of them.

Few actually protected them.

Joel smiled slightly.

“Out there.”

Sera studied him.

“You are serious.”

“Yes.”

Anton understood now.

Not an inspection tour.

Not diplomacy.

Not another fleet campaign.

They were going back to the frontier.

Small ships.

Small stations.

Contracts.

People nobody powerful cared about yet.

The exact environment where House von Hjertstedt had always been strongest.

And now there was another reason.

Kestrel and Ardent had become the House’s first openly held frontier territory.

If they were going to send hundreds of young officers out from it—

Joel wanted to know what kind of frontier they were inheriting.

Anton finished his drink.

“So we take a cruiser?”

Joel shook his head.

“Too obvious.”

“Destroyer?”

“No.”

“Frigate?”

Joel smiled.

“Better.”

Anton grinned.

“Now I am interested.”

Sera stared at both brothers.

“You are going wandering around frontier space in one frigate.”

Joel nodded.

“With a small crew.”

Mara folded her arms.

“This will end badly.”

Anton looked almost delighted.

“That is why it qualifies as sightseeing.”

Joel turned toward the viewport.

Beyond Kestrel Shield, ships vanished into the lanes one after another.

Some toward Ardent.

Others toward territories nobody had bothered to defend properly in years.

Joel watched them go.

“Let us see what is out there.”

---

## 2. Four Brothers, One Frigate

Departure was scheduled for the next morning.

Joel intended to leave quietly.

That failed immediately.

He reached the assigned Vigil frigate and found Anton already aboard, arguing with the quartermaster about ammunition allocation.

“You do realize this is sightseeing,” Joel said.

Anton looked over.

“Yes.”

“Then why are you requesting breaching charges?”

“Sightseeing equipment.”

Joel kept walking.

He should have known that would not be the worst problem.

At the end of the docking collar stood Christer.

Behind him was Daniel.

Both carrying travel bags.

Joel stopped.

“No.”

Christer smiled.

“Yes.”

Daniel nodded toward the frigate.

“We heard you were going sightseeing.”

Anton appeared behind Joel.

“Excellent.”

Joel looked between them.

“You are supposed to be working.”

Christer shrugged.

“So are you.”

Daniel added:

“Technically you are High Marshal.”

“That is exactly why I can decide what I am doing.”

Daniel nodded.

“Then as senior officers, we have decided to accompany you.”

“That is not how rank works.”

Anton leaned toward Joel.

“It could be.”

The frigate commander looked increasingly uncomfortable.

She had expected to carry Joel and Anton.

That was already enough trouble.

Now four senior members of House von Hjertstedt were standing in her embarkation bay.

Joel noticed her expression.

“Captain?”

She straightened.

“Sir.”

“You may say it.”

She hesitated.

“This is excessive.”

Anton laughed.

Christer looked offended.

Daniel nodded.

“Correct assessment.”

Joel sighed.

The captain continued.

“With respect, High Marshal, this ship is configured for patrol, not family reunions.”

Joel looked toward the others.

“She has a point.”

Christer lifted his bag.

“I packed lightly.”

Daniel did the same.

Anton looked down at his own equipment case.

Joel stared.

“What is in that?”

Anton answered:

“Essentials.”

“That case weighs more than a cadet.”

“Some cadets are small.”

Sera arrived before Joel could throw anyone off the ship.

Mara came with her.

Sera looked at the four brothers.

Then at the frigate.

Then back at Joel.

“No.”

Joel frowned.

“What now?”

“You said a small crew.”

“It is.”

“You now have four Hjertstedts aboard.”

Anton said:

“That is still only four people.”

Mara looked toward him.

“Four people who have personally caused more fleet-level complications than most governments.”

Christer smiled.

“That sounds exaggerated.”

Mara answered:

“It is not.”

Daniel stepped closer to the tactical board.

“We are not taking command.”

The frigate captain visibly relaxed.

Joel immediately added:

“Unless required.”

The relaxation disappeared.

Sera laughed.

The mission itself remained simple.

Visit several minor stations.

Review the frontier routes.

Observe mercenary traffic.

Check how Kestrel’s influence was spreading beyond the official patrol zone.

Talk to local commanders without arriving behind a wall of capital ships.

Small-scale work.

Real work.

The kind of work that had built House legitimacy long before Ardent.

Joel wanted the trip to feel ordinary.

Christer ruined that in less than ten minutes.

He opened the contract feed.

“There is a convoy escort paying badly.”

Joel looked over.

“Then why are you interested?”

“Because someone is hiding something.”

Daniel checked another file.

“This mining colony has requested security assistance three times in six months.”

Anton leaned over his shoulder.

“That sounds promising.”

Joel looked at all three.

“We are sightseeing.”

Christer nodded.

“Yes.”

Daniel added:

“Through problems.”

Anton smiled.

“Best kind.”

The frigate captain finally interrupted.

“High Marshal.”

Joel turned.

“Yes?”

“If all four of you are coming, I need command boundaries established before departure.”

Joel approved immediately.

“Good.”

She brought up the bridge authority map.

“I remain ship captain.”

“Yes.”

“Normal navigation, watch rotation, weapons readiness, and crew discipline remain mine.”

“Yes.”

“None of you issue direct orders to my crew unless I transfer authority or there is an emergency.”

Anton opened his mouth.

Joel raised a hand.

“Agreed.”

Christer nodded.

Daniel nodded too.

The captain looked toward Anton.

Anton sighed.

“Agreed.”

She continued.

“If combat occurs?”

Joel answered:

“You command the ship.”

“And you?”

“I advise unless formally taking flag authority becomes necessary.”

The captain studied him.

“That is very carefully worded.”

Joel smiled.

“It is accurate.”

She nodded once.

“Accepted.”

That settled it.

House doctrine placed command, legal authority, and fleet roles inside formal boundaries for exactly this reason: too much rank aboard one hull could become more dangerous than the enemy if nobody knew who held the bridge.

Departure began twenty minutes later.

The frigate detached from Kestrel Shield.

Traffic control cleared the lane.

Joel stood on the bridge beside Anton while Christer and Daniel occupied observer positions behind them.

The captain looked back.

“Destination?”

Joel brought up the frontier map.

Several small systems appeared.

A repair station.

A mining colony.

A relay settlement.

A cluster of independent habitats.

Nothing important enough for a major power to care about.

Exactly what Joel wanted.

He pointed toward the first route.

“There.”

Daniel looked at the marker.

“That place has no strategic value.”

Joel nodded.

“Correct.”

Christer frowned.

“No major contract.”

“Correct.”

Anton looked interested.

“No warfleet.”

“Hopefully.”

The three of them looked at Joel.

Joel smiled.

“That is why we are going.”

The frigate entered the lane.

Kestrel disappeared behind them.

For the first time in months, none of the brothers commanded a major fleet.

No strategic chamber.

No Seraph invasion.

No occupation government.

No Sol delegation.

Just one frigate moving toward places that might never appear on a major power’s map.

Christer leaned back.

“This feels strange.”

Daniel looked toward him.

“What does?”

“Not having several thousand ships nearby.”

Anton smiled.

“I find it refreshing.”

Joel looked at him.

“You requested breaching charges.”

“I said refreshing, not irresponsible.”

The captain stared forward very hard.

Nobody on the bridge wanted to laugh first.

Then Daniel did.

Christer followed.

Anton joined.

Joel tried not to.

Failed.

The first frontier station appeared on sensors hours later.

Small.

Old.

Independent.

Its transponder had been repaired enough times that the identification packet was mostly maintenance warnings.

The station hailed them.

“Unknown Hjertstedt frigate, state business.”

The captain looked toward Joel.

Joel shook his head.

“Your ship.”

She answered normally.

“Patrol visit. No warrant. No hostile intent.”

A pause.

Then:

“Understood. Docking available.”

Nobody there seemed to know who was aboard.

Joel smiled.

Perfect.

Anton noticed.

“You are enjoying this.”

“Yes.”

Christer stood.

“So are we going in anonymously?”

Joel considered.

“As much as possible.”

Daniel looked at the three of them.

“That should last approximately nine minutes.”

Anton shook his head.

“Optimist.”

The frigate approached the station.

Four brothers watched the frontier come closer.

Not as conquerors.

Not as admirals.

For the moment—

just travelers looking around.

---

## 3. The Rust Bucket

The first frontier station turned out to be exactly what Joel had hoped for.

Old.

Busy.

Poor enough that everyone repaired everything twice.

Independent enough that nobody wanted a flag hanging over them.

And ordinary enough that four von Hjertstedts arriving aboard a Vigil frigate immediately ruined the experiment.

It took six hours.

By then, half the station knew.

By the next morning, everyone did.

The frigate captain found Joel in a maintenance café with Anton, Christer, and Daniel.

“We have a problem.”

Joel looked up.

“What kind?”

“The local traffic authority has reassigned three patrol routes because they believe your presence means House protection has expanded here.”

Christer winced.

Daniel muttered:

“That was fast.”

The captain continued.

“Two mercenary companies cancelled departure because they assume we are inspecting contracts.”

Anton smiled.

“Efficient.”

Joel looked at him.

“This is exactly what I did not want.”

The House had become too recognizable.

A proper Vigil frigate was not simply transport anymore.

It was a declaration.

House ships carried doctrine, reputation, warrants, and political meaning with them.

Joel leaned back.

“We send the frigate home.”

The captain blinked.

“Sir?”

“Back to Kestrel.”

Anton slowly smiled.

Christer immediately became suspicious.

Daniel closed his eyes.

“No.”

Joel looked toward him.

“I have not said anything yet.”

“I know you.”

Christer pointed at the docking map.

“What exactly do you intend to travel in?”

Joel opened the civilian sales registry.

Anton moved closer.

“Oh.”

Daniel looked.

Then became visibly unhappy.

Most ships listed were old commercial hulls.

Courier craft.

Converted cargo boats.

Mining transports.

A few armed freighters.

Nothing House-built.

Nothing impressive.

Nothing that would cause a station commander to panic because a High Marshal had arrived.

Joel scrolled.

Stopped.

Anton leaned over his shoulder.

“That one.”

Christer looked at it.

“No.”

Daniel looked at it.

“Absolutely not.”

Joel smiled.

The ship was a small independent long-range transport that had once been a cargo courier.

Once.

Probably several owners ago.

Its hull plating consisted of at least five visibly different repair batches.

The port engine nacelle had been replaced with one from another production series.

One maneuvering thruster was technically listed as “intermittent.”

Its sensor mast leaned slightly.

There was corrosion around one docking collar.

The registry carried seventeen maintenance warnings.

And somebody had painted a faded blue stripe along the side badly enough that it stopped halfway across an armor patch.

Anton looked delighted.

“It is perfect.”

The seller was named Pell Orven.

He had the expression of a man who had spent thirty years selling ships to people who could not afford better.

He met them in Dock Forty-One.

The vessel sat behind him.

Up close, it looked worse.

Christer stared upward.

“That cannot be spaceworthy.”

Pell looked offended.

“She is certified.”

Daniel checked the hull registry.

“Barely.”

“Certified is certified.”

Anton walked underneath the ship.

A small piece of insulation fell beside him.

He looked down.

Then up.

“I like her.”

Joel asked:

“How much?”

Pell gave the price.

Joel stared at him.

“That is robbery.”

Pell shrugged.

“You arrived aboard a House frigate.”

Joel looked toward Anton.

“This is your fault.”

“My fault?”

“You look wealthy.”

Anton spread his arms.

“I am wearing a jacket.”

“A very expensive jacket.”

Christer walked toward the engine assembly.

“This thing has mismatched reactor shielding.”

Pell nodded proudly.

“Redundancy.”

Daniel looked at him.

“That is not what redundancy means.”

They spent an hour inspecting it.

The main drive worked.

Mostly.

Life support was ugly but reliable.

The reactor had been rebuilt twice.

Navigation was old.

Sensors were worse.

The cargo bay had enough space for equipment, supplies, and a small ground vehicle.

The ship possessed two legal defensive gun mounts.

Only one currently worked.

Anton discovered an old concealed boarding locker beneath the cargo deck.

Joel looked at Pell.

“You did not mention that.”

Pell shrugged.

“Previous owner.”

“What did they do?”

“Salvage.”

Daniel looked inside.

Magnetic clamps.

Cutting gear mounts.

Old restraint brackets.

Christer nodded.

“Pirates.”

Pell looked offended again.

“Salvagers.”

Anton smiled.

“Very aggressive salvagers.”

The universe was full of ships like this: patched commercial hulls, converted freighters, improvised escorts, and vessels held together by crews who knew every repair personally. Sci-Fi Universe Outline

Joel liked that.

Nobody would see this ship and think High Marshal.

Nobody would see it and call a fleet.

Nobody would assume territorial expansion.

They might assume bankruptcy.

That was useful.

Joel bought it.

Pell stared at the transfer confirmation.

“You actually want it?”

Joel looked at him.

“You were selling it.”

“Yes, but…”

Pell stopped.

Anton laughed.

Even the seller had expected negotiation to fail.

Christer looked toward Joel.

“We are not taking this beyond local space.”

Joel nodded.

“Correct.”

Daniel relaxed slightly.

Joel continued:

“Until we service it.”

Daniel’s relief vanished.

The Vigil frigate departed that afternoon.

Its captain looked almost too happy.

She stood at the boarding collar before leaving.

“High Marshal.”

“Yes?”

“I am formally recording that I advised against this.”

“Good.”

“I am also informing Kestrel Command where you are.”

Joel frowned.

“That seems unnecessary.”

“It is absolutely necessary.”

Anton nodded toward her.

“Good captain.”

Joel ignored him.

The frigate undocked and burned toward the return lane.

For the first time since leaving Kestrel, the four brothers had no House warship nearby.

Only the rust bucket.

Christer watched the frigate disappear.

“This feels irresponsible.”

Anton looked at the battered transport.

“Now it feels like sightseeing.”

Before they started work, Daniel laid a thermal survey on the cargo deck.

“A private challenge,” he said. “Coolant loop within tolerance on the first certified run.”

Anton folded his arms.

“Against?”

“Your gun passing the same inspection without a lecture.”

“Name the bottle.”

“Settle the eastern one first.”

“That was disputed.”

Christer looked up from the sensor parts.

“Only by the debtor.”

Joel took the survey slate.

“The mechanics certify both. Nobody grades his own work.”

They spent two days making the ship trustworthy enough to travel.

Not good.

Trustworthy.

Daniel took engineering.

Christer rebuilt parts of the sensor suite using commercial components purchased from three different shops.

Anton repaired the defensive gun mount, then quietly improved it.

Joel discovered this and removed half the modifications.

Anton put some back.

Joel removed them again.

They compromised.

Eventually.

The station mechanics began watching.

Then helping.

Not because of rank.

Because four apparently wealthy idiots had bought the worst ship in the dock and were genuinely repairing it themselves.

That earned respect faster than uniforms did.

A mechanic named Tessa Hal offered Joel a replacement heat exchanger.

“It is ugly.”

Joel looked at the ship.

“That seems appropriate.”

On the third morning, the transport powered up.

The reactor coughed.

The lights dimmed.

Then stabilized.

Daniel stared at the engineering display.

“I hate this vessel.”

Christer answered from sensors:

“I hate the sensor array.”

Anton called from weapons:

“I like the gun.”

Joel sat in the pilot seat.

“That worries me most.”

Tessa's signed checks lay between them. The coolant loop had passed on its first run. The gun had required a long conversation about power draw.

Anton read the sheet twice.

“I am having the eastern bottle sent to Kestrel for you.”

Daniel looked over.

“You accept the bridge ruling?”

“No. I accept that you will never shut up.”

“That was always the strongest part of my claim.”

Anton sent the instruction to his cellar keeper, with Daniel named as the new owner. Christer recorded the transfer beside the original wager. Joel took the gun's certified limits to the pilot seat.

They had also reached another problem.

The ship needed a name.

Anton suggested Glorious Mistake.

Daniel suggested Bad Decision.

Christer suggested Inspection Failure.

Joel looked at the faded blue stripe outside through the dock camera.

Then at the rusted hull.

“No.”

He entered a name into the registry.

WAYWARD HOUND

Anton stared.

“That is surprisingly respectable.”

Joel smiled.

“Give it time.”

They undocked.

No escort.

No House transponder.

No ceremonial identification.

Just four brothers aboard an old independent transport heading deeper into the frontier.

The station controller called:

“Wayward Hound, you are cleared.”

Joel pushed the throttles forward.

The port engine vibrated.

Daniel immediately said:

“That noise is new.”

Anton looked toward him.

“Bad?”

“I do not know yet.”

Christer laughed.

Joel watched the stars shift ahead.

Behind them stood Kestrel and Ardent.

House banners.

New territory.

Fleet power.

Responsibility.

Ahead lay stations that barely appeared on strategic maps.

Minor colonies.

Independent crews.

Old lanes.

Bad contracts.

People who might never have seen a House warship.

Exactly what Joel wanted.

Anton leaned against the console.

“So.”

Joel looked toward him.

“So?”

“Where first?”

Joel expanded the frontier map.

A dozen questionable destinations appeared.

He smiled.

“Somewhere nobody expects us.”

---

## 4. The Forgotten Map

The Wayward Hound found work immediately.

Not glamorous work.

Survey work.

The kind of contracts larger mercenary outfits ignored because there was little money and even less glory.

A mining colony wanted its outer approach rescanned after three navigation buoys stopped reporting.

A farming habitat needed someone to verify whether an old relay beacon was still transmitting usable lane data.

A refinery consortium wanted a debris field mapped before reopening a shipping corridor.

Joel accepted all three.

Anton stared at the contract list.

“We used to capture capital ships.”

Joel checked the fuel estimate.

“Now we get paid to look at rocks.”

Anton considered.

“I preferred the capital ships.”

Daniel called from engineering.

“Rocks are less likely to shoot back.”

Christer answered from the sensor station.

“You have just guaranteed they will.”

The first job took them six hours.

The Wayward Hound moved slowly along the edge of a mining system while Christer rebuilt a local navigation picture from commercial sensors, passive observations, and old colonial charts.

Joel flew.

Anton complained.

Daniel monitored the engines because nobody trusted them.

The missing buoys turned out not to be sabotage.

A micrometeor storm had destroyed one and pushed the others far enough off position that their directional broadcasts were useless.

Simple.

They recovered one.

Marked the second for salvage.

Deployed a temporary beacon.

The colony paid them.

Not much.

But enough for fuel, food, and two replacement coolant pumps.

Joel looked pleased.

Anton looked insulted by the amount.

“That barely buys ammunition.”

“We are not buying ammunition.”

Anton looked at him.

“We should always buy ammunition.”

The second contract became more interesting.

An agricultural station called Morrow’s Rest had inherited navigation records older than most of its current population.

The station administrator met them personally.

“You are surveyors?”

Joel nodded.

“Today.”

She looked at the four men.

“You do not look like surveyors.”

Anton smiled.

“What do surveyors look like?”

“Tired.”

Daniel answered:

“Give us time.”

The administrator handed over a corrupted navigation archive.

Most of it was useless.

Then Christer found something buried underneath modern route information.

An old gate designation.

No current traffic.

No recognized authentication.

No confirmed operational status.

Just coordinates.

They went to look.

The gate floated at the edge of a dead system.

Ancient.

Dark.

Far larger than anything the local colony needed.

The Wayward Hound approached carefully.

Joel kept them outside any plausible activation envelope.

Anton stared through the forward display.

“Dead?”

Christer studied the sensors.

“Maybe.”

Daniel shook his head.

“No.”

Joel looked over.

“What?”

“Power residue.”

“How old?”

“Cannot tell.”

Christer adjusted the scan.

“There is internal activity.”

Anton became interested.

Joel immediately said:

“We are not entering it.”

Anton looked offended.

“I said nothing.”

“You were about to.”

“I was thinking.”

“That was enough.”

They recorded everything.

Gate geometry.

Energy readings.

Local debris.

Approach vectors.

Old navigation markers.

Any remaining transponder fragments.

Then Joel created a separate archive.

Not part of the colony survey.

Not yet.

UNVERIFIED VARANTHI INFRASTRUCTURE — LOCAL RECORD 001

Christer looked at him.

“You are building a map.”

Joel nodded.

“Someone should.”

That became the second purpose of the trip.

Everywhere they went, they surveyed more than the contract required.

Not secretly against clients.

Simply broader.

Old gates.

Dead relays.

Abandoned stations.

Forgotten fuel depots.

Collapsed mining habitats.

Military structures from governments that no longer existed.

Drifting wrecks whose registries belonged to states none of them recognized.

Sometimes nothing remained except an old navigation beacon still repeating the same message after a century.

Anton called them ghosts.

Joel called them infrastructure.

Daniel called them maintenance nightmares.

Christer recorded everything.

The Known Reach was officially mapped.

The frontier was not.

Official charts showed safe lanes and active stations.

They did not show everything between them.

And sometimes what had been marked dead was merely unattended.

Three weeks passed.

The Wayward Hound accumulated scars.

And modifications.

Daniel replaced the unreliable maneuvering thruster.

Christer mounted a secondhand long-range sensor array beneath the old cargo spine.

Anton repaired both defensive gun mounts and somehow added another armored weapons locker.

Joel discovered it.

He stared at Anton.

“You promised.”

“I promised not to install another gun.”

“That is a locker.”

“Correct.”

“What is inside?”

Anton smiled.

Joel decided not to ask.

The ship remained ugly.

But increasingly capable.

They worked wherever they found jobs.

A moon colony paid them to survey an ice-mining approach.

They discovered an abandoned patrol station beyond the moon.

A trade habitat hired them to map pirate hiding locations inside an asteroid belt.

They found an old Varanthi navigation pillar embedded in one of the larger rocks.

A small religious settlement wanted confirmation that a nearby lane was safe after strange drive blooms appeared.

The blooms were harmless commercial traffic.

But beyond the normal approach, Christer found a dead station.

No power.

No bodies.

No registry.

The station had been stripped almost clean.

Joel marked it.

UNKNOWN STATION — RECORD 014

Anton looked at the growing archive.

“How many now?”

Christer answered.

“Fourteen stations.”

Daniel added:

“Five gates.”

“Two questionable relays.”

“Three military depots.”

“And one wreck field we are absolutely not entering again.”

Anton smiled.

“That was a good wreck field.”

Daniel stared at him.

“The reactor debris was still radioactive.”

“Yes.”

“That is not good.”

The local colonies started talking.

Not about House von Hjertstedt.

About the Wayward Hound.

A battered survey ship that actually arrived when promised.

Its crew took cheap jobs.

Returned missing people where they could.

Shared safe-route data.

Warned colonies about dangerous wrecks.

Refused obvious extortion contracts.

Accepted payment in fuel, supplies, repair parts, or sometimes information when money was scarce.

Nobody knew who they really were.

That was exactly what Joel wanted.

He learned more over cheap meals with dock workers than he could have learned from a hundred strategic reports.

Where ships vanished.

Which station administrators were corrupt.

Which routes were becoming dangerous.

Which colonies were growing.

Which ones were dying.

Which old gates occasionally showed unexplained power activity.

And which parts of the frontier had been forgotten because no major power considered them worth defending.

One evening, Joel and Anton sat in the tiny galley while Christer organized survey records and Daniel fought with a coolant alarm.

Joel projected their accumulated map onto the table.

Hundreds of local notes now surrounded the official route network.

Anton stared.

“That is becoming substantial.”

Joel nodded.

“They are all disconnected.”

“Not anymore.”

Joel expanded the map.

Old gates formed patterns.

Dead stations clustered around forgotten approaches.

Abandoned military infrastructure sat near routes no longer shown on public charts.

There was history beneath the modern map.

Infrastructure from older political borders.

Perhaps older lane networks.

Perhaps things nobody had properly surveyed since the Varanthi collapse.

Anton looked toward him.

“You planned this.”

Joel shook his head.

“No.”

“That worries me more.”

Joel smiled.

“We came sightseeing.”

Anton looked at the growing frontier archive.

“This is an unusual definition.”

Joel leaned back.

“We are seeing what everyone else stopped looking at.”

And somewhere inside the expanding map—

several dead gates appeared to point toward the same empty region.

---

## 5. The Old Road

Christer found the pattern at 02:17 ship time.

He woke everyone.

Daniel arrived in engineering clothes.

Anton arrived armed.

Joel arrived annoyed.

“What happened?”

Christer pointed toward the galley projection.

“Nothing.”

Anton stared at him.

“You woke me because nothing happened?”

“No. I woke you because five dead gates are pointing at the same place.”

That ended the complaints.

The Wayward Hound had recorded five Varanthi-era gates over several weeks.

Different systems.

Different sizes.

Different states of decay.

One had internal power residue.

Another had been surrounded by debris.

Two appeared completely dead.

The fifth had not responded to anything.

Individually, none mattered.

Together, they did.

Christer overlaid their surviving alignment data.

The vectors converged.

Not perfectly.

Thousands of years of drift, damaged navigation markers, and corrupted station records made exact geometry impossible.

But the direction was unmistakable.

Daniel leaned closer.

“The alignment is worth testing. I would not feed it to navigation yet.”

Joel studied the projection.

“Show the public lane network.”

Christer added it.

The modern routes appeared.

Bright.

Known.

Maintained.

The old gates sat outside them like forgotten bones.

Anton frowned.

“There is nothing there.”

“On current charts,” Christer said.

Joel looked toward the destination.

The region was marked as empty deep frontier.

No recognized system.

No authenticated lane.

No modern relay.

Nothing worth visiting.

Daniel opened historical astronomical data.

“There are stars.”

Anton looked at him.

“That is generally how space works.”

Daniel ignored him.

“Several candidates. But none registered as inhabited.”

Christer expanded one.

A dim yellow-white star.

Then another.

A red dwarf.

Then a binary.

Joel pointed toward the first.

“Distance?”

Christer checked.

“From the convergence? Close enough.”

Daniel frowned.

“But no usable lane goes there.”

Joel looked at the five dead gates again.

“Maybe one did.”

They spent the next day checking every record they had collected.

The abandoned military depot near Vesper Moon.

The stripped station designated Record Fourteen.

The Varanthi navigation pillar inside the asteroid field.

The dead relay near Morrow’s Rest.

Even an old freight chart a dock worker had given Joel in exchange for repairing his pressure regulator.

Pieces started fitting.

Not proof.

But structure.

The dead stations were not randomly placed.

They sat near the old gate approaches.

The depots were positioned where supply traffic would have needed them.

The navigation pillar referenced a route identifier that no longer existed in modern databases.

Christer found it embedded in damaged code:

AR-17 / INNER RETURN

Daniel stared.

“Return from where?”

Nobody answered.

Joel created a new archive category.

POSSIBLE LOST CORRIDOR NETWORK

Anton smiled.

“That sounds much better than survey work.”

Joel looked at him.

“We are still surveying.”

“Yes.”

“Not raiding.”

“Of course.”

“No boarding anything ancient.”

Anton looked genuinely offended.

“I understand restraint.”

Daniel laughed from engineering.

They visited the nearest gate again.

The one with power residue.

This time they approached more carefully.

The Wayward Hound coasted cold for most of the final distance.

No active scan until Christer had enough passive data.

The gate hung against the stars.

Dark.

Silent.

Ancient metal.

Joel kept them far outside the throat.

Christer whispered:

“Internal thermal variation.”

Daniel checked.

“Confirmed.”

Anton leaned forward.

“So it is alive.”

“Operational is a stronger word,” Daniel said.

Joel watched the gate.

“Any authentication response?”

Christer transmitted only a basic neutral query.

Nothing.

Then an old civilian Varanthi-compatible handshake copied from one of the frontier station archives.

Still nothing.

Daniel checked the energy profile.

“Wait.”

A tiny spike.

Gone almost immediately.

Everyone saw it.

Anton stood.

“It heard us.”

Joel’s expression hardened.

“Or something behind it did.”

Christer compared the response timing to their previous visit.

“It reacted faster this time.”

Daniel looked toward Joel.

“Because we used the old handshake.”

“Possibly.”

Joel ordered another transmission.

Not authentication.

Only route inquiry.

The gate remained dark.

Then the Wayward Hound’s navigation system flickered.

One line appeared.

Not from their software.

Not generated by Christer.

A bearing.

Then another.

Then a corrupted block of symbols.

Daniel immediately isolated the navigation computer.

“External injection.”

Anton reached for a weapon out of instinct.

Christer stared at the decoded fragment.

“It is giving us an approach.”

Joel did not move.

“Can we trust it?”

“No.”

“Can we record it?”

“Yes.”

“Then record it.”

The bearing pointed toward the same convergence region.

The empty space between known systems.

They returned to the station that evening and started asking questions.

Quietly.

Old mechanics.

Retired pilots.

Salvagers.

People whose knowledge existed outside official archives.

Most knew nothing.

Then an elderly cargo captain stopped talking when Joel showed him the route marker.

AR-17

The man stared at it.

“Where did you get that?”

Joel answered:

“Old records.”

The captain looked toward the door.

“My grandfather used to call that the Winterless Road.”

Anton frowned.

“Why?”

“Because ships went down it and did not come back.”

Christer asked:

“When?”

“Long before I was born.”

“Military?”

“Some.”

“Civilian?”

“Yes.”

“What happened?”

The old captain shook his head.

“No one knows.”

He pointed toward the dead gate.

“Then the relays stopped answering.”

“Authorities marked the route unsafe.”

“Years later they removed it from commercial charts.”

Daniel asked:

“Was there a system at the end?”

The captain hesitated.

Then nodded.

“A station, at least.”

“Name?”

“I only heard one.”

Everyone waited.

The captain looked toward Joel.

“Caulder.”

Silence.

Joel entered it into the archive.

CAULDER — UNVERIFIED

Anton looked toward the projected convergence.

Finally, the empty region had a name.

Back aboard the Wayward Hound, Christer expanded their reconstructed map.

Five dead gates.

Three abandoned support stations.

Two forgotten depots.

One corrupted route identifier.

All pointing toward Caulder.

Daniel looked at Joel.

“We send this to Kestrel.”

Joel nodded.

“Secure copy.”

“And wait?”

Joel stared at the map.

Anton smiled before Joel answered.

“No.”

Joel looked toward him.

Anton’s smile widened.

Joel sighed.

“We investigate the next approach.”

Daniel shook his head.

“Of course we do.”

The Wayward Hound turned toward another forgotten gate.

And for the first time, their sightseeing trip had a destination.

---

## 6. The Gate That Should Not Have Opened

The next gate on the reconstructed route was not dead.

Christer proved that when the Wayward Hound transmitted the recovered AR-17 / INNER RETURN route marker.

For three seconds, nothing happened.

Then the gate answered.

A low energy pulse rolled through the structure.

Ancient segments illuminated one after another.

Daniel stared at engineering.

“That is significantly more power than the first gate.”

Anton leaned toward the forward display.

“So it works.”

Daniel looked at him.

“I did not say that.”

Joel immediately ordered:

“Back us away.”

The Wayward Hound fired maneuvering thrusters.

The gate brightened.

Then every alarm aboard the ship activated.

Christer shouted:

“Navigation override!”

Daniel:

“Drive control is being pulled into synchronization!”

Joel grabbed the controls.

“Cut the engines!”

“I AM!”

The gate did not care.

The old transport began accelerating toward the throat.

Anton looked through the viewport.

“That seems bad.”

Daniel shouted:

“The gate thinks we already committed!”

Joel fought the controls.

“Emergency reverse!”

Nothing.

The ancient structure erupted with white transit light.

Christer looked at the data and went pale.

“Arrival solution is corrupted.”

“How corrupted?”

“I do not know where it is sending us.”

Joel had time for one response.

“Brace!”

The Wayward Hound vanished.

Transit lasted too long.

Then not long enough.

The ship emerged sideways.

Everything became noise.

The Wayward Hound spun through an arrival envelope filled with debris.

Joel slammed maneuvering control.

Daniel screamed:

“REACTOR STABLE!”

Christer:

“CONTACTS EVERYWHERE!”

Anton:

“THAT ONE IS VERY CLOSE!”

A burning frigate crossed their bow.

Not House.

Not any faction Joel recognized.

Its hull was broad, dense, heavily compartmentalized.

Another vessel rolled beside it, physically placing itself between the damaged ship and incoming fire.

Then the sky filled with missiles.

Joel threw the transport downward.

Point-defense fire erupted from warships across the battlespace.

Not a skirmish.

Not a raid.

A fleet battle.

A massive one.

Christer rebuilt the tactical picture.

His screen filled with contacts faster than he could classify them.

Hundreds.

Then more.

One side fought in organized formations built around overlapping support.

When one ship broke away damaged, another immediately shifted to cover it.

Command emissions came from everywhere.

No obvious flagship.

No single command vessel.

The other side—

Anton recognized immediately.

“Oh, excellent.”

Joel looked toward the display.

Garooth.

A convergence fleet.

Scrap cruisers.

Breacher frigates.

Missile hulks.

Ram ships.

Assault carriers.

Entire vessels made from captured frames welded together into brutal masses.

The battle had already collapsed from Hammer Range toward Knife Range.

Garooth boarding craft swarmed everywhere.

The Garooth existed to turn wreckage into more war, overwhelming formations with saturation fire before collapsing into grapples, rams, and boarding assaults.

Daniel stared at the numbers.

“We need to leave.”

Christer checked the gate behind them.

“No.”

Joel turned.

“What?”

“The gate is unstable.”

“Can we transit back?”

“Not without an authentication solution.”

Anton looked outside.

“So we are trapped.”

“Yes.”

Anton smiled.

Joel pointed at him.

“Stop enjoying this.”

A transmission struck their comm system.

Human.

But wrong.

Old linguistic structures wrapped around recognizable words.

Christer ran translation.

A woman appeared.

Armored collar.

Short dark hair.

Blood across one side of her face.

Behind her, a command deck shook under impact.

“Unknown civilian vessel. You are inside an active combat volume.”

Joel answered:

“We noticed.”

The woman paused.

“Identify.”

Joel hesitated.

One second.

Then:

“Independent survey vessel Wayward Hound.”

Anton looked at him.

Technically true.

The officer continued.

“Your emergence signature came through Gate Seventeen.”

Christer looked sharply toward Joel.

Seventeen.

AR-17.

The woman’s expression changed.

“That gate has not admitted outbound traffic for generations.”

Joel answered:

“It malfunctioned.”

Another explosion hit her bridge.

She did not flinch.

“Then your timing is unfortunate.”

Behind her, a tactical warning sounded.

The transmission abruptly shifted.

“Break vector immediately. Warband approaching your position.”

Christer shouted:

“CONTACT BLOOM!”

Three Garooth vessels burned toward them.

One corvette.

Two boarding cutters.

Anton stood.

“Finally.”

Joel pointed toward him.

“This is a cargo transport.”

“It has guns.”

“One and a half guns.”

“Two now.”

Joel looked at Daniel.

Daniel shrugged.

“He fixed the second one.”

The Wayward Hound accelerated.

Badly.

Its mismatched engines screamed.

The first Garooth cutter opened fire.

Rounds hammered across the transport’s stern.

Daniel shouted from engineering:

“WE ARE LOSING COOLANT!”

Joel rolled the ship behind a shattered Garooth frigate.

Christer tracked the approaching corvette.

“We cannot outrun it.”

Anton strapped into the weapons station.

“Then stop trying.”

Joel looked at the tactical picture.

Nearest organized human formation: four frigates and a crippled cruiser withdrawing toward a larger defensive cluster.

“Christer, give me their vector.”

“You want to run toward the battle?”

“I want to run toward people with better guns.”

“Reasonable.”

Joel drove directly toward the unknown human formation.

The Garooth followed.

That was the mistake.

The strange frigates reacted instantly.

No flagship order.

No visible command delay.

One shifted.

Then another.

A third rotated across the formation.

Their fire converged.

The pursuing Garooth corvette lost its drives in seconds.

The cutters tried to scatter.

Anton fired the Wayward Hound’s repaired defensive mount.

One cutter disappeared behind debris.

The second survived.

Then a broad human frigate punched through it with a precision burst.

Joel stared.

“They coordinated that without speaking.”

Christer shook his head.

“They were speaking.”

“Where?”

“Everywhere.”

Multiple authenticated command nodes were sharing authority across the formation.

Damage one command source and the others continued.

Destroy one ship and the formation reorganized.

It was nothing like the hierarchical fleets Joel knew.

The woman returned on comms.

“Survey vessel, fall inside our screen.”

Joel answered:

“Understood.”

“Your vessel is damaged.”

Daniel shouted from engineering:

“That is an understatement!”

The officer apparently heard him.

“Support craft will assist.”

Within seconds, a rescue corvette moved alongside them while the battle still raged.

Not after.

During.

The stranger formation covered damaged ships as naturally as it fired weapons.

Joel watched another crippled vessel being physically screened while boarding craft transferred survivors.

These people did not abandon damaged hulls easily.

That was doctrine.

Culture.

Not improvisation.

The woman looked directly at Joel.

“You crossed Gate Seventeen.”

“Yes.”

“From the far side?”

“Yes.”

Silence.

Her expression changed completely.

Behind her, someone whispered something Joel could not hear.

Then she asked:

“What human authority controls the territory beyond that gate?”

Joel exchanged looks with Anton.

Christer.

Daniel.

That question was much larger than the battle.

Joel answered carefully.

“There is no single human authority.”

The woman stared.

Then another voice entered the channel.

Calm.

Formal.

“Unknown vessel, this is the Talyrn Continuance.”

Joel became very still.

The name meant nothing to him.

Yet.

The speaker continued:

“You have crossed into the Odrava during an active defensive campaign.”

The tactical map widened.

The brothers finally saw the true scale.

Hundreds of Talyrn warships.

Thousands of Garooth contacts.

Fortified moons burning in the distance.

Stations under siege.

Entire fleets locked together across multiple engagement zones.

The supposedly empty region beyond the forgotten gates was not empty at all.

It contained a human civilization.

And that civilization was already at war.

The Talyrn Continuance had survived isolation by building its society and military around mutual obligation, distributed command, recovery, and the refusal to let any single lost leader or ship collapse the whole formation.

Anton slowly looked toward Joel.

“So.”

Joel kept watching the battle.

Anton smiled.

“Sightseeing.”

Joel sighed.

“Yes.”

Then the Talyrn tactical net issued a warning.

A Garooth capital formation was turning toward them.

Joel looked at the battered controls of the Wayward Hound.

“Daniel?”

“Yes?”

“How much ship do we have left?”

A pause.

“Enough to make another bad decision.”

Joel smiled.

“Good.”

---

## 7. Boarding the Beast

The Talyrn officer had not finished warning them before Joel made his decision.

“Where is their command vessel?”

The woman on the display hesitated.

Then a red icon expanded across the tactical plot.

It was not merely a capital ship.

It was a wound in space.

A vast Garooth flagship built around the armored corpse of something older and far larger. Layer upon layer of scavenged hulls had been welded into it. Engine towers jutted out in ugly masses. Ram spines projected forward like broken teeth. Gun decks glowed hot along the flanks. Smaller warships clung to it like parasites.

Anton stared at it.

“Beautiful.”

Daniel looked at him.

“No.”

Christer studied the approach vectors.

“If that thing anchors the center, the Talyrn line cannot stabilize.”

Joel nodded once.

“Then we remove it.”

The Talyrn officer stared.

“You are aboard a damaged courier transport.”

“Yes.”

“You cannot be serious.”

Joel looked toward his brothers.

“We are.”

He cut the channel and opened the ship-wide compartment link.

“Arm up.”

Anton smiled immediately.

Daniel sighed like a man accepting inevitable bad choices.

Christer simply stood.

The Wayward Hound had no proper armory, but that no longer mattered. The brothers had brought equipment in pieces, cases, and locked travel trunks that had looked excessive when loaded at the frontier station.

Now they opened them.

Joel armed first.

His war plate was House command armor, black and deep iron with crimson optic slits and a crowned wolf worked into the chest in subdued metal. It was not ornate. It was efficient. Reinforced shoulder plating covered the damage still healing from Ardent. A mag-locked boarding axe settled against his back, and a heavy sidearm locked onto his thigh.

Anton’s armor was broader and far less restrained. Assault plates layered over dense powered musculature. Breach claws were built into one gauntlet. His helmet optics burned a violent red. Massive pauldrons carried scar marks rather than decoration, and a brutal boarding blade locked along one leg. He checked the seal and grinned.

“Now it feels right.”

Christer’s armor looked cleaner. More disciplined. Heavy tactical plate fitted with sensor vanes, command-link uplinks, and reinforced articulation for precise movement rather than raw impact. His war helm had a narrow visor and integrated tactical projection crown, making him look more executioner than knight.

Daniel’s was the most practical. Engineer-command armor modified for combat. Heavier than line officer plate, lighter than Anton’s assault rig, with servo-assist braces, tool hardpoints, and a compact breaching kit built into the back assembly. He carried a rifle, a short blade, and enough demolition tools to worry everyone else.

Anton looked at him.

“You packed charges.”

Daniel checked a magazine.

“I know you.”

“Fair.”

The Talyrn sent a boarding corvette to retrieve them.

Its crew saw the four Hjertstedts emerge from the Wayward Hound and stopped speaking for several seconds.

Joel stepped aboard first.

“We are going to the flagship.”

The Talyrn boarding commander looked at the display, then at the four armored men.

“You intend to board that?”

Anton answered before Joel could.

“Yes.”

The commander stared.

Joel added:

“You need the center broken.”

“Yes.”

“Then give us the fastest route in.”

The Talyrn officer did not argue again.

As the corvette burned toward the Garooth capital, the battlefield widened around them.

Talyrn formations bent but did not collapse. Damaged ships were still being screened. Garooth ram craft smashed into defensive lines. Boarding pods struck everywhere. The flagship sat at the center of it all, vomiting smaller craft and hammering the Talyrn line with crude but overwhelming firepower.

Joel checked the insertion plan.

“Mid-hull weapon trench. We cut inward toward command.”

Christer nodded.

“Fastest route with acceptable survival odds.”

Daniel looked at the numbers.

“Acceptable is doing terrible work there.”

Christer checked the fallback route again.

“Beat my junction time from the cruiser, then.”

“Without your security access.”

“Yours. You insisted it counted.”

Daniel's answering smile was brief. He sent the fallback route to all four suits before closing the private link.

Anton activated external comms.

“Can I challenge the war boss now?”

Joel looked at him.

“You may challenge the war boss when we find the war boss.”

Anton sounded disappointed.

“That is bureaucratic.”

The Talyrn corvette hit the flagship under covering fire from three frigates. Enemy guns raked space around them. The hull ahead was a mountain of scorched scrap and armor plates riveted over older wounds.

“Clamp!”

The corvette slammed on.

Locks engaged.

Daniel planted charges on the inner hatch.

Joel gave one signal.

Breach.

The hull blew inward.

Heat, smoke, and the stink of grease, blood, and burning metal hit them all at once.

Inside waited Garooth boarders.

Anton laughed over the squad channel.

“This will be fun.”

Then the brothers hit them.

Joel went through the first three like a blade through wet cloth. Pistol shots at point-blank range, axe in the backswing, never slowing. Christer pivoted left, cutting down two gunners before they could bring heavier weapons to bear. Daniel dropped a shock charge into a side passage, sealing reinforcements behind a collapsing spray of sparks. Anton simply hit the center like an orbital strike.

A hulking Garooth brute swung a chain cleaver at him.

Anton caught the haft in his clawed gauntlet, tore it free, and headbutted the creature hard enough to break its skull casing. Another lunged from the flank. Anton split it from shoulder to hip and kept moving.

The interior alarms changed.

A deeper roar rolled through the ship.

Christer checked the local command bursts.

“That got attention.”

“Good,” Anton said.

They carved forward through gun decks and cargo trenches turned into barracks. Everywhere the flagship was crude mass and armored brutality. Bodies piled behind them. The Talyrn breach teams following in their wake stared at the speed of it.

Then the bulkhead ahead split open.

Something enormous came through.

The war boss.

It wore stolen power armor fused with industrial exoskeleton parts and ship plating hammered into place. Its shoulders were wider than Anton’s. One arm ended in a rotary cannon. The other carried a giant cleaver crackling with unstable power.

Anton stepped forward before Joel spoke.

“Mine.”

The war boss bellowed something in its own tongue and slammed the cleaver against the deck.

Anton pointed his blade.

“I am Anton von Hjertstedt.”

He spread his arms slightly, red optics burning.

“Come on, then.”

The war boss charged.

---

## 8. Let Him Have It

The war boss hit Anton like a vehicle.

The impact drove both of them through a bulkhead.

Metal folded.

Cables snapped.

The deck shook hard enough that one of the Talyrn boarders behind them stumbled.

Anton disappeared into smoke.

Then his laughter came through the squad channel.

“Good.”

Joel did not even turn.

“He is fine.”

Christer looked at the hole in the wall.

“That definition is becoming generous.”

Another impact sounded.

Then a roar.

Then Anton flew back through the same hole, struck the opposite wall, and landed on one knee.

His left shoulder plate was split.

One gauntlet sparked.

Anton stood.

The war boss emerged behind him.

It was enormous.

Its armored frame filled most of the corridor, scrap plate bolted over powered musculature. The rotary cannon on one arm was mangled from the first exchange, but the cleaver remained intact.

Anton raised his blade.

The war boss charged again.

Behind Joel, the Talyrn cutter commander opened the channel.

“Boarding element, we are advancing to support.”

Joel stopped.

“No.”

A pause.

“Repeat?”

“Return to your cutter.”

The Talyrn officer clearly thought translation had failed.

“You are engaged with the enemy command caste.”

“Yes.”

“We have intervention personnel ready.”

“I know.”

“Then we are coming in.”

Joel turned toward the Talyrn breach team.

“No.”

The word carried differently this time.

Even without House command authority over them, the tone made several Talyrn soldiers stop.

Joel pointed back toward the breach.

“Your cutter is exposed.”

The commander answered:

“We can secure this section.”

“You can secure your ship.”

Another shockwave rolled through the corridor as Anton and the war boss collided again.

Joel continued.

“This is your defensive campaign. Your people need that cutter more than we do.”

“You are four men inside an enemy capital ship.”

Daniel glanced at Joel.

“He has a point.”

Joel ignored him.

The Talyrn commander tried again.

“If you are lost—”

“You lose four strangers.”

Joel pointed toward the distant battle outside.

“If that cutter is lost, you lose trained crew, medical capacity, extraction capability, and another hull your fleet is already short of.”

That stopped the argument.

The Talyrn culture understood recovery.

Redundancy.

Not wasting useful people for prestige.

Joel had understood that quickly.

“Go,” he said.

The commander hesitated.

Then:

“Under protest.”

Joel nodded.

“Accepted.”

The Talyrn boarders withdrew.

The cutter detached.

Anton saw the tactical marker moving away.

“You sent them back?”

“Yes.”

“I liked them.”

“They were trying to help.”

“That was their mistake.”

The war boss swung.

Anton caught the cleaver between both powered gauntlets.

Servos screamed.

The blade stopped centimeters from his helmet.

The war boss pushed.

Anton’s boots slid backward through blood and debris.

Then he twisted.

One gauntlet locked around the weapon haft.

The other punched into the war boss’s side.

Once.

Twice.

The third strike dented armor.

The fourth tore through it.

The war boss roared and headbutted Anton.

Anton staggered.

The cleaver came down.

He barely moved aside.

It struck the deck and cut through plating.

Anton drove his knee into the war boss’s chest.

Nothing.

The war boss laughed.

Anton paused.

Then laughed back.

Over the squad channel, Daniel muttered:

“That is concerning.”

Christer raised his rifle toward incoming Garooth reinforcements.

“Focus.”

Joel, Christer, and Daniel formed around the duel.

They were not watching.

They were protecting it.

Garooth infantry poured into the adjoining passages, drawn by the war boss’s challenge.

Joel met them first.

His command armor moved with frightening economy.

No wasted motion.

Heavy pistol.

Axe.

Short bursts.

Close cuts.

A charging brute lost one knee, then its throat.

Another raised a weapon.

Christer killed it before it fired.

Daniel dropped a breaching charge against a side junction and detonated it as a larger mob approached.

The passage collapsed inward.

“Left sealed.”

Joel nodded.

“Good.”

Christer checked the local tactical feed.

“They are concentrating on us.”

Joel answered:

“That is useful.”

Daniel looked at him.

“For whom?”

Joel pointed upward.

“The Talyrn.”

The flagship’s boarding reserves were being pulled inward.

Away from the external battle.

Away from the Talyrn line.

Every Garooth fighter coming to kill four brothers was one less hitting the defensive formations outside.

Joel smiled.

“Let them come.”

Anton lost his sword.

The war boss knocked it aside and buried the cleaver into Anton’s chest plate.

The impact cracked the outer layer.

Anton caught the war boss’s arm.

The two locked together.

Power armor against scavenged industrial exoskeleton.

The deck beneath them began to buckle.

The war boss growled something.

Anton’s translator rendered it badly:

SMALL KING. BREAK.

Anton stared into its face.

“Small?”

He slammed his helmet into the war boss’s jaw.

Again.

Again.

Blood sprayed across his visor.

Then Anton ripped one of the enemy’s shoulder armor plates free and used it as a weapon.

The war boss staggered.

Anton drove the broken plate edge into the damaged side armor.

The war boss grabbed him by the neck and lifted him.

Christer half-turned.

Joel barked:

“No.”

Christer stopped.

Anton’s legs hung above the deck.

The war boss squeezed.

Anton’s collar armor compressed.

Warning lights flashed across his visor.

Then Anton reached behind his own back.

Found the emergency breaching spike clipped to his armor.

And drove it directly through the war boss’s forearm.

The grip opened.

Anton dropped.

He seized the hanging arm.

Twisted.

Powered joints screamed.

The war boss tried to pull away.

Anton did not let it.

He broke the arm at the elbow.

The roar filled the corridor.

Anton picked up his sword.

Joel checked the tactical map.

The Talyrn cutter was almost clear.

Good.

He switched channels.

“Cutter One, confirm withdrawal.”

The commander answered immediately.

“Clear of flagship. Returning to formation.”

“Good.”

A pause.

Then the Talyrn officer added:

“You are aware we can still come back.”

Joel watched Anton advance toward the wounded war boss.

“No need.”

Anton raised his blade.

The war boss raised its remaining arm.

Joel smiled faintly.

“My brother is having fun.”

He cut the channel.

Anton charged.

This time the war boss was slower.

The first strike took its weapon hand.

The second opened the chest armor.

The third drove it backward against a machinery column.

Anton slammed one gauntlet around its throat.

The war boss still tried to bite him.

Anton laughed.

“Better.”

Then he drove the sword upward beneath the jaw and into the skull.

The war boss went rigid.

Anton held it there for several seconds.

Then let the corpse fall.

Silence.

Only for a moment.

Because across the flagship, Garooth command signals began fragmenting.

Christer looked at his display.

“There.”

Daniel checked the external battle.

“Formation disruption.”

Joel watched the Garooth fleet begin to lose coordination around the capital ship.

Not collapse.

Not yet.

But wobble.

That was enough.

Anton pulled his sword free.

His armor was ruined across the chest and shoulder.

Blood ran down one side of his face inside the helmet.

He looked at Joel.

“Worth it.”

Joel nodded toward the deeper command decks.

“Good.”

Anton picked up the war boss’s broken cleaver.

“What now?”

Joel turned toward the heart of the ship.

“Now we take the rest of it.”

---

## 9. Red Authorization

The war boss hit the deck and stayed there.

For half a second, the corridor went quiet.

Not silent.

The flagship still groaned around them. Distant guns thundered through the hull. Somewhere farther aft, something large was burning. But inside that section, the Garooth had just lost the center of its will.

Christer checked the command burst traffic.

“Confirmed disruption.”

Daniel pulled up the external battle feed.

“The Talyrn line is pushing.”

Anton planted one boot on the dead war boss and yanked his sword free.

“Good.”

Joel was already moving.

“Bridge.”

The four brothers advanced deeper into the flagship while dead Garooth infantry, shattered bulkheads, and burning ammunition lined the passage behind them.

What remained of the defenders came in broken clusters now.

Not coordinated.

Angry.

Joel cut down the first two with pistol and axe. Christer shot a third through the throat. Daniel blew open a sealed blast door and let Anton hit the next chamber first.

It was not a fight anymore.

It was clearance.

They crossed a gun deck where wounded Garooth crew were trying to turn a macro-battery manually. Anton killed the loaders. Joel shot the gun captain. Christer marked the weapon as neutralized and kept moving.

Then they reached command.

The bridge was larger than expected, built inside the armored skull of the giant vessel. Crude thrones, command cages, slave-linked fire controls, and welded tactical displays filled the space. Garooth officers turned when the brothers entered, brick-red faces split by broad jaws and old burn scars. One laughed as he dragged a rotary gun around, heedless of the officers standing in its path.

They died quickly.

One tried to trigger a fleet broadcast.

Christer shot his hand off.

Another reached for a detonator.

Daniel buried a blade in its neck.

Anton physically lifted the largest by the chest armor and threw it through the primary command pit.

Joel stepped over the body and looked out through the forward iron viewport.

Beyond the shattered debris and gunfire, another Garooth capital was burning toward the fight.

Larger engines.

Heavier escort cluster.

Signal density higher.

Christer saw it too.

“There.”

Daniel checked the traffic patterns.

“New command concentration.”

Anton smiled beneath the blood on his visor.

“The next one.”

The second capital ship sat just beyond the Talyrn pressure line, rallying nearby hulks and assault carriers around itself. Even wounded Garooth formations were bending toward it.

A successor command node.

A new war boss.

Joel understood immediately.

“If that ship stabilizes them, the battle drags on.”

Christer nodded.

“Yes.”

Daniel looked around the bridge.

“You are thinking something terrible.”

Anton looked delighted.

“I know that face.”

Joel studied the crippled flagship beneath them. Drives damaged, not dead. Forward mass enormous. Armor thick enough to survive one deliberate impact if properly aligned.

He pointed at the external plot.

“We ram it.”

Daniel closed his eyes.

“Of course.”

Anton laughed.

“Yes.”

Christer was already calculating.

“Distance is acceptable.”

“Closure rate?”

“High if we reactivate the forward drives and dump everything into acceleration.”

Daniel checked engineering from the captured bridge.

“Reactor containment is unstable.”

Joel looked at him.

“Will it hold?”

Daniel considered.

“For one bad decision?”

Joel nodded.

“Yes.”

“It will hold for exactly one.”

The Talyrn called again.

Their boarding cutter commander appeared first, then the senior fleet officer from before.

“You have taken command of the enemy flagship?”

Joel answered:

“Yes.”

The officer looked past him at the bridge full of bodies.

“We can see that.”

Christer patched the tactical view through.

The second Garooth capital expanded on the shared display.

Joel said:

“We are going to ram that ship.”

Silence.

Not because the Talyrn disliked the idea.

Because they had immediately understood it.

The officer asked:

“Can you steer?”

Daniel answered:

“Steer is an ambitious word.”

“Can you hit?”

Anton leaned over the command pit.

“Yes.”

The Talyrn officer watched the secondary capital gathering ships around it.

Then asked:

“What do you need?”

Joel looked at the closing battlespace.

“Open the lane.”

The officer nodded once.

“Approved.”

Daniel took engineering control.

Christer seized navigation and target alignment.

Joel opened the flagship-wide internal broadcast.

Every surviving Garooth voice channel screamed with overlapping rage.

He did not understand most of it.

That was fine.

He switched to Talyrn and general band instead.

“Enemy capital ship under hostile capture.”

“Clear its forward path.”

Anton looked toward him.

“That is very polite.”

Joel holstered the pistol and rolled his shoulders.

“Now for the rest.”

He opened the private House channel.

“All brothers. Prepare limiter release.”

No one joked.

Not even Anton.

The last time they had done it at this level, Ardent had bled for it.

Joel placed his gauntlet on the blood-smeared command console.

“Authorization: High Marshal Joel von Hjertstedt.”

The bridge recognized nothing.

Daniel routed it through their armor control net instead.

Four acknowledgments blinked green.

Joel spoke the final words.

“Limiter removal approved.”

The world changed.

Inside each suit, safeties released. Hormonal governors fell away. neural dampers unlocked. Combat processors shifted from protected restraint to full war output.

Crimson flooded their optics.

Not symbolically.

Literally.

Four pairs of eyes began to scream red behind their visors.

The surviving Garooth crew on lower decks probably never understood why the intruders had just become faster.

The first counterattack hit from the aft bridge doors.

Too late.

Anton met it with the dead war boss’s cleaver in one hand and his own sword in the other. The first brute split open from shoulder to hip. The second lost both legs. Joel hit the center, axe biting through armor seams with ugly efficiency. Christer shot past them, placing rounds exactly where exposed plating opened under motion. Daniel set the bridge charges in case the command pit needed to be denied later, then joined the line with blade and rifle both working.

They killed everything that entered.

Fast.

Brutally.

Without pause.

Meanwhile, the flagship accelerated.

Daniel shouted from the engineering feed:

“Drives responding!”

Christer checked the vector.

“Talyrn line is opening.”

On the forward display, disciplined Talyrn formations shifted just enough to clear a corridor. Damaged ships were still protected. Rescue craft still moved. But a lane had been cut through the chaos.

Straight toward the second capital.

The next war boss had noticed.

Heavy guns from the target ship began traversing toward them.

Anton looked up at the impact alarms.

“Can we go faster?”

Daniel answered:

“No.”

Then, after a beat:

“I can make it louder.”

The damaged flagship lunged forward.

Gunfire tore into its flanks. Armor peeled away. One engine tower exploded. Another kept burning. The bridge shook so hard Christer had to lock his boots to the deck.

Range closed.

The second capital filled the viewport.

Joel looked at his brothers.

“Ready?”

Anton’s red eyes burned.

“Yes.”

Christer checked boarding approach geometry.

“Collision will open at least three viable breach routes.”

Daniel grinned despite himself.

“I hate how good this plan is.”

The next war boss’s ship fired again.

Too late.

The captured flagship slammed into it like a falling city.

The bridge disappeared in white metal and screaming force.

---

## 10. The Line Surges

For several seconds after impact, nobody in the Talyrn fleet spoke.

The captured Garooth flagship had crossed their entire defensive line under fire, accelerated directly into the enemy command formation—

and rammed another capital ship.

The collision appeared across hundreds of tactical displays at once.
