Two mountains of scrap and armor folded into each other.

Weapons towers broke free.

Engine sections tore apart.

Entire internal decks opened to vacuum.

Then the tactical net began updating.

Garooth COMMAND COHERENCE: COLLAPSING

SECONDARY FORMATIONS: DISRUPTED

BOARDING WAVES: LOSING COORDINATION

The Talyrn fleet commander stared at the display.

One of her officers finally whispered:

“They actually did it.”

Another answered:

“They said they were going to.”

“That does not make it reasonable.”

“No.”

A pause.

“It does make it accurate.”

The fleet commander recovered first.

“Exploit.”

Every Seythar command node received the intent simultaneously.

No dramatic flagship order was needed.

Across the battlefield, the Talyrn Continuance moved.

Damaged frigates withdrew behind fresh screens.

Destroyers accelerated through openings.

Cruisers redistributed command responsibilities.

Missile groups shifted targets from the now-crippled command hulks toward isolated Garooth assault carriers.

The entire formation changed shape.

Not like a line charging.

Like a machine discovering its opponent had suddenly lost half its gears.

“Forward groups, Fold Pattern.”

“Reserve formations advance.”

“Thyrak intervention carriers prepare assault.”

“Do not pursue isolated wrecks.”

“Break organized resistance first.”

The Talyrn fleet surged.

Garooth formations tried to answer.

Some did.

Most could not agree on who was commanding.

The death of the first war boss had already damaged their hierarchy.

Now another command capital had been physically impaled by the captured flagship.

Several war chiefs immediately began broadcasting competing orders.

One demanded everyone attack the Talyrn center.

Another ordered ships toward the wrecked capitals.

A third simply declared himself supreme commander and fired on a rival cruiser that refused to acknowledge him.

The Talyrn tactical officer stared.

“They are fighting each other.”

The commander answered:

“Good.”

“Do we intervene?”

“No.”

She pointed elsewhere.

“Kill the ones still fighting us.”

Then images from the boarding action reached the Talyrn command net.

The footage came from the cutter Joel had ordered away.

Four armored humans.

Different armor.

Different fighting styles.

All bearing the same underlying House design language.

Joel in disciplined command plate, black and iron, crimson optics burning.

Christer in sensor-heavy tactical armor, moving with calculated precision.

Daniel in reinforced engineer-combat plate, carrying breaching systems alongside weapons.

Anton—

The recording paused automatically when analysts identified him standing over the first war boss.

His assault armor was heavily damaged.

Chest plating split.

Shoulder almost destroyed.

Blood visible beneath the helmet seal.

The dead war boss beneath him was significantly larger.

A Talyrn intelligence officer stared.

“That one challenged the war chief personally.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

Nobody answered.

Then the footage continued.

Anton laughing.

The fleet commander slowly rubbed her forehead.

Another analyst brought up armor telemetry intercepted during the final approach.

“Commander.”

“What?”

“We detected a major physiological change immediately before the ram.”

The commander turned.

“Seraph contamination?”

“No.”

That brought everyone’s attention.

“Human.”

“What changed?”

“Neural response.”

“Muscle output.”

“Reaction timing.”

“Pain regulation.”

“All four simultaneously.”

The analyst enlarged the data.

“And their eyes.”

The boarding footage froze.

Four faces briefly visible through damaged helmets.

Red.

Violently red.

Not lighting.

Biological.

The Talyrn medical officer stepped closer.

“They removed internal safety restrictions.”

The commander frowned.

“Voluntarily?”

“It appears so.”

“For combat?”

“Yes.”

“That is extraordinarily dangerous.”

The medical officer watched Joel cleave through another Garooth fighter.

“Yes.”

A Thyrak commander standing nearby said quietly:

“They knew.”

Everyone looked toward him.

He pointed toward the footage.

“Movement compensations.”

“They know exactly what unrestricted output does to their bodies.”

He watched Anton hit the war boss again.

“And they did it anyway.”

That earned a different kind of silence.

The Talyrn understood choosing personal risk to preserve a formation.

Perhaps too well.

Outside, the battle was turning.

A Talyrn destroyer formation smashed through the Garooth missile line.

Three frigates formed around a crippled cruiser while its surviving guns continued firing through them.

Thyrak assault carriers advanced behind the breakthrough.

Boarding craft launched.

Not toward random prizes.

Toward command hulks.

Engine control ships.

Mobile scrap forges.

The Garooth fleet began coming apart structurally.

A Talyrn captain reported:

“Enemy center folding.”

Another:

“Left warband disengaging.”

Another:

“Three capital hulks attempting independent withdrawal.”

The fleet commander studied them.

“Let them separate.”

Her deputy understood immediately.

“Then destroy them individually.”

“Yes.”

The Garooth had come expecting one giant brawl.

Instead, the Continuance began dissecting it.

Then a signal came from the wrecked capital ships.

Weak.

Distorted.

Human.

“Talyrn command…”

Static.

The commander leaned forward.

“Identify.”

Joel appeared through broken transmission.

Half his helmet was gone.

His eyes still burned crimson.

Blood ran from his nose.

Behind him, Anton was trying to pull a bent bulkhead open manually while Christer and Daniel worked beside him.

Joel looked terrible.

Calm.

But terrible.

“Your opening looked useful.”

The Talyrn commander stared at him.

“You rammed two capital ships together.”

“Yes.”

“You boarded one with four people.”

“Yes.”

“You ordered our extraction cutter away.”

“Yes.”

“You then deliberately disabled your own biological safety systems.”

Joel paused.

“That one is complicated.”

Anton shouted from behind him:

“Tell them it was fun!”

The entire Talyrn command chamber heard it.

Joel closed his eyes briefly.

The Talyrn commander stared at the screen.

Then, against all expectation, laughed once.

Short.

Disbelieving.

“You people are insane.”

Joel smiled faintly.

“We have been told.”

She looked toward the tactical display.

The Garooth center was collapsing.

Talyrn forces were advancing everywhere.

“Your insanity was strategically useful.”

Joel nodded.

“We aim for useful.”

“Can you extract?”

Joel looked behind him.

Daniel answered loudly:

“We are working on that.”

The commander immediately turned.

“Thyrak recovery group.”

The augmented commander stepped forward.

“Ready.”

“Bring them back.”

Joel heard.

“No heroics.”

The Thyrak commander looked directly into the transmission.

“That instruction seems unusually hypocritical.”

Anton started laughing again.

For the first time since the battle began—

so did several people on the Talyrn bridge.

The Continuance continued advancing.

And somewhere inside two fused Garooth capitals, four strangers had just become impossible for them to ignore.

---

## 11. Recovery and Questions

The Thyrak reached them through the wreck.

Not around it.

Through it.

Their boarding cutter latched onto the fused capital ships while both hulks were still venting atmosphere and shedding burning armor into space.

The first Thyrak through the breach was enormous.

Not Anton enormous.

Different.

Dense Orun-Khal armor wrapped around a heavily augmented human frame, built less like ceremonial power armor and more like something designed to survive a station collapsing around it.

Three more followed.

One carried a medical pack.

One carried cutting equipment.

The last carried a weapon large enough that Daniel stared at it despite himself.

Anton noticed.

“You like that?”

Daniel ignored him.

The Thyrak commander looked at the four brothers.

Then at the dead Garooth surrounding them.

Then at the fused wreck.

“You were instructed to remain recoverable.”

Joel leaned against a broken support.

“We remained recoverable.”

The commander stared at him.

“That interpretation is offensive.”

The first medical scan immediately became a problem.

The Thyrak medic scanned Joel.

Stopped.

Scanned again.

“Your cardiac load is extreme.”

Joel nodded.

“Expected.”

“Neuromuscular microtearing.”

“Yes.”

“Joint damage.”

“Yes.”

“Possible retinal vascular injury.”

“Probably.”

The medic looked toward Anton.

Anton’s scan was worse.

The Thyrak medic stared at the results.

“You fought in this condition?”

Anton shrugged.

“It happened during.”

The medic looked at Joel.

“You authorized this?”

“Yes.”

The commander’s helmet turned slowly toward him.

“You deliberately removed physiological protection systems from all four of you.”

Joel pushed away from the wall.

“Temporary combat limiter release.”

“That is not a reassuring name.”

“It is not intended to be.”

Christer’s hands had begun shaking now that the combat state was fading.

Daniel had blood running from one ear.

Anton’s chest plate was split badly enough that the medic could see bruising beneath the undersuit.

The Thyrak understood augmentation well enough to recognize what the brothers had done to themselves.

“You disabled your own protection systems to board one capital and ram another,” the commander said.

Joel nodded.

The commander paused.

“You are difficult people to recover.”

Anton smiled.

“We hear that often.”

The Thyrak did not allow them to walk out.

That became a separate argument.

Joel looked at Daniel’s bloodied ear and Christer’s shaking hands. He stopped arguing first.

“All four of us,” he said.

Anton protested until Joel held his gaze. Then the recovery team loaded them onto stretchers and moved them back through the cutter.

Daniel looked toward Joel.

“This is humiliating.”

Joel answered:

“Quiet.”

Christer smiled weakly.

“You approved the limiter release.”

“That does not mean I approved stretchers.”

The Thyrak commander heard them.

“You are all staying on them.”

Anton tried to sit up.

The restraint automatically tightened.

He stared at it.

Then at the commander.

“I respect this device.”

The Talyrn medical carrier received them under full command-integrity protocol.

Weapons removed.

Armor opened.

Bloodwork.

Neurological scans.

Identity verification.

Repeated authentication.

Joel noticed everything.

So did Christer.

They were not being treated as prisoners.

They were being treated as recovered commanders.

Authority was not assumed merely because someone survived.

The Talyrn verified identity first.

Only then did they begin the formal debrief.

The chamber was plain.

No banners.

No ceremonial throne.

Joel sat in a medical support chair.

Anton hesitated beside his own chair.

“Annette will read the medical report,” Christer said.

The Talyrn had no idea who she was. Anton sat.

Christer and Daniel remained under monitoring.

Across from them sat the fleet commander from the battle, the Thyrak commander, two intelligence officers, one civilian official, and a legal observer.

The fleet commander began.

“State your identities.”

Joel considered lying.

Then decided the time for that had passed.

“Joel von Hjertstedt.”

One intelligence officer froze.

Anton spoke next.

“Anton von Hjertstedt.”

Then:

“Christer von Hjertstedt.”

“Daniel von Hjertstedt.”

The Talyrn intelligence staff exchanged looks.

The names meant nothing historically.

But the titles attached to the damaged identity files they had recovered from the Wayward Hound did.

The officer read slowly.

“High Marshal.”

Joel nodded.

“Breach Commander.”

Anton smiled.

“Senior Banner command.”

Christer nodded.

“Strategic engineering command.”

Daniel sighed.

“Yes.”

The civilian official stared at all four.

“You are senior military leadership.”

“Yes.”

“And you were traveling aboard that?”

She pointed toward an image of the Wayward Hound.

Its faded blue stripe looked especially bad under Talyrn imaging.

Joel nodded.

“Yes.”

“Why?”

Anton answered:

“Sightseeing.”

Nobody reacted.

Anton looked disappointed.

The fleet commander leaned forward.

“What polity do you represent?”

“House von Hjertstedt.”

“A state?”

“A hereditary sovereign House and a mercenary military order,” Joel said. “We hold territory in some regions. Elsewhere we work by contract.”

The legal observer frowned.

“And here?”

“Guests. We have no authority over your people.”

The Talyrn civilian official looked increasingly disturbed.

“One family controls strategic fleets?”

“Yes.”

“How is that legitimate?”

Joel smiled faintly.

“There it is.”

The fleet commander frowned.

“What?”

“The real question.”

The Talyrn official did not soften.

“Who holds you accountable?”

Joel answered immediately.

“Our Charter.”

“Your own law.”

“Yes.”

“Your own Justicars.”

“Yes.”

“Your own courts.”

“Yes.”

“That is circular.”

Joel nodded.

“It can be.”

That answer surprised them.

Anton looked toward him.

Joel continued.

“If we abuse power, legitimacy collapses.”

“If our contracts stop being trusted, our influence dies.”

“If civilians begin seeing our banners as predation instead of protection, the House fails even if the fleets survive.”

The legal observer studied him carefully.

“That is an unstable system.”

Joel let the objection stand.

“You should judge what we do here. Not what I promise you.”

Then the Talyrn asked about the Known Reach.

How many human powers?

How many populated systems?

Who controlled the gates?

Who claimed authority over humanity?

Joel answered that one carefully.

“No one.”

The room went still.

The civilian official looked troubled.

“No central human defense authority?”

“No.”

“No unified command?”

“No.”

“No common gate authority?”

“No.”

The fleet commander sat back.

“Then who responds when humanity faces a civilizational threat?”

Joel thought of Ardent.

The Seraph fleet.

Imperium ships burning beside Zenith vessels.

House banners holding the wall.

“Whoever gets there.”

That answer disturbed the Talyrn more than anything else.

Their civilization had been built around the belief that no one should stand alone.

The wider human sphere apparently did exactly that as standard practice.

The Thyrak commander finally asked:

“And the Seraph?”

Every brother became still.

Joel looked directly at him.

“You know them?”

The commander’s expression changed.

“Yes.”

Now the debrief belonged to both sides.

---

## 12. Different Answers to the Same Nightmare

The debrief changed the moment the Thyrak commander said:

“Yes.”

Joel leaned forward despite the medical brace across his chest.

“You have fought the Seraph.”

The Talyrn fleet commander nodded.

“Not at the scale you are implying.”

“How much?”

“Isolated incursions. Compromised crews. Captured commanders. Several attempted infiltration chains.”

Christer asked:

“Systems lost?”

The commander hesitated.

“One.”

That silenced the brothers.

Daniel looked toward the Talyrn officials.

“What happened?”

The civilian representative answered.

“We destroyed the gate connection.”

Anton’s expression changed.

“You cut off your own people.”

“Yes.”

“Population?”

The official did not answer immediately.

“Millions.”

Nobody joked after that.

Joel understood.

Every major human civilization had eventually faced the same Seraph equation.

A compromised world could become a weapon against everything beyond it.

The House had seen smaller states destroyed that way.

The Talyrn had learned independently.

And their solution had been just as brutal.

The Thyrak commander expanded a tactical record.

A human cruiser appeared.

Normal.

Friendly transponder.

Correct crew codes.

Then a second image.

Boarding teams entering what they believed was a damaged vessel.

Then silence.

“Recovered officers displayed no immediate behavioral abnormality,” he said.

“Forty-three hours later, three separate command nodes issued contradictory orders.”

Christer immediately understood.

“Decapitation.”

“Attempted.”

“But your command structure survived.”

“Yes.”

The commander brought up the Seythar architecture.

Authority distributed across validated nodes.

No single admiral could surrender a fleet.

No recovered commander simply resumed authority.

Authentication expired automatically after isolation.

Neurological integrity had to be proven.

Daniel stared at the system.

“So a recovered commander cannot authenticate his own return to command.”

“Yes.”

Anton smiled faintly.

“That must annoy them.”

The Thyrak commander almost smiled.

“It appears to.”

Joel opened the Ardent files.

Not all of them.

Enough.

PROJECT SHEPHERD.

Human transport records.

Manufactured custody orders.

Missing prisoners.

Experimental host classifications.

Compromised officials.

The Talyrn room became increasingly quiet.

Joel traced the chain from false custody warrants to the laboratories, the recovered elders and the Seraph invasion. He included the Interpreter and the gate records.

Then he showed Central, Bastion, and Gatehold under House banners.

The civilian Talyrn representative stared.

“You conquered the state.”

Joel corrected her.

“We excised it.”

“That distinction appears convenient.”

Annette would have liked her.

Joel continued.

“Ardent’s authority knowingly supplied people into a Seraph procurement network.”

“Entire government?”

“No.”

“Then you displaced everyone?”

“No.”

He showed civil service screening.

Courts reopening.

Food distribution.

Medical supply.

Infrastructure protection.

Local administrators retained after audit.

“No loyalty oath,” Joel said.

“No mass seizure of civilian property.”

“No purge of personnel simply for having worked under Ardent government.”

The Talyrn legal observer asked:

“But the strategic stations remain yours.”

“Yes.”

“Permanently?”

Joel paused.

“Until surrendering them no longer risks another Seraph gate.”

That answer did not satisfy everyone.

It was not supposed to.

The Talyrn fleet commander studied Kestrel next.

“This is also House territory?”

“Affiliated territory.”

Anton added:

“Complicated.”

The official ignored him.

He showed how Sera’s protection contract had grown into Kestrel’s affiliation, with local command retained and House engineers arriving later. Ardent and Kestrel now formed the House’s visible frontier.

The Talyrn civilian representative asked:

“Why expand now?”

Joel looked toward the Seraph files.

“Because eventually protecting a road means holding the gate.”

The Thyrak commander nodded slowly.

That logic they understood.

Perhaps too well.

Then the medical alarm sounded.

Not emergency.

Confusion.

A Talyrn physician entered the room carrying four scan tablets.

“You need to return to recovery.”

Anton looked annoyed.

“I feel better.”

“That is the problem.”

Joel frowned.

The physician brought up Anton’s original scans.

Extensive muscle tearing.

Ligament damage.

Bone stress.

Microvascular trauma.

Neurological overload.

Then the current scans.

The damage was still present.

But healing.

Too quickly.

Daniel leaned forward.

“How long?”

“Seven hours since admission.”

Christer studied the numbers.

“That is within House expectation.”

The Talyrn physician stared at him.

“No.”

“Yes.”

“No human physiology repairs this quickly.”

Anton smiled.

“Apparently ours does.”

The physician looked at Joel.

“You suffered damage that should require several weeks before unsupported movement.”

Joel stood.

Slowly.

Painfully.

But without assistance.

The entire Talyrn side of the room became silent.

The physician scanned him again.

“That is not medically reasonable.”

Joel looked at the reading.

“Probably not.”

Anton removed one of his stabilization braces.

The physician stepped forward.

“Do not—”

Anton rolled his shoulder. The joint moved farther than it should have, then caught.

His smile thinned. He let the physician refit the brace.

The Thyrak commander stared.

“You are augmented.”

Daniel answered:

“Yes.”

“To what degree?”

Christer glanced at the physicians.

“Give them what they need to treat us,” he told Joel. “The rest stays outside this briefing.”

The commander did not appreciate the answer.

Joel sat again.

“We are still human.”

“I did not suggest otherwise.”

“But?”

The commander looked at the recovery data.

“Your baseline biology does not behave like ordinary human baseline.”

The brothers exchanged glances.

That touched territory they did not discuss with outsiders.

The House bloodline was already unusual.

Military augmentation pushed it further.

Limiter release caused horrific damage precisely because their bodies could produce outputs normal humans never could.

And once the limits returned—

they recovered.

Fast.

Not instantly.

Not magically.

But disturbingly fast.

Anton looked at the Thyrak.

“How long would one of you take?”

The commander answered:

“Longer.”

Anton smiled.

That clearly bothered him.

The Seraph subject returned immediately.

The Talyrn intelligence officer looked at the medical data.

Then at Joel.

“You said the Seraph studied your family.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

Silence.

Joel chose his answer carefully.

“They have failed to convert members of our bloodline.”

The room changed.

The Talyrn commander leaned forward.

“Failed?”

“Repeatedly.”

“Why?”

“We do not fully know.”

He was not giving them everything he knew. The failed conversions were established; the rest was not for this room.

Christer added:

“The recovered trials record repeated failures.”

Daniel continued:

“They do not record a working explanation. That is what we can say from those files.”

Anton smiled without humor.

“They dislike us.”

The Talyrn civilian official looked between the four recovering brothers.

Fast healing.

Extreme augmentation tolerance.

Limiter release.

Seraph conversion failure.

A hereditary military House commanding hidden fleets beyond an unreachable home system.

Her expression became much more cautious.

The debrief had started with the Talyrn wondering whether House von Hjertstedt was legitimate.

Now another question was forming.

What, exactly, had the Known Reach been hiding on the other side of Gate Seventeen?

Joel saw it in their faces.

He smiled slightly.

“Now you understand why introductions take time.”

---

## 13. The Veyr-Khal

The Veyr-Khal arrived twelve hours later.

By then, all four brothers were walking.

That created its own diplomatic problem.

The Talyrn physicians had expected Joel to remain under assisted recovery for days.

Instead, he entered the meeting chamber under his own power.

Anton followed with his damaged shoulder immobilized beneath a temporary brace.

Christer still moved stiffly.

Daniel had stopped bleeding from the ear and was already complaining about not being allowed near the Wayward Hound.

The Talyrn medical delegation watched them enter.

One physician whispered:

“That is unreasonable.”

Anton heard him.

“Yes.”

Joel gave him a look.

Anton stopped smiling.

Mostly.

The Veyr-Khal waited alone at the far end of the chamber.

No ceremonial guard.

No throne.

No display of wealth.

Only an older woman wearing dark military clothing beneath a light armored command mantle.

Tall.

Grey hair cut close.

One artificial eye.

Both hands visibly scarred.

Her name translated approximately as:

Veyr-Khal Ortheva Sair.

Supreme military coordinator of the Talyrn Continuance.

She studied the four brothers before speaking.

“High Marshal.”

“Veyr-Khal.”

Joel stopped opposite her.

Ortheva looked toward Anton.

“The one who challenged the war boss.”

Anton nodded.

“That description is acceptable.”

Then Christer.

“Distributed tactical analysis identified your sensor work during the boarding.”

Christer inclined his head.

Then Daniel.

“You redirected a hostile capital ship’s engineering network while its reactor containment was failing.”

Daniel answered:

“It was poorly maintained.”

Ortheva stared.

“It was an Garooth flagship.”

“Yes.”

“That is assumed.”

Finally she looked back toward Joel.

“You ordered four senior officers into an enemy capital vessel.”

Joel corrected her.

“Three.”

Her eyebrow moved slightly.

“I ordered myself.”

Ortheva considered that.

“I am uncertain whether that improves the decision.”

“It does not.”

For the first time, she almost smiled.

The tactical table activated.

Ardent appeared.

Then Kestrel.

Then Gate Seventeen.

Ortheva pointed toward Ardent.

“You seized this state following Seraph involvement.”

“Yes.”

“You retained its strategic stations.”

“Yes.”

“You are expanding their defenses.”

“Yes.”

“You also maintain another affiliated territory here.”

Kestrel expanded.

“Yes.”

“And beyond all of this lies your hidden homeland.”

Joel did not answer.

Ortheva noticed.

“You protect its location.”

“I protect access.”

“There is a difference?”

“Yes.”

She accepted that without pressing.

Talyrn officers understood protected infrastructure.

Then she asked the real question.

“What happens when Gate Seventeen becomes stable?”

Silence.

Anton looked toward Joel.

Christer remained still.

Daniel studied the gate projection.

Joel answered:

“That depends on you.”

Ortheva's artificial eye narrowed.

“Explain.”

“You are not a lost colony.”

“No.”

“You are not asking to be rescued.”

“No.”

“You are a sovereign civilization.”

“Yes.”

“Then we do not cross your gate with fleets because we discovered you exist.”

That answer clearly surprised her.

Ortheva asked:

“What if the Throne Imperium demands access?”

“No.”

“The Hiigaran Restoration?”

“No.”

“Terra?”

“No.”

“What gives you the authority to deny them?”

Joel smiled faintly.

“Nothing.”

A pause.

“I simply will not give them the route.”

Ortheva stared at him for several seconds.

“That is an unusual diplomatic position.”

“It has worked for Vintergard.”

Anton smiled.

Daniel muttered:

“Do not encourage him.”

Ortheva ignored them.

“And House von Hjertstedt?”

Joel looked toward Gate Seventeen.

“If you invite us, we come.”

“If you do not, we remain outside.”

“And if another Garooth convergence attacks?”

Joel answered immediately.

“We ask.”

Ortheva's expression hardened.

“And if we cannot answer?”

That was more difficult.

Joel considered carefully.

“Then we decide whether waiting for permission means watching humans die.”

There.

The fault line.

Ortheva nodded slowly.

“The Talyrn would likely make the same decision.”

“I suspected that.”

“Which should concern both of us.”

“Yes.”

She changed the display.

Footage from the battle appeared.

Talyrn ships covering damaged formations.

The captured Garooth flagship ramming the second capital.

The Continuance counterattack.

Ortheva pointed toward the moment Joel ordered the Talyrn cutter away.

“My officers found this decision interesting.”

Anton leaned toward Christer.

“That is diplomatic language for stupid.”

Christer whispered:

“Quiet.”

Ortheva continued.

“You refused recovery assistance because the cutter had greater value to the wider formation.”

“Yes.”

“You then risked four people whose strategic value, by your own political structure, is enormous.”

“Yes.”

“That appears contradictory.”

Joel shook his head.

“No.”

“Explain.”

“The cutter belonged to your war.”

“And you?”

“We chose to enter it.”

The room went quiet.

Joel continued.

“Your people did not owe us their lives because our names happen to matter somewhere else.”

Ortheva watched him carefully.

That answer landed directly inside Talyrn philosophy.

Strength created obligation.

It did not erase everyone else's value.

Then Ortheva opened the Seraph file.

Her demeanor changed completely.

“You claim the Seraph cannot successfully convert your bloodline.”

Joel answered:

“I claim they have repeatedly failed.”

“You know more than that.”

Joel said nothing.

Ortheva did not push.

Instead:

“If that resistance could be understood, it might save millions.”

Anton’s expression became colder.

Daniel stopped looking at the tactical display.

Christer watched Joel.

Joel answered carefully.

“Our family has been dissected, imprisoned, tested, and harvested by people trying to understand exactly that.”

Ortheva became still.

“Project Shepherd.”

“Yes.”

“Then I understand your caution.”

Joel nodded.

“We can cooperate.”

“Medical exchange?”

“Limited.”

“Seraph samples?”

“Yes.”

“Host-rejection data?”

“Case by case.”

“Your blood?”

“No.”

Immediate.

Absolute.

Ortheva accepted it.

“Fair.”

She dismissed the Seraph layer.

Gate Seventeen remained.

“I have one final question.”

Joel waited.

“Do you intend to return to the Known Reach?”

“Yes.”

“With knowledge of us?”

“Yes.”

“Will you report our existence?”

Joel looked toward his brothers.

Then back at her.

“To my House.”

“Only your House?”

“For now.”

Ortheva studied him.

“Why?”

Joel answered:

“Because the first thing the Known Reach will see when it learns about you is territory, fleets, gates, and leverage.”

He pointed toward the battle record.

“That is not what I saw first.”

Ortheva asked:

“What did you see?”

Joel looked toward the image of Talyrn frigates covering a crippled cruiser.

“A civilization that did not leave its wounded behind.”

Silence.

Then Ortheva extended her hand.

Joel took it.

“High Marshal.”

“Veyr-Khal.”

She looked toward Gate Seventeen.

“When the road opens again, perhaps we should decide together who gets to walk it.”

Joel nodded.

“That sounds reasonable.”

Anton sighed.

Everyone looked toward him.

“What?”

He gestured toward the two commanders.

“You are both being far too sensible.”

Ortheva finally smiled.

“I was told you were the difficult brother.”

Daniel answered before anyone else.

“No.”

All three pointed at Joel.

For the first time, the Veyr-Khal laughed.

---

## 14. The Dead Gate

Gate Seventeen did not stabilize naturally.

The brothers helped make sure of that.

Daniel spent most of the next two days inside the Talyrn gate-control complex with engineers who had maintained systems older than their civilization’s written records.

Christer worked beside the navigation teams.

Joel and Anton mostly stayed out of the machinery.

Mostly.

The problem was simple.

Gate Seventeen could now open.

That was also the problem.

If the route became reliably usable, sooner or later somebody else would find it.

A trader.

A spy.

A Garooth scout.

A Seraph-controlled vessel.

Or one of the major powers of the Known Reach.

Joel stood above the gate-control pit while Daniel displayed the reconstructed authentication architecture.

“We cannot make the gate disappear,” Daniel said.

The Veyr-Khal nodded.

“But we can make it useless.”

That got her attention.

The solution came from combining two traditions.

The Talyrn knew Gate Seventeen.

They understood its ancient internal systems, maintenance logic, and the Odrava-side approach.

The Hjertstedts understood something different.

How to make a functioning Varanthi route look dead.

Vintergard had survived precisely because access depended on more than coordinates. A ship needed the correct approach, valid credentials, and the correct sequence of authentication events.

Christer proposed adapting the same principle.

Not copying the Winter Road.

Never that.

Building something new.

A Gate Seventeen Key Chain.

Daniel projected the sequence.

“First layer: passive recognition.”

A vessel approaching normally would receive nothing.

No beacon.

No guidance.

No handshake.

The gate would appear inert.

Anton leaned over the railing.

“Dead.”

“Exactly.”

Christer continued.

“Second layer requires an authorized approach vector.”

Not merely coordinates.

The ship had to enter along a narrow, changing geometry.

Wrong approach?

Nothing.

The gate remained black.

“Third layer,” Daniel said, “is the key.”

But not a fixed key.

That was important.

A stolen authorization code could not be allowed to open the road forever.

The Talyrn engineers proposed rotating cryptographic credentials.

House experience added vessel identity.

Crew authority.

Time window.

Mission authorization.

A key would therefore belong not simply to a ship—

but to a specific ship, under specific authority, during a specific period.

The Veyr-Khal studied the design.

“And if someone captures the vessel?”

Joel answered:

“The key expires.”

Then Anton contributed.

Everyone became nervous.

“What if the wrong people keep trying?”

The senior Talyrn Gatewright looked at him.

“The gate refuses them.”

Anton shook his head.

“That tells them there is something worth refusing.”

Joel slowly smiled.

Daniel noticed.

“Oh no.”

Anton continued.

“Make it boring.”

The room became quiet.

Christer understood first.

“False failure states.”

Daniel turned toward the gate architecture.

“Yes.”

A vessel without correct authorization would not receive a dramatic rejection.

No warning.

No security challenge.

No indication the gate was protected.

Instead the structure would behave exactly like a dead Varanthi gate.

Cold.

Unresponsive.

Ancient.

Useless.

Its visible power signature would remain at background-maintenance levels.

Any active diagnostics would return corrupted or incomplete responses consistent with age and system failure.

The wrong people would conclude what everyone had concluded for generations:

Dead gate. No route. Nothing here.

The Veyr-Khal looked toward Anton.

“That was unexpectedly subtle.”

Anton looked offended.

“I can be subtle.”

All three brothers stared at him.

Anton sighed.

“Occasionally.”

Joel added one final requirement.

“Authorized ships must also be able to verify the gate before commitment.”

Daniel nodded.

They built an anticipatory handshake.

A valid vessel would transmit a low-power encrypted challenge well before entering the throat.

The gate would answer only inside a narrow authenticated sensor exchange.

Nothing visible to ordinary traffic.

Nothing strong enough to advertise activation.

The ship would know:

Gate alive.

Credential accepted.

Transit available.

Only then would the approach sequence begin.

Christer called it Silent Recognition.

The Talyrn preferred Veiled Passage.

Anton suggested Dead Man’s Door.

Nobody chose Anton's version.

Testing began with an old Talyrn courier.

First pass:

No key.

The courier approached Gate Seventeen.

Nothing happened.

No light.

No response.

Its sensors showed only a dormant ancient structure.

The pilot transmitted a standard gate inquiry.

Silence.

A second inquiry.

Silence.

A diagnostic sweep.

Fragmentary static.

The ship withdrew.

Perfect.

Then the courier returned.

This time with authorization.

At long range, it transmitted the encrypted challenge.

For five seconds—

nothing.

Then one tiny symbol appeared on the pilot's navigation display.

RECOGNIZED

No visible gate activity.

The courier entered the authorized vector.

A second confirmation arrived.

ROUTE HELD

Only when the vessel reached final commitment did the gate wake.

Light spread around the ancient ring.

The transit plane formed.

The courier vanished.

Thirty seconds later, confirmation arrived from the far side.

Successful.

The Talyrn control room erupted.

Not cheering.

Talyrn officers seemed constitutionally incapable of proper cheering.

But several engineers hugged.

That was close enough.

Then Daniel implemented the dangerous part.

Revocation.

Every authorized vessel could be removed individually.

Every key could expire.

Every access class could be suspended.

If command integrity became uncertain, all non-essential access could be killed instantly.

A Seraph-compromised ship with yesterday’s credentials would arrive tomorrow—

and find a dead gate.

A captured House ship?

Dead gate.

A stolen Talyrn courier?

Dead gate.

A fleet trying to brute-force its way through?

Dead gate.

The road existed only for those both sides chose to recognize.

Joel looked through the observation window toward Gate Seventeen.

“That is better.”

The Veyr-Khal joined him.

“You have effectively helped us hide an operational gateway.”

Joel shook his head.

“No.”

She looked toward him.

“We helped you control who knows it is operational.”

That distinction mattered.

The first outbound authorization was issued to the Wayward Hound.

Daniel stared at the ship record.

Then at the battered transport itself.

“You are seriously making this the first recognized vessel?”

Joel smiled.

“She found the road.”

Anton nodded.

“She deserves it.”

Christer looked at the faded blue stripe.

“She deserves paint.”

“No,” Joel and Anton said together.

The Veyr-Khal almost smiled.

A sealed diplomatic archive was loaded aboard.

Limited Talyrn contact protocols.

Gate rules.

Emergency recognition procedures.

And one hard restriction:

No Known Reach vessel would receive Gate Seventeen access without joint authorization.

For now, the Talyrn and House von Hjertstedt would both have to agree.

The gate would not become a trade route.

Not yet.

It would become something far more valuable.

A controlled door.

And to everyone else—

a ruin.

---

# Chapter 7: The Living First

## 1. The First Envoys

The Wayward Hound returned to Kestrel looking worse than when it had left.

That impressed people.

The battered transport limped into Kestrel Shield with mismatched armor, fresh burn scars, temporary Talyrn repairs, and one engine running slightly hotter than the other.

Mara Vale stared at it from command.

“What did they do?”

Sera stood beside her.

“Sightseeing.”

Mara looked at her.

“That word has lost all meaning.”

Then the passenger list arrived.

Joel.

Anton.

Christer.

Daniel.

Alive.

Followed by a sealed priority package for House command.

Sera immediately stopped smiling.

Lars reached Kestrel less than a day later.

He did not come alone.

Three elders traveled with him.

Elder Mikael Winge came first—old, broad-shouldered, carrying the quiet authority of a man whose family had served beside the Hjertstedts for generations.

Elder Ingrid Falken followed, silver-haired and severe, one of the House’s oldest legal and diplomatic voices.

The third was Elder Rasmus Skar, a former Banner commander whose damaged left eye had been replaced decades earlier and who still walked as though every corridor belonged to a warship.

Annette arrived separately from Ardent.

By the time Lars entered Kestrel’s secure strategic chamber, Joel had already prepared the first briefing.

The doors sealed.

No recording left the room.

Joel looked around the table.

Lars.

Annette.

Sera.

Mara.

The three elders.

Anton, Christer, Daniel.

Then he opened the Talyrn file.

THE TALYRN CONTINUANCE

Nobody spoke for several seconds.

Lars finally looked at Joel.

“You went sightseeing.”

Anton smiled.

“Yes.”

Lars looked at the image of two fused Garooth capital ships.

Then at Joel.

“Your definition is becoming hereditary.”

The briefing lasted four hours.

The Odrava.

The Talyrn.

Their distributed command systems.

Their Thyrak.

Their recovery culture.

The Garooth invasion.

Gate Seventeen.

The new authentication system.

The fact that the Talyrn knew the Seraph.

The fact that they had deliberately severed one of their own systems rather than risk wider contamination.

That drew the elders’ full attention.

Then Joel played the message from Veyr-Khal Ortheva Sair.

Her image appeared above the table.

Formal.

Controlled.

“To House von Hjertstedt: Gate Seventeen may open again under joint authorization. We are prepared to receive a limited diplomatic mission. Military transit beyond agreed escort strength will not be accepted.”

The message ended.

Lars leaned back.

“A limited mission.”

Annette nodded.

“Good.”

Anton looked disappointed.

“How limited?”

Ingrid Falken answered without looking at him.

“More limited if you keep asking.”

Lars brought up Gate Seventeen.

“No major fleet.”

Joel nodded.

“That would send the wrong message.”

“No Seraph Hunters.”

Annette agreed.

“No Black Guard formation beyond personal security.”

Mikael Winge looked toward Joel.

“You are going.”

“Yes.”

“Anton?”

“Yes.”

Ingrid sighed.

“Why?”

Anton answered:

“Cultural exchange.”

Nobody believed him.

Christer and Daniel were also included because both had worked directly with Talyrn officers and Gate Seventeen’s new access architecture.

Then Lars made the decision that changed the scale of the meeting.

“I am going too.”

Joel looked at him.

“You do not need to.”

“I know.”

“That makes it worse.”

Lars ignored him.

“This is first contact with an isolated human civilization that possesses functioning war fleets, unknown infrastructure, Seraph experience, and authenticated access to routes the Known Reach believed dead.”

He looked toward the elders.

“That is not a junior diplomatic assignment.”

Mikael Winge nodded.

“Agreed.”

Ingrid Falken:

“I am joining.”

Rasmus Skar gave a dry smile.

“I assumed that was why I was dragged here.”

Lars nodded.

“Then three elders.”

Joel looked toward Annette.

She shook her head.

“I remain in Ardent.”

That surprised him.

Annette continued.

“Someone must remain outside the mission with full authority if Gate Seventeen closes.”

Kira whispered:

“Statistically sensible.”

Lucy added:

“Also prevents the entire senior family from becoming trapped beyond an unstable gate.”

Anton muttered:

“Pessimistic machines.”

Lucy answered:

“Correct machines.”

The mission structure appeared.

One House diplomatic cruiser.

Two Vigil frigates.

One Sanctuary tender.

No capital ships.

No heavy assault formation.

No grand fleet demonstration.

The ships would approach Gate Seventeen openly, stop outside activation distance, transmit the joint authorization, and wait.

Only after Talyrn confirmation would the gate wake.

Sera studied the plan.

“This is the first time outsiders are deliberately going through?”

Joel nodded.

“From our side, yes.”

“And if someone follows?”

Daniel answered:

“They see a dead gate.”

“Even after you transit?”

“Yes.”

Christer added:

“The activation closes behind authorized traffic. No persistent beacon. No useful response to standard scans.”

Lars approved.

“Good.”

Then he looked at Joel.

“The Talyrn trust you enough to open the road.”

Joel shook his head.

“They trust the system enough to control it.”

Lars smiled faintly.

“Better.”

Three days later, the mission departed Kestrel.

No public announcement.

No diplomatic broadcast.

Officially, Lars was traveling to inspect frontier defenses.

Foreign intelligence noticed anyway.

They always did.

But nobody knew where the small House formation went after it left normal traffic.

Gate Seventeen appeared ahead.

Dark.

Ancient.

Dead.

The House formation stopped well outside the throat.

Lars stood on the bridge beside Joel.

Mikael Winge, Ingrid Falken, and Rasmus Skar watched from the command tier.

Anton stood near tactical.

Christer and Daniel monitored the authentication sequence.

Daniel transmitted the first challenge.

Nothing happened.

Ingrid looked toward him.

“Problem?”

“No.”

A second passed.

Then another.

One tiny encrypted symbol appeared.

RECOGNIZED

Christer smiled.

“Talyrn side has accepted us.”

The gate remained dark.

Then Daniel transmitted the final mission authorization.

For several more seconds, nothing changed.

Then ancient lights moved around the ring.

Slowly.

Deliberately.

The dead gate woke.

Lars watched the transit plane form.

“So this is the door.”

Joel nodded.

“To another human civilization.”

Anton smiled.

“And hopefully another war.”

Ingrid turned toward him.

“No.”

Anton looked at Lars.

Lars gave him the same answer.

“No.”

The fleet entered.

On the far side, the Talyrn were waiting.

Not hundreds of warships.

A formal escort.

Precise formations.

Broad, armored vessels holding disciplined separation.

No weapons targeted.

No ambiguity either.

A transmission arrived.

Veyr-Khal Ortheva Sair appeared.

Her artificial eye fixed immediately on Lars.

“Primarch Lars von Hjertstedt.”

Lars inclined his head.

“Veyr-Khal.”

Ortheva looked past him toward the elders.

Then Joel and his brothers.

Her expression shifted slightly when she saw Anton.

“You brought him back.”

Lars glanced toward his son.

“I was advised against it.”

Anton looked betrayed.

Ortheva almost smiled.

Then her voice became formal.

“House von Hjertstedt, welcome to the Odrava.”

Lars looked through the forward display at the unknown human ships waiting beyond the gate.

For the first time in generations, two human civilizations that had believed the road between them dead were meeting deliberately.

Not through war.

Not by accident.

At a table.

“Lead the way,” Lars said.

---

## 2. We Do Not Tolerate the Garooth

The Talyrn war map opened in layers.

At first, Lars watched in silence.

Seven inhabited systems.

Industrial moons.

Transit complexes.

Population centers.

Fleet yards.

Then the red markers appeared.

Hundreds.

Then thousands.

The battle Joel and his brothers had stumbled into appeared near Gate Seventeen.

A massive engagement.

Capital ships.

Fortified approaches.

Hundreds of Talyrn hulls.

A Garooth convergence large enough to have terrified most frontier states.

Then Veyr-Khal Ortheva Sair widened the map.

Their battle became almost insignificant.

Anton stopped smiling.

Four major fronts burned across the Odrava.

Garooth warbands were not raiding anymore.

They were moving inward.

System by system.

One captured industrial moon had become a scrap forge.

A civilian orbital yard now produced Garooth boarding craft.

An entire mining network had been stripped and converted into mobile supply infrastructure.

Behind the current fronts, still more clan formations were converging.

Christer stared at the movement tracks.

“This is migration.”

Ortheva nodded.

“Yes.”

Daniel studied the production estimates.

“They are eating your industrial base.”

“Yes.”

“And converting every loss into additional fleet mass.”

“Yes.”

Then Ortheva revealed the projection beyond the known warbands.

A moving concentration.

Huge.

Its true size uncertain.

Surrounded by forge hulks, captured yards, resource convoys, capital ships and entire lesser warbands following in its wake.

Rasmus Skar leaned forward.

“What is that?”

“We believe it is becoming their primary convergence.”

“How many ships?”

“We do not know.”

“How many Garooth?”

“We do not know.”

Then Ortheva gave them the final number.

Not ships.

Time.

“Without a major strategic change, our central industrial network begins failing within fourteen to twenty-two months.”

The chamber went silent.

Lars moved.

Immediately.

No council.

No deliberation.

No whispered conversation with the elders.

He turned toward Joel.

“Secure channel to Vintergard.”

Joel knew that voice.

So did Anton.

Anton slowly smiled again.

Ortheva looked at Lars.

“Primarch, we did not show you this to demand military—”

Lars raised one hand.

“Veyr-Khal.”

She stopped.

Lars looked at the red map.

At inhabited systems being eaten.

At people fighting a war the wider human sphere had not even known existed.

Then at the Garooth convergence.

“We do not tolerate the Garooth.”

The secure House system came alive.

It took several authentication layers before Vintergard answered.

The Crown of Winter appeared on the tactical display.

Annette stood in the command chamber.

Behind her were House officers, Justicars, fleet coordinators and logistics chiefs.

“Lars?”

“Full strategic alert.”

Annette's expression changed instantly.

“Against what?”

Lars transferred the Talyrn map.

For several seconds she said nothing.

Then Kira began processing.

Lucy followed.

Numbers filled the screen.

Known Garooth concentrations.

Industrial losses.

Probable fleet reproduction rates.

Talyrn defensive lines.

Gate Seventeen.

The moving convergence.

Annette looked back at Lars.

“How much?”

Lars answered:

“Everything we can move without weakening Vintergard below home-defense threshold.”

The room around Ortheva went completely still.

Joel looked sharply toward his father.

Even he had not expected that.

Annette asked:

“Clarify everything.”

Lars pointed toward Gate Seventeen.

“Anchor the gate.”

“Permanent House gate command?”

“Joint with the Talyrn.”

“Defenses?”

“Massive.”

“Fleet staging?”

“Yes.”

“Sanctuary?”

“Yes.”

“Engineering?”

“Yes.”

“Marine?”

“Yes.”

“Reserve Banners?”

“Yes.”

Annette paused.

“Capital assets?”

Lars looked at the Garooth convergence.

“All deployable assets appropriate to the theater.”

Anton whispered:

“Oh, this is going to be magnificent.”

Ingrid Falken looked toward Lars.

“The major powers will see the movements.”

“I know.”

“They will demand explanations.”

“I know.”

“The Hiigarans will assume we found another gate network.”

“Yes.”

“Terra will think we are beginning an expansion.”

“Yes.”

“The Imperium may mobilize.”

Lars finally looked toward her.

“Then they may scream from Sol while we kill Garooth.”

Ortheva stared at him.

“You are committing military forces before a treaty exists.”

Lars turned toward her.

“Yes.”

“You have not seen our full logistics picture.”

“Then show it to my logisticians when they arrive.”

“You do not know whether the Ordan will authorize foreign strategic forces.”

“Then place them at Gate Seventeen until it does.”

She frowned.

“You would move that much military power through a gate without certainty you can deploy it?”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

Lars pointed toward the destroyed Talyrn systems.

“Because those are human worlds.”

Then toward the Garooth lines.

“And those are Garooth.”

For Lars, that apparently completed the legal argument.

Vintergard began moving before the diplomatic chamber had finished processing what he had ordered.

Annette's screen populated with mobilization notices.

Reserve Banner readiness.

Capital-ship crews recalled.

Vigil formations rerouted.

Oath cruisers reassigned.

Sanctuary tenders activated.

Fuel reserves opened.

Marine formations placed on deployment status.

Engineering fleets recalled from non-critical contracts.

Gatewright teams assembled.

Mobile defense platforms prepared for transport.

Prefabricated fortress modules released from storage.

Joel stared at the accumulating force-state.

“This is going to expose a lot.”

Lars nodded.

“Yes.”

“Fleet depth.”

“Yes.”

“Mobilization speed.”

“Yes.”

“Industrial capacity.”

“Yes.”

Anton leaned toward Joel.

“You say that like it is bad.”

Joel ignored him.

Lars looked toward his sons.

“We spent generations making sure Vintergard could survive without the galaxy.”

Then he looked toward the Talyrn war map.

“That does not mean we watch another human civilization die because secrecy is convenient.”

Mikael Winge nodded slowly.

That was the end of the argument.

Daniel enlarged Gate Seventeen.

“If we anchor it properly, we can create a secure logistics throat.”

Christer followed.

“Talyrn-side staging outside the civilian traffic lanes.”

Joel added:

“Authentication remains joint.”

Lars nodded.

“No foreign access.”

“Known Reach powers?”

“Dead gate.”

“House ships?”

“Authorized.”

“Talyrn?”

“Authorized.”

“Anyone else?”

Lars looked toward Ortheva.

She understood.

“Nothing.”

Daniel smiled faintly.

“Then we build the door into a fortress.”

A new projection appeared.

The first House deployment estimate.

Even Rasmus Skar stopped speaking.

Not one fleet.

Not three.

A sustained expeditionary force.

Combat Banners.

Reserve formations.

Tenders.

Repair groups.

Fuel ships.

Marine carriers.

Gatewright vessels.

Engineering convoys.

Mobile fortifications.

The kind of deployment that did not merely reinforce a battle.

It created a front.

Ortheva looked at Lars.

“Your other human powers will believe you are preparing for war.”

Lars answered:

“They will be correct.”

“With whom?”

Lars pointed toward the Garooth convergence.

“With them.”

His eyes hardened.

“Anchor Gate Seventeen.”

He looked at Annette through the secure channel.

“Send everything.”

Then at Ortheva.

“When your government is ready, we discuss command boundaries.”

A pause.

“But the Garooth does not get fourteen months.”

Anton smiled.

Joel did too.

Because somewhere beyond the dead gate, Vintergard had just begun opening its arsenals.

And the Known Reach was about to discover that House von Hjertstedt had considered the Garooth a nuisance only because none had yet given the House a reason to mobilize properly.

---

## 3. The Gate Opens and Sol Screams

Gate Seventeen changed in less than six days.

What had once been a dead ruin now became a fortress throat.

Talyrn engineers raised control spines around the gate perimeter. House Gatewrights arrived with prefabricated command modules, power relays, armored shield nodes, and authentication towers. Mobile defense platforms were towed into anchored positions above and below the transit plane. Missile bastions unfolded from transport cradles. Sensor webs spread outward in layered shells. Sanctuary tenders took station behind the main defense line. Fuel ships, repair docks, marine carriers, and logistics barges formed a growing wall around the route.

Ortheva watched the construction from the command gallery.

“They really build like this all the time?”

Joel stood beside her.

“No.”

She looked at him.

“This is restrained?”

Joel said nothing.

That was answer enough.

Then the first heavy formation arrived from Vintergard.

Gate Seventeen woke.

A House battleship came through first, black and iron beneath crimson-lit armor seams. Then two Oath cruisers. Then escorts. Then tenders. Then more carriers and engineering ships. The formation spread with practiced discipline and immediately began integrating into the defensive shell.

Talyrn officers murmured quietly.

The scale alone was impressive.

Then the second wave came.

This time the gate stayed bright longer.

Something vast emerged.

At first it looked like a station moving under its own power.

Then the Talyrn plot resolved the shape.

A dreadnought.

Ancient lines.

Immense armor.

Layered spinal weapons.

Broadside fortresses built into a mobile hull.

Black and crimson House heraldry burned across its flanks.

Another followed.

Then another.

Eight in total.

Eight monster ships.

Eight dreadnoughts so large that some Talyrn officers instinctively checked whether the gate could actually support the passage of such mass. These were not ordinary capital ships. They were relic-beasts from a scale of war most modern human powers only remembered in broken archives, the kind of hulls not commonly seen since the Fall shattered older eras of grand fleet warfare. They carried the visual weight of history and the practical weight of annihilation.

Anton looked genuinely pleased.

“There they are.”

Ortheva stared at the eighth dreadnought clearing the gate.

“You had eight of those hidden.”

Lars answered from behind her.

“Yes.”

He had remained at Gate Seventeen’s primary command station since giving the order. There was no triumph in his voice. Only intent.

The dreadnoughts formed the core of the new line. Around them came more ships: Vigil frigates, destroyers, marine transports, repair vessels, heavy ammunition carriers, and what looked like half a fortress district under tow. Mobile fortifications followed in segmented masses—defense citadels, mass-driver platforms, fighter bastions, armored logistics hubs, and expandable shield nodes that could be locked together into temporary war stations.

Daniel watched the deployment plot update and smiled despite himself.

“We are building a front.”

Christer corrected him.

“We are building a wall.”

“No,” Lars said.

They all looked at him.

His gaze remained on the red zones deeper inside the Odrava.

“We are building a fist.”

Sol detected the mobilization before the fourth dreadnought finished transit.

The first message reached Annette at Ardent Central.

Then the second.

Then twelve more.

Terran Protectorate observers reported unprecedented House fleet movement.

Zenith frontier arrays tracked mass transfers disappearing into a supposedly dead gate corridor.

Hiigaran intelligence requested emergency clarification.

Imperium channels demanded to know why House reserve deployments now exceeded previous public strategic estimates by a margin nobody wished to state aloud.

Annette stood in Central’s strategic chamber with Kira and Lucy processing the flood.

Kira spoke first.

“Sol emergency assembly requested.”

Lucy added:

“Correction: demanded.”

Annette touched the pink rose in her hair.

“Who is screaming loudest?”

Lucy answered immediately.

“Terrans first. Hiigarans second. Zenith third. Imperium least loudly, but with the most military focus.”

That sounded correct.

The main chamber opened as House officers entered with updated frontier plots. More fleet movements had been detected leaving Vintergard-adjacent shadow routes. Ardent’s three major stations were also reinforcing, not by stripping themselves bare, but by rotating fresh defensive groups into place. Anyone watching would see the truth.

House von Hjertstedt was not improvising.

It had depth.

And now the galaxy could see it.

Annette opened the first Sol transmission.

The Terran chairwoman appeared immediately.

“Head Justicar, explain these deployments.”

Annette answered calmly.

“No.”

The woman stared.

“No?”

“You asked for an explanation as though that grants authority over House fleet movement.”

“You are mobilizing dreadnoughts.”

“Yes.”

“Eight.”

“Yes.”

“That class has not been seen openly in centuries.”

“That sounds like a failure in your records.”

The chairwoman’s expression hardened.

“You are moving mobile fortifications and expeditionary logistics through an unregistered corridor.”

“Yes.”

“Toward what?”

Annette looked at her for a long moment.

“A war.”

The Hiigaran admiral entered the call as priority override.

“With whom?”

“The Garooth.”

The chamber quieted.

Not because the answer was reassuring.

Because it was worse.

If House von Hjertstedt was mobilizing eight ancient dreadnoughts and an anchored fortified front against the Garooth, then either the House had lost all restraint—

or it had found something terrible enough to justify it.

The Zenith representative appeared next.

“Where?”

Annette smiled faintly.

“That is the correct question.”

“And the answer?”

“No.”

The Terran chairwoman looked furious.

“You expect us to accept this blind?”

Annette answered with perfect calm.

“I expect you to do what House von Hjertstedt is currently doing.”

“Which is?”

“Killing Garooth.”

At Gate Seventeen, the last of the eight dreadnoughts took position.

The anchored fortress line was now fully visible from the Talyrn command galleries. House and Talyrn defenses interlocked around the gate like the armored throat of a new shared frontier.

Ortheva stood beside Lars.

“You were serious.”

Lars looked at the deployment.

“I usually am.”

The Veyr-Khal watched the monster ships settle into formation.

“For generations, we believed Gate Seventeen opened only toward ghosts.”

Lars nodded once.

“Now it opens toward allies.”

Behind them, another wave of House ships emerged.

And across known space, Sol kept screaming.

---

## 4. The First Hammer

The first joint offensive began before Sol’s delegates reached Ardent.

Lars wanted it that way.

Not because he cared about political theater.

Because the Garooth had already started stripping the industrial system of Kharos Reach.

Waiting for diplomacy would cost people.

The tactical map showed three Talyrn habitats under direct pressure, two orbital foundries burning, and a mining moon partly occupied by Garooth forge crews.

Ortheva stood beside Lars at Gate Seventeen command.

“We can still evacuate the inner habitats.”

Lars looked at the population numbers.

“And abandon the yards?”

“Yes.”

“No.”

Ortheva glanced toward him.

“You intend to hold the system.”

“I intend to recover it.”

That distinction she understood.

The attack force was enormous.

But it was not purely Hjertstedt.

Talyrn formations led the route.

Their Seryth corvettes and Varyk frigates built the sensor picture first, cross-checking every contact across multiple nodes.

Behind them came Kelvar destroyers and Othrenn cruisers.

Then the House.

Vigil frigates.

Destroyers.

Oath cruisers.

Marine carriers.

Sanctuary tenders protected deep inside the formation.

And behind all of them—

two of the eight dreadnoughts.

The others remained anchored around Gate Seventeen or held in reserve.

Even two were enough.

The first Talyrn crews to see them translating into formation stopped referring to them as ships.

They called them moving fortresses.

Lars approved neither description nor correction.

Whisper Range lasted nine minutes.

Christer sat inside the joint tactical command, watching Talyrn sensor nodes and House witness systems argue with one another.

“Multiple false blooms.”

A Talyrn operator answered:

“Corroborated. Decoy hulks.”

“Real formation behind them.”

Ortheva pointed.

“Mark.”

The Garooth had filled the approach with wreckage, heat sources, junk transmitters, and captured civilian transponders.

Talyrn corroboration stripped away the lies.

House witness drones confirmed them.

By the time the Garooth realized its deception had failed, Hammer Range had already begun.

Lars gave one order.

“Break the forge line.”

The dreadnoughts fired.

Space turned white.

Their main batteries did not target the largest enemy ships.

They targeted the infrastructure holding the Garooth offensive together.

Mobile scrap forges.

Engine barges.

Ammunition hulks.

Boarding-carrier spines.

The first salvo shattered the enemy’s ability to sustain the fight.

Then the Talyrn moved.

Not in one heroic charge.

In layers.

One formation crippled drives.

Another forced vectors.

A third isolated targets.

House destroyers punched through the openings.

Oath cruisers followed.

The Garooth tried to collapse the battle into Knife Range.

Anton was already waiting.

“Now,” he said.

House marine carriers opened.

Boarding craft flooded forward.

Thyrak assault teams launched beside them.

For the first time, Talyrn Keth Var and Hjertstedt White Warrant doctrine hit the same battlefield together.

Fix.

Blind.

Root.

Seal.

Declare.

Breach.

Take the heart.

Different traditions.

The same enemy.

At Ardent Central, diplomacy was considerably less elegant.

The Terran delegation arrived first.

Hiigaran ships followed.

Then Zenith.

Then the Imperium.

Not warfleets.

But each delegation brought enough armed escort to demonstrate displeasure.

Annette watched them approach Central through the observation gallery.

Behind them, House banners covered the station.

Farther out, the new defensive platforms tracked every arriving ship.

Lucy whispered:

“Terran delegation requests priority audience.”

Kira added:

“Hiigaran delegation has submitted a formal gate-right inquiry.”

Annette sighed.

“Of course they have.”

The diplomatic chamber filled quickly.

Terran representatives.

Hiigaran admirals.

Zenith strategic officers.

Imperial observers.

All of them wanted the same answer.

Where had the House sent the fleet?

Why were eight dreadnoughts moving?

Why had mobile fortifications vanished into a route nobody else could identify?

Why had unprecedented logistics tonnage been mobilized?

The Terran chairwoman began.

“We detected two of your dreadnoughts entering combat deployment.”

Annette nodded.

“They are.”

“Against whom?”

“Garooth.”

“Where?”

“No.”

The Hiigaran admiral leaned forward.

“That is becoming unacceptable.”

Annette looked at him.

“To whom?”

“To every major power whose security could be affected by an unregistered gate network.”

Annette smiled faintly.

“There it is.”

“What?”

“Gate network.”

She leaned forward.

“You are not concerned that humans are being attacked.”

“You are concerned we found a road before you did.”

The admiral’s expression hardened.

“That is an unfair characterization.”

“Then prove me wrong.”

Silence.

A tactical alert interrupted the chamber.

Annette looked toward Kira.

“Battle update?”

“Confirmed.”

A projection opened.

Sanitized.

No gate coordinates.

No route data.

Only the battlefield.

The delegates saw a Talyrn fleet for the first time.

Unknown human warships.

Hundreds.

Disciplined.

Broad armored hulls.

Distributed formation control.

Then House ships among them.

And two Hjertstedt dreadnoughts driving directly through the Garooth center.

The entire chamber went silent.

The Terran chairwoman whispered:

“Who are they?”

Annette answered:

“Human.”

The Hiigaran admiral stood.

“From where?”

“Somewhere you cannot reach.”

Then the battle feed updated.

KHAROS REACH — FORGE LINE BROKEN

CIVILIAN HABITATS — SECURED

TALYRN COUNTEROFFENSIVE ADVANCING

HOUSE BOARDING GROUPS — KNIFE RANGE

The Imperial representative stared at the display.

“You found an entire human civilization.”

Annette said nothing.

That was confirmation enough.

Back at Kharos Reach, the Garooth center finally broke.

Talyrn destroyers cut down fleeing assault craft.

House boarding teams seized three mobile forges intact.

Thyrak teams recovered hundreds of trapped civilians from a partially captured habitat.

Sanctuary tenders moved in before the shooting had fully stopped.

The mining moon remained contested.

But the system was no longer falling.

Ortheva looked at Lars.

“First front stabilized.”

Lars shook his head.

“No.”

She frowned.

He pointed toward the retreating Garooth formations.

“First front opened.”

Then toward the deeper red concentrations.

“We keep going.”

At Ardent, the delegates watched the same advance.

The Zenith representative finally asked:

“Is House von Hjertstedt entering another war?”

Annette looked at the tactical display.

“Yes.”

“On behalf of this unknown civilization?”

Annette corrected him.

“Beside them.”

The distinction mattered.

The Terran chairwoman stared at the Talyrn formations.

“How large is this war?”

Annette looked at the red markers still beyond Kharos Reach.

“Large enough that Lars stopped caring how loudly Sol screams.”

Nobody laughed.

Because eight dreadnoughts had already answered the question.

---

## 5. Four Ways to Kill a War

The mining moon of Kharos Reach was not a moon anymore.

The Garooth had turned it into a machine.

Mining shafts became troop tunnels.

Ore processors became ammunition works.

Cargo elevators carried armored mobs instead of iron.

Captured civilian habs had been welded into defensive blocks.

Deep beneath the surface, surviving Talyrn workers were still trapped behind sealed industrial sections.

That made orbital bombardment unacceptable.

So Joel chose the slower option.

Boarding.

The Talyrn assigned two Thyrak intervention companies to the assault.

Their commander studied the operational plan.

“Your family will remain with command?”

Joel looked at him.

“No.”

The Thyrak commander paused.

“Of course not.”

Four assault craft descended separately.

Four objectives.

Four brothers.

The Talyrn expected them to stay mutually supporting.

Instead, Joel split them across the entire battlefield.

The Thyrak commander stared at the plan.

“That separates your highest-value personnel.”

Joel locked his command helmet into place.

“It also separates their problems.”

The commander frowned.

Joel pointed at four sectors.

“Watch.”

Joel

Joel entered through the administrative spine.

Not the weakest point.

The most important.

His black-and-iron command armor carried fewer visible weapons than Anton’s, but everything about it was built around control—sensor integration, command uplinks, battlefield authentication, hardened communications.

The moment his cutter breached, Joel stopped fighting the enemy in front of him.

He started fighting the moon.

“Seal Junction Twelve.”

Talyrn engineers obeyed.

“Open maintenance shafts C-Seven and C-Nine.”

“Enemy movement there.”

“I know.”

The shafts opened.

Garooth reinforcements rushed toward what looked like exposed routes.

Joel vented the empty sections behind them and locked the forward blast doors.

Three mobs disappeared from the tactical map without a firefight.

The Thyrak attached to him stared.

Joel was already moving.

He marked civilians.

Separated power systems.

Redirected elevators.

Cut enemy communications between the upper mines and the refinery districts.

Every shot he fired solved an immediate problem.

Every command created another advantage somewhere else.

Within nineteen minutes, Garooth forces in the administrative sector were still alive—

but no longer part of the battle.

A Talyrn officer whispered:

“He is not clearing the station.”

The Thyrak commander answered:

“No.”

“He is taking away its ability to resist.”

Anton

Anton entered through the main ore-loading hall.

The Talyrn had identified it as the worst possible breach point.

Open ground.

Heavy defenses.

Thousands of tonnes of industrial equipment.

A Garooth kill box.

Anton selected it immediately.

His assault armor was massive black-and-crimson plate built around impact survival. Reinforced shoulders. Breach claws. Heavy melee systems. Thick frontal armor.

His cutter hit the loading doors.

Anton went through before the smoke cleared.

The first Garooth heavy gun fired.

Anton crossed the distance before it could correct.

His powered fist crushed the weapon housing.

His blade killed the gunner.

Then he took the gun emplacement itself and rotated it manually toward the next defensive position.

The Talyrn Thyrak following him stopped for half a second.

Anton fired.

The entire barricade vanished.

“MOVE!”

He did not clear corridors carefully.

He created corridors.

A sealed bulkhead blocked the advance.

Anton did not wait for Daniel.

He tore the emergency actuator out of the wall and forced the door apart with both hands.

A Garooth brute charged through.

Anton killed it with the broken actuator.

The Thyrak commander watching remotely said:

“He is tactically reckless.”

Joel answered over the command net:

“No.”

Another Garooth position disappeared beneath Anton’s advance.

“He knows exactly how much violence the structure can survive.”

That was worse.

Christer

Christer never entered the largest fight.

He made sure nobody else had to.

His tactical armor carried expanded sensor vanes, targeting systems, drone links, and a command suite optimized for battlefield awareness.

He landed in the refinery towers.

Within seven minutes, every Talyrn unit began receiving cleaner targeting data.

Enemy firing arcs appeared before the guns fired.

Ambush points were marked.

Hidden access shafts lit up.

Garooth decoys vanished from the tactical picture.

Christer found the enemy by noticing where information was wrong.

One heat signature remained too constant.

“Forge control room.”

The Talyrn officer beside him frowned.

“Scans show empty.”

“That is why it is occupied.”

They breached.

A Garooth command group died before realizing it had been found.

Then Christer redirected the entire western assault around a collapsing refinery stack twenty seconds before structural failure.

Hundreds of Talyrn troops avoided the blast zone.

The Thyrak commander stared at the timing.

“How did he know?”

Joel answered:

“He watches everything.”

Daniel

Daniel went underground.

Deep.

Toward power.

His engineer-command armor was the least ceremonial of all four. Tool arms, breaching systems, diagnostic interfaces, compact weapons, reinforced environmental seals.

The Garooth had wired the old reactor network into its forge system.

Destroy it, and the mining moon could become uninhabitable.

Leave it intact, and the enemy could keep producing weapons.

Daniel looked at the system for forty seconds.

Then started issuing orders.

“Shut coolant loop three.”

A Talyrn engineer stared.

“That will overheat the secondary plant.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“Because they will divert cooling from the forge.”

They did.

Daniel smiled.

“There.”

The redistribution exposed the improvised Garooth power bypasses.

He remotely overloaded one.

Three enemy forge levels went dark.

Then Daniel opened a maintenance gate and led his team straight through the now-unpowered machinery.

No explosion.

No dramatic demolition.

He simply turned the enemy’s own infrastructure against them.

When they reached the primary reactor control, the Garooth had placed explosives around the core.

The Talyrn engineer looked at Daniel.

“Can you disarm them?”

Daniel examined the mess.

“I can isolate the remote circuit.”

“How long to disarm all of them?”

“Longer than we have. Watch the local triggers.”

He pulled one cable.

Half the charges died.

Then another.

The rest switched to local triggers.

Daniel shot the detonator operator through the face.

“Done.”

Forty-three minutes after the assault began, the moon’s tactical map changed.

Red sectors collapsed one after another.

Not because the Garooth had all been killed.

Because they had been divided.

Blinded.

Trapped.

Disarmed.

Broken.

Anton shattered the strongest resistance.

Christer made sure nothing remained hidden.

Daniel took away the infrastructure sustaining the occupation.

Joel controlled the whole battlefield until every separate action became part of one result.

The surviving civilians were reached alive.

The main reactor remained stable.

Three Garooth forge complexes were captured intact.

Talyrn losses were far below projections.

The Thyrak commander stood in the recovered command center, staring at the four brothers as they removed their helmets.

“No limiter release?”

Joel shook his head.

“No.”

The commander looked genuinely disturbed.

Anton smiled.

“That bothers you more, does it not?”

The Thyrak commander considered the question.

“Yes.”

The Ardent records Joel had shared had taught the Talyrn what the Hjertstedts looked like when they removed their limits.

Kharos Reach had just shown them something else.

What they looked like when they did not need to.

---

## 6. Release the Hunters

Kharos Reach had not finished counting its dead when Lars ordered the next offensive.

The Talyrn expected a pause.

Repair.

Reorganization.

A formal review of the recovered mining moon.

Lars gave them fourteen hours.

Veyr-Khal Ortheva Sair stared at the operational clock.

“Fourteen?”

“Fuel, ammunition, medical transfer, structural inspection.”

“That is not enough time for a full fleet reset.”

Lars looked at the retreating Garooth formations.

“It is enough time for them to start building another army.”

That ended the discussion.

The Garooth survived through momentum. Wreckage became armor. Captured factories became forges. Abandoned ships became new hulls.

So Lars refused to give them wreckage and time in the same place.

“Next objective.”

The tactical display shifted.

A chain of captured extraction stations and mobile forge hulks sat between Kharos Reach and the deeper convergence.

Christer marked the route.

“If we break this corridor, their forward warbands lose local supply.”

Daniel added:

“And we recover three industrial stations before they finish converting them.”

Ortheva studied the numbers.

“They will defend it heavily.”

Anton smiled.

“Good.”

Lars looked toward him.

“Not you.”

Anton frowned.

“What?”

“You have already had your fun.”

“That is not a military reason.”

“No.”

Then Lars opened a different channel.

A symbol appeared.

Black.

Bronze.

Crimson.

Joel recognized it immediately.

Anton stopped complaining.

“Oh.”

The transmission connected.

Commander Dennis Rask appeared.

He looked almost completely recovered.

Almost.

There were still scars along one side of his face from the Seraph command ship. One eye had been replaced by a dark combat optic. His old blackened armor stood open behind him on a maintenance cradle.

Around it waited something much larger.

Proctor-class Hunter armor.

Bronze-black plate.

Massive shoulders.

Reliquary seals.

Null-projector housings.

Heavy containment systems.

Armor designed for environments where normal infantry stopped being enough.

Dennis looked at Lars.

“You finally remembered I exist.”

Lars ignored that.

“Operational status?”

“Bored.”

“Medical status.”

“Operational.”

Annette appeared on another channel from Ardent.

“No.”

Dennis looked toward her.

“I was not speaking to you.”

“You are always speaking to me when medical compliance is involved.”

Dennis sighed.

“Cleared for combat.”

Annette checked something.

Then reluctantly nodded.

“Technically.”

Dennis smiled.

Lars gave the order.

“Release the Seraph Hunters.”

The Talyrn command chamber became very quiet.

Ortheva looked toward Joel.

“Seraph Hunters?”

Joel answered:

“Specialists.”

Anton added:

“Maniacs.”

Joel glanced at him.

“Specialists.”

Three old formation identifiers appeared.

DUSKBRINGER.

EXTERMINATUS SEAL.

PURIFIER.

They had been created for Seraph containment.

For Husk Guard.

Conversion nests.

Captured command decks.

Infested laboratories.

Places where a normal boarding unit could win tactically and still lose because something survived behind the walls.

Against the Garooth, their anti-Seraph systems were unnecessary.

Their armor was not.

Neither was their training.

Dennis looked at the Garooth corridor.

“What are the restrictions?”

Lars answered:

“Civilian infrastructure remains recoverable.”

“Understood.”

“Industrial assets intact where practical.”

Dennis looked mildly disappointed.

“Understood.”

“Prisoners where useful.”

Dennis stared at him.

“From Garooth?”

“Where useful.”

“So almost none.”

Joel muttered:

“That is probably accurate.”

Lars continued.

“Primary objective: break the corridor fast enough that nothing retreats inward in organized strength.”

Dennis smiled.

“That I can do.”

The Hunters deployed from assault carriers at the front of the offensive.

The Talyrn had already seen Thyrak.

They understood augmented troops.

They understood powered armor.

They were still not prepared for the Seraph Hunters.

The first Hunter stepped from the transport bay like a walking bunker.

Then another.

Then dozens.

Not identical.

Some carried massive shields and great anti-Seraph blades.

Others carried incineration cannons originally designed to destroy biological contamination inside armored environments.

Heavy breaching weapons hung from reinforced hardpoints.

Their suits moved slower than Thyrak armor.

Until they charged.

Then the mass became horrifying.

A Talyrn Kharven officer watched Duskbringer board the first captured refinery station.

“Those are infantry?”

The Thyrak commander beside him answered:

“Apparently.”

Dennis led the breach personally.

Of course he did.

The Garooth had barricaded the refinery’s main freight spine.

Heavy guns covered the approach.

Armored brutes waited behind industrial loaders welded into fortifications.

Dennis looked once.

“Wall.”

A Hunter beside him answered:

“Yes, commander.”

Dennis raised the incineration cannon.

The Talyrn observer suddenly remembered Joel’s stories about him.

“Oh no.”

Dennis fired.

The barricade disappeared inside a concentrated blast that turned welded scrap into glowing ruin.

A section of the wall behind it collapsed too.

Dennis walked through the opening.

“Better.”

Duskbringer followed.

The Garooth countercharged.

That was exactly what the Hunters wanted.

A huge brute struck one Hunter’s shield with enough force to move him half a meter.

The Hunter stabilized.

Caught the weapon.

The second Hunter cut the attacker apart.

Another Garooth mob flooded a side corridor.

Dennis fired once.

The corridor ceased to be contested.
